  **Two more, both in measuring the dialogs themselves.** A DPI-unaware probe process querying `GetWindowRect`/`GetClientRect` on this per-monitor-aware app is handed *virtualized* coordinates scaled by 96/monitor-DPI — 0.8 on a 120-DPI display — which read as "every dialog is 20% too small" and led to a `SetDialogDpiChangeBehavior(DDC_DISABLE_ALL)` "fix" for a bug that did not exist. In-app instrumentation showed the client rect was exact at every step; the call was removed rather than kept. Separately, `GetClassNameW`/`GetWindowTextW` marshalled as ANSI truncate a wide string at its first NUL, reporting the class as `#`. And when driving the Find-in-Files fields, a cross-process `SetWindowTextW` writes USER32's cached window caption, which a ComboBox's internal edit never sees — the probe read its own text back happily while the app read an empty query. `SendMessage(WM_SETTEXT)` is marshalled to the real window proc and works.

- **~~Replace in Files: handle open files like Notepad++ does.~~ Done for clean tabs (Phase 5); dirty tabs still declined.** The orchestrator used to skip every file the user had open, so a Replace in Files silently did nothing to them. It now hands the replacement it computed to `Shell::drain`, which applies it to the tab's Scintilla buffer — the user keeps undo, the file watcher stays silent, and nothing is written underneath them. Living in `drain` rather than in each backend's results dock means both platforms get it with no UI wiring, and it is testable without one: the end-to-end test runs a real worker pool over real files and asserts the closed file is rewritten on disk while the open one is not and its buffer receives the edit instead.

  **A tab with unsaved edits is still declined**, and that is deliberate rather than unfinished. The worker computes its replacement from the bytes *on disk*, which are not what the user is looking at in a dirty buffer, so applying it would discard their work. Closing that case means re-searching the buffer itself, and the two searches do not use the same engine: find-in-files compiles to the `regex` crate because it runs off the UI thread, while an in-editor search goes through Scintilla's C++11 engine, and the dialects disagree on lookarounds, backreferences and more. Re-searching would risk replacing a different set of matches than the results dock just reported for that file. Resolving it properly means either accepting that divergence explicitly, or moving find-in-files onto Scintilla's engine — which would mean running searches on the UI thread, against DESIGN.md §5.4. Worth deciding rather than drifting into.

  Also outstanding, and small: the counts are reported through `FifEvent::ReplacedInOpenBuffer` but no backend surfaces them yet. They belong on the completion toast alongside the existing "scanned N files" summary.

- **~~Synchronous plugin notification delivery for `NPPN_FILEBEFORECLOSE` (and friends).~~ Landed (Phase 5), on all three backends — and the fix this entry prescribed would not have worked, which is the more useful half of the record.**

  The entry called for "a callback registered by the UI at startup that dispatches a single notification mid-operation". That cannot deliver the contract, on any backend: the UI's `&mut Shell` is on the stack for the whole of `close_active_tab`, and a plugin's `NPPM_GETFULLPATHFROMBUFFERID` from inside `beNotified` needs a *fresh* borrow of the same state. Every backend's state accessor correctly declines that nested borrow rather than aliasing one — `state_from_hwnd` under `PluginCallGuard` on Win32, `with_state` on GTK and Cocoa — so a notification delivered from inside the operation would have reached the plugin at the right *moment* and still answered its lookup with -1. Delivering it before the tab is removed is only half of the requirement; the other half is that the host holds **no borrow at all** while the plugin runs.

  **What ships is a two-phase close with a value handed across the borrow.** `Shell::begin_close_active_tab` returns a `SyncNotification` — the `FileBeforeClose`, the announced buffer's id, and a `NotifyTargets` snapshot of the loaded plugins' `beNotified` entry points, a list of `Copy` function pointers valid until `Shell` drops, since plugins are never unloaded before that. The UI takes it under a borrow, delivers it with none, then calls `close_announced_tab(id)`, which queues no second FILEBEFORECLOSE and **refuses** to close a *different* tab than the one announced — a plugin that moves `active_tab` from inside its handler (`NPPM_SWITCHTOFILE`, `NPPM_ACTIVATEDOC`) would otherwise get a buffer closed that never passed the Save / Don't Save / Cancel gate. The plain `close_active_tab` stays as the unannounced fallback, delivering FILEBEFORECLOSE through the deferred queue at the old timing, so no path can lose the event. The announcement sits after the save prompt, where N++'s `doClose` fires it: a Cancel means nothing closes and nothing is announced. Each backend has exactly one close call site, so the wiring is one hunk per backend.

  **The first version kept the announced id on the shell, and the pre-commit review found why that is wrong.** A marker field consumed by the next close reads naturally, and it survives every test a single close can pose. It does not survive a *nested* close: a plugin that sends `WM_COMMAND(ID_FILE_CLOSE)` straight to the host window from inside its FILEBEFORECLOSE handler — ordinary Win32 plugin technique, and reachable precisely because the delivery now runs with no guard — runs a complete inner close, whose `close_active_tab` takes the marker. The outer close then resumes, finds no announcement, falls back to the unannounced path, and removes whichever tab is now in front, with no prompt. The id therefore travels *in the value* and is passed back explicitly; an id the caller holds cannot be taken from it, and the outer close is refused because its tab is gone. Pinned by a shell test that stages exactly that nesting. The same review found the shared marker could also go stale on an early return between announce and close, which the value design removes for free.

  **The finding underneath is larger than the entry, and it is why the fix reaches past `FILEBEFORECLOSE`.** Delivery of *every* queued notification also ran inside a borrow — `notify_plugins` through `&Shell` under `PluginCallGuard` on Win32, inside `with_state` on GTK and Cocoa — so every re-entrant `NPPM_*` from any `beNotified` was declined on every backend, silently, with the plugin reading a plausible 0 or -1. Win32's plugin menu-command arm armed the guard too, "for depth", after NLL had already ended the lookup borrow, which made every `NPPM_*` from a plugin *command* declined as well; the in-tree plugins only appeared to work because `codepp-plugin-sdk` falls back to the handles `setInfo` provided when `NPPM_GETCURRENTSCINTILLA` answers 0. `drain_shell`'s own comment on Cocoa promised the opposite of what it did. All of that now runs the same way as the close: snapshot under the borrow (`Shell::notify_targets`), call the plugin after it ends, no guard. `PluginCallGuard` stays only where a plugin genuinely runs inside a borrow that cannot be dropped first — the lazy load's `setInfo` / `NPPN_READY`, the `WM_DESTROY` shutdown pair, the dark-mode re-broadcast — and its doc now says that the refusal is the guard's cost, not a side effect.

  `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID` with the alert flag no longer reloads silently: the dispatcher queues the file watcher's own `ConfirmReload` on `deferred_dialogs` (which did not exist when the Phase 4 limitation was written) and every backend presents it **before the plugin's `SendMessage` returns** — new `present_deferred_dialogs` on each backend, called after every NPPM dispatch and after every notification delivery, since a plugin call posts no worker wake and nothing else would surface the prompt until an unrelated drain. `WM_DESTROY` takes the delivery-only variant and drops any dialog a plugin queues there rather than pumping a modal on a window mid-teardown.

  **Verified by driving the real Win32 app against a pre-change build of the same tree**, with the four plugins staged and a file open: with this change, the host debug log shows `plugin_notify{plugin="Example Hello" code=1003}: plugin set status bar … text="Closing: C:\…\closing-demo.txt"` — the plugin resolved the path *and* its status-bar write was answered, both from inside the FILEBEFORECLOSE span — and cppexport's "Copy HTML to Clipboard" puts 364 bytes of HTML on the clipboard and "Export: HTML copied to clipboard" on the status bar. On the pre-change host the same plugin DLLs produce no log line, no status change and an untouched clipboard from either path: **that command has never worked on Windows**, which nothing in the suite noticed because the in-tree plugins report their own failure only through the very status-bar message that was also declined.

  Two smaller things the review turned up on Cocoa, both newly reachable because a plugin call can now present a real prompt: the announce-and-close span holds a `DrainFreeze`, as GTK's whole close already did, so a wake dispatched into a plugin-triggered alert cannot move the tab under the close; and every dialog on Cocoa now goes through one queue and one single-flight pump — GTK's `DIALOG_QUEUE` / `PRESENTING` shape — so a second prompt queued from inside an alert's run loop waits its turn instead of stacking. The security audit caught that the first version guarded only the plugin-queued path and left `drain_shell`'s own prompts presenting inline, outside the rule; a guard that covers one of two presenters is not single-flight. `example-hello` is what makes that observable: its `beNotified` now handles `NPPN_FILEBEFORECLOSE` the way real plugins do, through a new `sdk::buffer_path`, so it doubles as the demo. Two instrument notes. The status-bar text itself is overwritten within the same close by the host's own `update_status` for the newly active tab — N++ does the same — so the log line, not the chrome, is the evidence, and a `tracing::debug!` in `HostBridge::set_status_bar` exists for exactly that. And a first version of the cppexport check sent `SCI_INSERTTEXT` from the driving process, which cannot marshal a pointer across processes, so the "empty buffer" it reported was the probe's fault; the check now copies the opened file's text before closing it.

  Headless coverage: shell tests pin the protocol in both directions (announced close delivers once and before removal; unannounced close falls back to the queue; a moved `active_tab` refuses the close and the stale announcement does not suppress the next one; the reload prompt is queued and no load issued until Yes), and a Linux end-to-end test drives the real `libexample_hello.so` through a recording mock host and asserts the path lookup and the status write both happen from inside the notification. Source-scan guards on Win32 pin the three rules a diff cannot show — delivery from a snapshot with no guard, announce-then-deliver-then-close in that order, and no guard on the command arm; Cocoa's existing `notifications_are_delivered_outside_the_drain_borrow` guard still holds and now describes what actually happens. The core suite's tracing-sigil guard caught the new log line using `%` rather than `?` on its first Linux run, which is the guard doing its job.

  **Still open, recorded rather than folded in.** `NPPN_FILEBEFORESAVE` is the remaining "friend" and the one with a real consequence: a plugin that edits the buffer from it (trailing-whitespace trimmers) sees its edit land *after* the write, leaving the buffer dirty. It fits the same `SyncNotification` shape but needs an announce step at every UI save site, and a decision for the plugin-initiated `NPPM_SAVECURRENTFILE`, where the dispatch borrow is live and synchronous delivery is impossible by construction. `NPPN_FILEBEFOREOPEN` is *deliberately* left on the queue: it carries no buffer id, so there is nothing a handler could query about the in-flight open and the deferral changes nothing observable. The lazy load remains the one window where a plugin cannot re-enter — `setInfo`, `getFuncsArray`, and the per-plugin `NPPN_READY` / `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` fire inside `PluginHost::load`'s borrow — and plugins that query the host from READY (version, config directory) still read 0 there; the same snapshot mechanism can close it by having the load return the READY pair for delivery outside the borrow, and it is the next thing to do with this machinery. *(Since closed, twice over: the load was split into phases so `setInfo`, `getFuncsArray` and the load-time notifications run with no borrow held — `PluginHost::load` no longer exists — and those notifications are no longer per plugin either, but one batch per load pass in Notepad++'s measured order; see the lifecycle entry further down.)* `NPPN_BEFORESHUTDOWN` still cannot veto shutdown. `NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT`'s `SCI_CONVERTEOLS` has since landed — see its own entry below. And the security audit noted a pre-existing gap one step *earlier* in the same Win32 function: the save prompt's modal pump can drain the shell and move `active_tab` before the announcement reads it, so the tab prompted and the tab announced could differ — the same shape the announced-id fix closes for the step after it. *(Since closed, both ways: Win32's close path holds a `DrainFreeze` across the prompt, which stops the host's own drain, and carries the prompted tab's id through the answer, which refuses a plugin's nested close — see the close-confirm entry below for the measurement that showed the freeze alone was not enough.)*

- **~~`NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT` should issue `SCI_CONVERTEOLS` on the addressed buffer.~~ Landed (Phase 5), on all three backends.** The trait grew `UiPlatform::convert_doc_eols(doc, eol)` — `SCI_SETEOLMODE` then `SCI_CONVERTEOLS` on the addressed document, reached through the same doc-pointer swap `replace_doc_text` uses when it is not the bound one — and `Shell::set_buffer_eol_by_id` now takes the UI and calls it, with the plugin bridge refreshing the status bar when the addressed buffer is the active one, the way `set_buffer_lang_type` already did for its slot. Required rather than defaulted, on `release_doc`'s reasoning: a no-op default would leave a backend answering TRUE and converting nothing.

  Two decisions differ from the entry's original sketch. **It converts unconditionally**, even when `Tab::eol` already reads the requested format: the label is detection's verdict on the first 64 KiB at load time, and a paste or a plugin's own `SCI_INSERTTEXT` can leave an "LF" buffer holding CRLF lines, so a plugin asking for LF wants the bytes normalised rather than the label checked — a same-value short-circuit would answer TRUE and do nothing, which is the silently-half-working shape this section keeps recording. And **a document that does not exist yet is handled too**: a tab loaded in the background and never activated has `scintilla_doc == 0` and holds its content only in `Tab::text`, which `bind_and_fill` installs on first activation, so that string is converted (`core::eol::convert`, the headless twin of `SCI_CONVERTEOLS`) — otherwise the first click onto the tab would install the old endings under a label claiming the new ones. `Eol::Mixed` is refused: it means "keep each line's own ending", so there is no sequence to convert to, and the ABI cannot ask for it.

  **The pre-commit gate found the shadow half was a data-loss bug as first written, and the fix reaches one function further than this entry.** `bind_and_fill` installs the shadow through `set_buffer_text`, which empties the undo buffer and *sets the save point* — so a converted-but-uninstalled shadow read clean the moment it was installed, every backend's dirty poll overwrote the cached bit with that answer, the close gate (live bit OR cached bit) let the tab go without a prompt, and the conversion was simply gone with no undo to reach it. `unsaved_restore_ids` is the mechanism that exists for "seeded at a save point but not on disk", and the first fix put the id there directly — which the test written for it then failed, instructively: `has_unsaved_work` ORs that set, and `has_unsaved_work` is what `bind_and_fill` reads to decide whether installing the shadow would clobber the user's edits, so an unsaved shadow marked that way was read as unsaved *document* work and blocked the very install it was waiting for. The two meanings are now distinct: `Tab::shadow_unsaved` carries the fact until the install, and `bind_and_fill` promotes it into the id set at the moment the save point is set, or drops it when the install is skipped. `save_session` had the matching hole one level down — `capture_text_from_doc(0)` answers an empty string by contract, so a doc-less tab that needed a backup would have been restored *empty* over the file on the next launch — and backs up the shadow now. **`apply_open_buffer_replacement`'s doc-less arm had the identical hole**, pre-existing: a Replace-in-Files into a background tab set the cached bit alone and was lost the same way. It goes through the same helper now. A reload landing on a tab **keeps** an unsaved shadow and drops the loaded text, the answer the document branch already gives unsaved edits and for the same reason — the file watcher and `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID` reload with no prompt, and the shadow is the only copy of that work; logged at `warn`, since the log is the only record of a declined reload. The first draft gated that on the tab being inactive, on the claim that activation always installs and promotes the shadow first — and both the review and the audit disproved it the same way: `NPPM_SWITCHTOFILE` / `NPPM_ACTIVATEDOC` move `active_tab` and leave the rebind to the backend, and Win32 skips that rebind while a load is pending, so three plugin calls in a row (convert, reload, switch) land the reload on an *active* tab whose shadow is still uninstalled, where the gate would have overwritten the shadow and left its flag set forever — every later crash backup then carrying the stale shadow instead of the live edits. The arm is now gated on the flag alone, and when the tab is active it installs the shadow into the view and promotes it itself, the same promotion `bind_and_fill` makes; pinned by a test staging exactly that interleaving. The same install branch is where a reload of *fresh* disk content releases a tab from `unsaved_restore_ids`: a `DirtyFromBackup` tab whose recovered text the user (through the reload prompt) or a plugin (through `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID`) has just replaced with the file no longer holds anything unsaved, and nothing had ever removed it — so it kept a red glyph, a recovery backup and a close prompt for content it no longer held. Only the active branch releases; the background arm keeps unsaved work and its marker with it.

  **Consent now travels with the reload request (`ReloadConsent`, `Tab::reload_confirmed`), which is what the two items the gate left open turned out to share.** Every reload went through one `confirm_reload`, so the shell could not tell the watcher prompt's Yes, File → Reload and `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID(alert = TRUE)`'s Yes — all answered against "reload from disk and discard any unsaved edits?" — from the one route with no prompt at all, `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID(alert = FALSE)`. The "never over unsaved edits" rule was therefore applied by *position*: declined on a background tab, whoever asked, and granted on the active tab, whoever asked. Both halves were wrong at an edge. A Yes for a background file with edits did nothing, silently, contradicting the dialog the user had just answered; and a plugin's silent reload of the active tab overwrote a crash-recovered buffer whose backup is rewritten from the live document at the next session save — so the recovered content was gone within seconds, with no prompt and no record (the audit's Medium on the release change). Now the rule is applied by *consent*: unsaved work — edits, the durable marker, an uninstalled shadow — yields to a confirmed reload wherever the tab is, and never to an unconfirmed one, which is declined and logged at `warn` (N++ would overwrite; the plugin can ask with the alert). The decision is made twice, at landing and — for a background tab — again at the activation that installs it, so the consent stays on the tab until the install that consumes it, or a failed load, clears it; a click onto the tab while the reload is still in flight binds the stale document as it is and leaves the consent for the landing. Four tests that had pinned the position rule became the unconfirmed-route tests, three new ones pin the confirmed route and the mid-flight click, and both gates are mutation-verified. Writing it up exposed a second prompt-less route to consent: `NPPM_RELOADFILE` carries the same `wParam` alert flag as `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID`, and the dispatcher ignored it and called `confirm_reload` outright, under a comment claiming the reload path would prompt. It now honours the flag exactly as `reload_buffer_id` does. The review then found the consent being consumed by the wrong bind: `save_all` binds every titled tab as bookkeeping before it moves `active_tab`, and keyed on the flag alone it would have force-installed a landed reload over the very edits Save All exists to keep. The forced install is therefore gated on the bind being the activation (`active_tab == Some(idx)`), and a save retires any reload outcome still waiting on its tab — the save is the later, explicit intent, and the document is now what is on disk. Mutation-verified. The audit found the other edge of the same consent: it is answered against the buffer as it stood, and a Replace-in-Files into that background document during the async window before the read came back was being discarded under a Yes given for a clean tab, with no record. A write to a tab after consent now withdraws it while the load is in flight (`cancel_reload_consent`, from both Replace-in-Files arms and from an EOL conversion that changed something), so the landing keeps the newer work as it would for an unconfirmed request; once the load has landed, the write's own bookkeeping already decides the survivor. The consented discard itself is logged at `info`, since it is the one branch where content leaves memory. Both mutation-verified. Two refinements from the delta review. The withdrawal is keyed on the *edge* — a conversion that created unsaved state where there was none — not on the level, because a consent already covers the unsaved work the buffer held, and keying on the level let a plugin's no-op `NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT` between the user's Yes and the landing quietly turn the reload into a decline. And `bind_and_fill` now takes the activation fact as a parameter rather than inferring it from `active_tab == idx`, because that inference is false on GTK and Cocoa: their NPPM dispatch did not rebind after `NPPM_SWITCHTOFILE` / `NPPM_ACTIVATEDOC` at all — a pre-existing gap Win32 closed in Phase 4 with its post-dispatch `needs_rebind`, and the "save writes the bound document's bytes to the active tab's path" split this document calls the most damaging bug the crate can produce, reachable there as a plugin's switch followed by `NPPM_SAVECURRENTFILE`. Both backends rebind on that edge now, mirroring Win32 down to leaving an in-flight load to its landing. Recorded as verified by build and test only: there is no GTK or Cocoa runner on this host, and the rebind is one call into a function every other tab switch on those backends already goes through.

  The audit's second finding was in the stale-document arm (`doc_needs_text`: a background tab holding pre-reload content, with the reload waiting in `Tab::text`). The first version converted both copies "so the outcome is right whichever survives", and converting the stale document is not harmless: the modify bit it flips is exactly what `bind_and_fill` reads to decide, so a clean stale document became "unsaved work", the activation kept it and silently dropped the reload — and a save then wrote pre-reload content over a newer file. Only the copy that will survive is converted now, decided with the same `has_unsaved_work` read the activation will make. `FakeUi::convert_doc_eols` models the modify bit (dirty only when bytes changed) so this arm is exercised by the suite rather than staged.

  The dirty marker needed work on all three backends, in two shapes. `SCI_CONVERTEOLS` emits its `SCN_MODIFIED` / `SCN_SAVEPOINTLEFT` synchronously, inside the dispatch borrow, where the GTK and Cocoa notification handlers are declined — the same contract `on_sci_notify`'s docs state and the m4a Replace case already paid for. On Win32 that notification is *not* declined, and that is the problem rather than the fix: the plugin-dispatch arm holds `shell`/`ui` out of `state.split()` across the dispatch, and the nested `SCN_SAVEPOINT*` arm would take a second `&mut WindowState` from the raw pointer — the aliasing the Replace-in-Files loop already arms `PluginCallGuard` against around its `SCI_REPLACETARGET`. `convert_doc_eols` arms the same guard (only when one is not already armed, since `enter` asserts against nesting), and the shell re-reads the addressed tab's cached `Tab.dirty` from the live state after every conversion instead. Then each backend repaints on what actually moved: `dispatch_nppm` (GTK, Cocoa) and the Win32 NPPM arm snapshot every tab's cached bit as well as the bound document's live bit either side of the dispatch, and refresh only on a change — the bound bit alone could never see a background conversion, because its document is swapped in and out inside the dispatch. Gated on a change rather than unconditional because a plugin command typically makes a dozen `NPPM_*` queries, and a strip rebuild per query is not a cost worth paying for the three messages that can flip a bit; it also closes the same pre-existing gap for `NPPM_MAKECURRENTBUFFERDIRTY` and `NPPM_SAVECURRENTFILE`. Win32's `SCN_SAVEPOINT*` arm additionally attributes an edge to the tab that *owns the bound document* rather than to the active tab, which fixes a pre-existing Replace-in-Files case: an in-buffer replacement into a background tab, which runs unguarded from the drain, painted the active tab's glyph.

  Headless coverage: `core::eol::convert` unit tests (a CRLF pair is one ending, not two — the trap that would double every Windows line on a CR conversion); shell tests for the bound-document, unmaterialised-tab and stale-document arms plus the `Mixed` and unknown-id refusals — each following through to the activation and asserting the tab still counts as unsaved, with the promotion mutation-verified against all three; and a dispatch-level test pinning that `NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT` on the active tab both converts and repaints the status bar with the new label. **Verified by driving the real Win32 app** from a PowerShell probe (integer-only `wparam`/`lparam`, so a cross-process `SendMessage` reaches the dispatcher): a file holding `one\nmixed\r\ntwo\nthree\n` is on disk as uniform CRLF after `SETBUFFERFORMAT(WIN_FORMAT)` + Save, and uniform LF after `SETBUFFERFORMAT(UNIX_FORMAT)` + Save, while the same probe against the pre-change build returns 1 from both calls and leaves every byte as it was. The Scintilla side was measured on the live control rather than read from `Document::ConvertLineEnds`: a three-line LF buffer grows 14 → 17 bytes with `SCI_GETMODIFY` 0 → 1 and `SCI_CANUNDO` 0 → 1, one `SCI_UNDO` returns all three (14, clean, nothing to undo), and a same-format request on an already-uniform buffer leaves it clean — so the unconditional convert costs a scan and never a phantom undo action.

- **~~Win32's dirty glyph can momentarily go blue on a restored buffer after edit-then-undo; GTK's does not.~~ Landed (Phase 5), with the `bind_active_view` routing above — the savepoint handler and the close gate consult `is_unsaved_restore` now.** Original entry follows. Restored-but-unsaved buffers (untitled from backup, or a `DirtyFromBackup` saved file) are tracked in `Shell.unsaved_restore_ids` so they paint red and keep their recovery backup even though their Scintilla document sits at its save point. GTK's `refresh_active_dirty` ORs `Shell::is_unsaved_restore(id)` into every dirty poll, so its cached `Tab.dirty` stays red until a real save. Win32's `Tab.dirty` is driven solely by the `SCN_SAVEPOINTREACHED`/`SCN_SAVEPOINTLEFT` handler, which was **not** changed and does not consult the set — so on Win32, editing one character in a restored buffer and undoing it returns the doc to the seeded save point, `SCN_SAVEPOINTREACHED` fires, and the glyph flips to blue. **No data loss** — `Shell::tab_needs_backup` still ORs the id set, so the backup is retained regardless of the glyph — but the indicator is momentarily wrong. Backport the GTK fix to Win32's savepoint handler (consult `is_unsaved_restore` before clearing `Tab.dirty`) in the Phase 5 cross-platform pass; until then GTK is the more correct of the two here. (Could not be done in the same change that added the set because Win32 has no runner on the Linux dev host.)

- **~~Win32's close-confirm modal does not freeze `WM_APP_WAKE` dispatch; GTK's now freezes its wake — the two backends diverge on a tab-identity race.~~ Landed (Phase 5): `ui_win32` has its own `DrainFreeze`, and all three backends now protect the close prompt the same way.** `handle_close_active_tab_inner` takes the guard as its first statement — before the dirty sample, so nothing can move `active_tab` between the sample, the prompt it feeds, the `NPPN_FILEBEFORECLOSE` announcement and the close — and the `WM_APP_WAKE` arm, which is the backend's only `shell.drain` call site, returns without draining while one is held. Both Close-All loops (`close_multiple_documents` and the `ID_FILE_CLOSE_ALL` arm) hold one outer guard across the whole operation, which is what the depth count GTK's guard already carried "for a future Close All" was for.

  **The one Win32-shaped difference is who flushes.** On GTK and Cocoa the caller drains once after the guard drops; a swallowed wake there is a skipped idle, and the channels still hold the work. On Win32 a swallowed `WM_APP_WAKE` is a *consumed* window message, so the guard records it and the outermost `Drop` posts a fresh one to the main window. That moves the flush obligation into the guard rather than leaving it at every site that takes one — a future freeze site cannot forget it — and the re-post is asynchronous by construction, landing on the next pump iteration after the `wnd_proc` that held the guard has returned, so the deferred drain never runs with a close's borrow live. A `thread_local` depth and flag rather than a process-wide atomic like `PLUGIN_CALL_ACTIVE`, because only the UI thread touches them and per-thread storage lets the unit tests exercise a real guard on a test thread: with a null `HWND` the re-post lands on that thread's own queue, where `PeekMessageW` reads it back, so "a swallowed wake is re-posted exactly once, on the outermost release, and after a panic" is asserted against the actual `PostMessageW` rather than a mock.

  **This also closes the gap the FILEBEFORECLOSE entry above left open — and the first version of this change closed only half of it, which the pre-commit audit caught.** That gap was that the save prompt's pump could move `active_tab` before the announcement read it, so the tab prompted and the tab announced could differ; the entry's own suggested fix was to carry the id from the prompt too. The first draft argued the freeze made that unnecessary, on the grounds that the drain was the only path that could move `active_tab` under an owner-disabled modal. It is the only *host* path — a user cannot click the strip and `MessageBoxW`'s pump does not run the accelerator table — but the pump still dispatches a plugin's cross-thread `SendMessage(WM_COMMAND, ID_FILE_CLOSE)`, and this function deliberately holds no `PluginCallGuard` (the announcement needs the plugin's `NPPM_*` answered), so that runs a complete **nested** close whose own prompt the user may answer first. Staged against the pre-change build with the probe playing the plugin's worker thread: outer prompt for the dirty tab B, nested close from another thread, inner prompt answered Don't Save (B closes, A comes to the front), outer prompt answered Save — and the outer Yes **rewrote A.txt**, a file the user was never asked about, then announced and closed A as well, leaving a workspace of one untitled tab. Not the "confusing error message" the old comment promised as the floor. So the id *is* carried: the prelude records `prompted_id`, and `active_tab_is` re-checks it after the prompt before the answer is acted on, and again after the Save As chooser before the dirty re-read is trusted, aborting the close otherwise — the same stale-id refusal `close_announced_tab` already makes one phase later, applied to the prompt. Same probe on the fixed build: A.txt untouched, A still open, B closed by the inner close the user actually answered. Pinned by a source scan on both re-check positions, mutation-verified against removing either.

  **Verified by driving the real app against a pre-change build of the same tree, with a two-tab session.** `NPPM_MAKECURRENTBUFFERDIRTY` on the active tab, `WM_COMMAND(ID_FILE_CLOSE)` posted, then — with the Save / Don't Save / Cancel prompt up and the main window disabled — the *other* open file appended to on disk, which is a real file-watcher wake. The pre-change build shows **two** owned `#32770`s while the prompt is up, the reload prompt stacked on top of the Save prompt, i.e. the wake drained inside the modal pump exactly as the entry predicted; the fixed build holds at **one**, and the reload prompt appears on a *fresh* dialog handle only after Don't Save is answered, which is the re-posted wake landing after the close. Both builds persist one tab at quit, so the close itself is unchanged. Four source-scan guards pin what a headless test cannot see — the freeze precedes the sample and the prompt, the wake arm checks and returns before it drains, both Close-All loops hold an outer guard, and the wake arm is the only drain call site — and all eight mutations (freeze removed at each of the three sites, a Close-All guard moved *inside* its loop at each of the two, the wake arm falling through instead of returning, the guard re-posting on every release rather than the outermost, and never re-posting) are killed. The Close-All scan pins the guard against the loop's `for`, not only against the close call inside it, because a per-iteration guard precedes the close textually while re-posting a swallowed wake into the next iteration's prompt — the review found the first version of that scan would have passed it.

  **The second review round found the same hole one call deeper, and it is the one Cocoa's Save As rationale describes.** `run_save_as_flow` read the active tab only *after* `GetSaveFileNameW` returned and wrote it through `save_buffer_as`, so a nested close dispatched by the chooser's pump — the vector the freeze does not and cannot cover — put a different buffer's contents at the path the user had just chosen. The first draft of this entry claimed the close path's Save As was "covered by nesting"; that was true of the drain vector only. Staged against the pre-change build through the menu route, which needs no inner prompt: Save As opened on a clean titled B, a plugin thread's `ID_FILE_CLOSE` closes B under the chooser, the user types `C.txt` and presses Save — and **C.txt is written with A's contents and A is rebound to it**, so the user's session ends with a file named for one buffer holding another's text. `run_save_as_flow` now records the tab it is for before the chooser and refuses the write, with a `warn`, if that tab is no longer active — the same probe on the fixed build writes nothing and leaves A where it was. Because the gate lives in `run_save_as_flow`, the menu-driven Save As and the close path's untitled branch share it. The scan pins it between the chooser and the write, mutation-verified.

  **Not widened past that, recorded plainly.** The freeze covers the close path, which is what this entry tracked, and the id re-check covers the two places a stale answer could reach a write. Cocoa froze *every* modal; here the Open dialog, the reload prompt itself and the settings dialogs still run with the drain live, and each is one `DrainFreeze::new(hwnd)` away if a sample-then-pump-then-act shape is ever found in them. Original entry follows. When GTK's Save/Don't Save/Cancel prompt (the data-loss gate on closing a dirty tab) landed, a security audit found that a worker wake dispatched into the modal's nested main loop could `drain` the shell mid-prompt — `apply_load_result`'s failed-fresh-open branch and `apply_file_change` both move `active_tab` — sliding a different buffer under the user's decision. GTK closed it by prevention: a `DrainFreeze` RAII guard (a depth count, so a future Close All that loops the close path stays frozen until the outermost close finishes) makes `drain_shell` a no-op for the span of the close, then flushes once against the post-close state; the channels are unbounded so nothing is lost, only deferred. **Win32 has the same race still open** — `handle_close_active_tab_inner` documents it as accepted low-severity (its own comment: "the worst case is a confusing error message, not silent data loss"), on the reasoning that no current path clears an already-set `tab.path`. That reasoning is narrower than GTK's guard, which blocks the `active_tab`-shift vector outright. Backport the same freeze around Win32's modal pump in the Phase 5 plugin-host/sync sweep so the two backends protect the close prompt identically; until then, record that GTK is the stricter of the two here.

- **~~`EditorHandle` has no lifetime tie to the Scintilla widget it points at — decide the ownership model before tabs land on GTK.~~ Decided (Phase 5 m3): single view.** The question was real — `EditorHandle` is `Copy` with no `Drop` and holds raw pointers into a widget without expressing a lifetime, so nothing in the type system stops a copy outliving what it points at. `ui_gtk` resolves it the way `ui_win32` already did: one Scintilla widget is created at startup and never destroyed, removed or reassigned, and tabs get their own buffers through `SCI_SETDOCPOINTER` rather than their own views. The dangling-`direct_ptr` case therefore cannot arise, because there is no path that finalises a view.

  Recorded in three places rather than one, because a decision that lives only in a design document is not enforced: `EditorHandle::from_gtk_widget`'s safety section now states the *continuing* obligation (the original wording covered only the call, not the handle's later use), `GtkUiState.sci_widget`'s doc states how this backend discharges it, and `ui_gtk` carries a source-level guard asserting exactly one `scintilla_new()` with no destroy or reassignment. The guard is source-level on purpose: destroying the view would fault inside vendored C++ on the next direct call rather than fail an assertion, so a runtime test cannot observe the failure this exists to prevent. Mutation-verified against a second view, a destroy, and a reassignment.

  **This constrains any future backend.** A `ui_cocoa` that gives each tab its own `NSView` inherits the original problem and would have to tie the handle's lifetime to the widget instead.

- **~~Find-in-Files symlink hardening is Unix-only; Windows needs `FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT`.~~ Landed (Phase 5).** `read_capped` (`crates/shell/src/fif.rs`) grew a `#[cfg(windows)]` arm that passes `FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT` (`0x00200000`) through `OpenOptionsExt::custom_flags`. `CreateFileW` then returns a descriptor to the reparse point itself; `Metadata::file_type()` (via the handle-bound `GetFileInformationByHandleEx(FileAttributeTagInfo)` std makes for reparse-tag classification) surfaces `is_symlink() == true` and `is_file() == false`, and the existing `is_file()` gate refuses it as `NotARegularFile`. Bare-hex constant instead of pulling `windows::Win32::Storage::FileSystem` into `codepp-shell` — the value is ABI-frozen since Windows 2000.

  Windows adversarial coverage: two new tests, both `#[ignore]`d because their setup (`std::os::windows::fs::symlink_file`) needs `SeCreateSymbolicLinkPrivilege` (developer mode). `#[ignore]` was chosen deliberately over a runtime skip after the security audit demonstrated that a runtime skip is invisible in `cargo test`'s default output — a runner without developer mode would silently drop coverage while reporting fully green. The two ignored tests are `read_capped_refuses_a_symlink` (pins the fix — the audit mutation-tested that neutralising `FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT` surfaces here as an `Ok(_)` where a reject was required) and `atomic_write_replaces_a_symlink_at_the_destination` (closes the sub-item this section previously flagged as untested — `NamedTempFile::persist` via `MoveFileExW` replaces the destination reparse point rather than following it). A non-ignored `read_capped_refuses_a_junction` runs on every runner (junctions need no privilege via `mklink /J`) as belt-and-suspenders coverage — it does not itself regression-cover `FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT` (its reject fires via `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` on the directory open, independent of the flag), documented plainly in the test's own comment.

  CI runner requirement recorded in `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` §2.6: the `windows` runner needs developer mode enabled and runs the workspace tests with `cargo test -- --include-ignored`, otherwise the fix has no live regression coverage.

- **`core::session::Session` has no `clamp`, so every string a hostile `session.xml` carries is materialised in full before any cap applies.** Found by the security audit of the name-keyed UDL persistence entry later in this section, and informational rather than a finding against it: the new `@udl` is one more unbounded `String` on `core::session::Tab` beside `path`, `custom_name` and `backup`, all of which have had the same shape since they shipped. `MAX_SESSION_TABS` (512) is applied by `Shell` at the two consumption sites, *after* `quick_xml::de::from_str` has already built every `Tab`, so a file with a million tabs each carrying a megabyte string is parsed before it is trimmed. `Styles::clamp` and `PluginShortcuts::clamp` are the precedent for what this wants — bound on read, warn when the on-disk value did not survive verbatim — and a `Session::clamp` called from `load_from_xml` would cover all four fields at once rather than capping whichever one a future audit happens to look at. No exposure beyond memory: none of these strings reaches a display sink unsanitized, and the resolved UDL *name* a backend renders comes from the registry entry rather than from the session file. A dock panel's `@seal` is the same shape once more: it is checked for being exactly 64 hex digits only after the parser has built the string.

- **Decide, explicitly, whether the 43% memory rise from m1 is accepted.** §8 now carries the per-mapping attribution rather than a guess: PSS went 8.17 MB (m1) to 11.81 MB (m3), and the growth is **+2.60 MB of `.text`, +0.85 MB of `.rodata`, and exactly zero heap**. So it is static footprint — code and tables that are resident because startup executes them — not a leak, and it does not scale with the number or size of open files. That is the strongest case for accepting it, and 11.8 MB is 47% of the 25 MB budget. It is still past §8's own ">20%" bar, and §8 says non-negotiable with no headroom clause, so the rule wants an explicit answer rather than a silent pass. If the answer is "reduce it instead", the three untried levers are named in §8 — compile `lang_theme`'s 85-branch chain to a table lookup, `opt-level = "s"`, or `lto = "fat"` — and each is a measurable experiment, not a guess. Whoever decides should also fix the baseline: §8's m1 row is the *only* figure the doc ever recorded before m3, which is how a two-milestone drift went unnoticed; recording PSS at every phase boundary would have caught it at m2.

- **Find what stalls ~1% of keystrokes above roughly 15 characters per second.** §8 now characterises the tail properly — 5 016 samples at a fast-human rate put p99 at 4.62 ms, inside the 5 ms budget — but at 20 char/s p99 jumps to 65 ms while p50 and p90 barely move. That shape is a cliff, not a backlog: something occasional blocks for tens of milliseconds rather than everything getting slower. Two candidates were tested and refuted, so do not start there: the 7-second session auto-save (interval raised to 100 000 s, p99 unchanged at 65.4 ms) and Lexilla restyling (same-size plain-text file with no lexer, p99 unchanged at 60.5 ms). It is content-independent and rate-dependent, and the worst sample at human rates was 17.8 ms, so it never reaches the budget in normal use.

  Next step is a profiler, not another black-box run — the remaining suspects (GTK frame-clock scheduling, compositor frame misses, a status-bar label width change forcing a relayout, GDK event coalescing) are indistinguishable from the outside. `--perf` now reports the arrival distribution alongside latency, which is what any repeat of this needs: the input rate has to be measured rather than assumed, and it must be checked against the numbers in §8 rather than against a tool's `--delay` flag.

- **~~A failed load leaks its Scintilla document when the tab was bound before the load resolved, on every backend.~~ Landed (Phase 5): `UiPlatform::release_doc`.** The trait grew exactly the channel the original entry (kept below) called for — `release_doc(&mut self, doc: isize)`, a `doc == 0`-guarded `SCI_RELEASEDOCUMENT` on all three backends, **required rather than defaulted** for the reason recorded there: a no-op default would leave a backend silently leaking while looking implemented. `Shell::apply_failed_load` releases the removed fresh tab's document before the `remove`; the borrowed-scratch arm keeps its tab and releases nothing, since the kept tab still owns its document.

  The release may run while the document is still bound (the active-tab trigger), which is safe by the refcount shape `ui_cocoa`'s `LAST_SEEDED_DOC` docs already establish: the view holds its own reference, so a bound document only drops 2→1 and is freed by the rebind that follows — after the incoming document has been allocated — while an unbound one (the clicked-away trigger) frees immediately. Both existing failed-load regression tests were extended, and the pair was mutation-verified in both directions: deleting the release call fails the prematurely-bound test's `released_docs == [doomed_doc]` assertion, and keying the release on "owns a document" instead of on `is_fresh` fails the kept-scratch test's `released_docs.is_empty()` assertion. Only the shipped shape — release inside the `is_fresh` arm — passes both.

  The original analysis, kept because the triggers are what any future change to the binder must preserve: `Shell::bind_active_view` materialises a document for any active tab that lacks one, with no `pending_load` gate — so a tab whose load is still in flight acquires one as soon as the user clicks back onto it, and `Shell::load_npp_session` reaches the same state deterministically by restoring the recorded-active tab mid-flight. If that load then failed, `apply_failed_load` removed the tab and `self.tabs.remove` dropped its `scintilla_doc` with it; `close_active_tab`'s `ClosedTab` was the only channel by which a document pointer ever reached a backend's `SCI_RELEASEDOCUMENT`, so nothing freed it. Long-standing rather than new — surfaced by the `open_file_replacing_scratch` audit — and not data loss (the leaked document is empty), but a native-resource leak reachable from ordinary use.

- **~~A background load left its tab's Scintilla document stale, and `Save All` then wrote that document over the real file.~~ Landed (Phase 5), on all three backends — the shell half (`Tab::doc_needs_text`, `has_unsaved_work`, `bind_and_fill`, `save_all` routed through it) in the original change, and the Win32 tab-activation half in `ec07779`; see the struck-through sub-item at the end of this entry.** Reported by a user on macOS: File → Open All Recent Files opened every file, and the first showed its filename over an empty buffer; reopening the same file through File → Open worked, which made it look like a loader bug rather than a binding one.

  `bind_active_view` filled a tab's document only when `scintilla_doc == 0`. That is a correct proxy for "never populated" exactly while every background load targets a *fresh* tab — the assumption `apply_load_result`'s own comment states. `open_file_replacing_scratch` breaks it by re-targeting a load onto the startup `new 1`, whose document already exists: the first file in a multi-open is the only one that reuses the scratch, and the opens that follow move `active_tab` off it before its load lands. New `Tab::doc_needs_text` records the case and a new `Shell::bind_and_fill` — the single place that knows how to make a document match its tab — consumes it.

  **The pre-commit gate found the reported symptom was the mild half of the problem.** `Shell::save_all` bound each tab's document with a bare `activate_tab` and read it straight back with `get_buffer_text` to write to disk, so a stale binding there *overwrote the file with the wrong bytes* rather than merely displaying them — reachable as Open All Recent Files followed by Save All without ever clicking the first tab. That predates the flag; it is what made the flag worth having rather than what the flag caused. Worse, the same path applied to a tab whose document was never created at all: `activate_tab(idx, 0)` hands back an *empty* document, so an unvisited background tab's file was rewritten empty. `save_all` now routes through `bind_and_fill` like every other binder, and `Tab::doc_needs_text`'s doc states the obligation for the next one.

  **Filling must never happen over unsaved edits**, and that hazard is created by the fix rather than pre-existing. `confirm_reload` re-targets a load onto whichever tab holds the path, active or not, and is reached from the file-watcher prompt, File → Reload and — with no prompt at all — a plugin's `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID`. Before the flag, a background reload was inert: it updated `Tab.text` and left the document alone. With it, the next activation would have filled through `set_buffer_text`, which empties the undo buffer and sets the save point — silent, unrecoverable loss of the user's work, on every backend and with no user action beyond clicking a tab. The flag is therefore gated on `is_doc_dirty` where it is *set*, and again where it is *consumed*, because Replace-in-Files can dirty a background document in between; `apply_open_buffer_replacement` also clears it, for the reverse ordering its own `is_doc_dirty` check cannot see.

  **The first version of that guard asked the wrong question, and the audit reproduced the consequence against a real `Shell` before it shipped.** It checked `is_doc_dirty` alone — but a buffer restored from a crash-recovery backup is seeded through `set_buffer_text`, which *sets the save point*, so Scintilla reports it clean while its contents exist nowhere but memory. `Shell::unsaved_restore_ids` is the durable record, and `tab_needs_backup` had always ORed the two; the new guards did not. A plugin's `NPPM_RELOADBUFFERID` on a background restored buffer therefore flagged it, and the next click replaced the recovered work with the on-disk text — then `save_all` persisted that and cleared the recovery marker, so nothing recorded the work had ever existed. All three guards now go through one `has_unsaved_work` helper, `apply_open_buffer_replacement`'s pre-existing `is_doc_dirty` gate included, since Replace-in-Files had the same blind spot. `FakeUi`'s dirty flag was per-*fixture* rather than per-document, which is the second reason an ordinary test could not have caught this; it now takes per-document overrides.

  Reproduced end to end against the real app, both directions: without the fix the first tab reports `("file1.txt", 0, "")` while the other two are correct; with it, all three carry their content. Four shell-level regression tests, all mutation-verified, and each asserts its own precondition so none can pass vacuously.

  **`FakeUi` was upgraded to model one text per document**, which is the reason this class of bug could ship: its single shared text slot meant `save_all_writes_every_titled_tab` passed while `save_all` was binding an unpopulated document, because the previous tab's text was still sitting in the slot. Two existing tests' expectations encoded that leak and were corrected — they now assert each file gets *its own* buffer's content.

  **~~Win32 is not covered by the `bind_and_fill` half and keeps a pre-existing gap.~~ Closed (Phase 5): `handle_tab_selchange` goes through `Shell::bind_active_view`, and Win32's savepoint handler and close gate consult `is_unsaved_restore`.** Closed on a Windows host because the `NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT` work made the gap load-bearing rather than merely stale: a shadow `Tab::text` converted on a background tab is marked `shadow_unsaved` and relies on `bind_and_fill` promoting that into `unsaved_restore_ids` at install, and the hand-rolled Win32 path installed it clean at the save point instead. `Win32Ui::activate_tab` and `set_buffer_text` already did everything the hand-rolled block did (plus the line-number remeasure it lacked), so the replacement is one `split()` and one call.

  That turned out to be half of it, and the probe is what said so. Routed through the shell, the promotion happened — and the tab still closed without a prompt, because on this backend the id set was consulted by *nothing*: the `SCN_SAVEPOINT*` arm wrote `Tab.dirty` from the notification alone, and the close gate read `SCI_GETMODIFY || Tab.dirty`. The install's own `SCN_SAVEPOINTREACHED` therefore cleared the cached bit an instant before the id went into the set. That is the same mechanism as the §7.4 "Win32's dirty glyph can momentarily go blue on a restored buffer" entry, and the fix is the one that entry prescribed: the handler ORs `is_unsaved_restore` in, as `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` always have, the close gate does too as defence in depth, and both promotion sites re-assert the cached bit — so a crash-restored buffer's glyph no longer flips clean after an edit-then-undo either. Consulting the set from the handler has an ordering cost, and it bit twice before landing: the two save paths and the reload's install branch all released the id *after* the `SCI_SETSAVEPOINT` whose notification now reads it, which would have left the glyph red after a save or a reload; all three release first now, and the reload arm clears the cached bit explicitly rather than trusting the notification — `release_marker_before_install` / `settle_marker_after_install`. *Original entry:* `ui_win32` has no `bind_active_view` call site at all: `handle_tab_selchange` carries its own materialise-on-first-activation logic, gated on `scintilla_doc == 0`, so it ignores `doc_needs_text`. The reused-scratch case cannot reach it (Win32 does not call `open_file_replacing_scratch` — see the entry below), but a background *reload* can, and leaves a stale document exactly as it did before. No regression, since that path was equally stale before the flag existed; the `save_all` fix, being shell-level, does reach Win32. Closing it means routing Win32's tab activation through `Shell::bind_active_view` as `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` do, which is a change to the largest crate in the workspace with no runner on the macOS development host.

  **Verified by driving the real Win32 app, with a control.** Two-tab session, `NPPM_SETBUFFERFORMAT(WIN_FORMAT)` on the background tab, `NPPM_ACTIVATEDOC` onto it — both builds show the converted text (length 9 → 12, EOL mode CRLF, `SCI_GETMODIFY` 0). Then `ID_FILE_CLOSE`: the fixed build keeps two tabs open with the main window disabled under the Save / Don't Save / Cancel prompt; the pre-change build drops to one tab with no prompt, and the conversion is gone. The control also wrote a recovery backup at quit, which looked like the fix working and was not: it was `shadow_unsaved` stranded `true` by the hand-rolled path — `tab_needs_backup` ORs it — which is the frozen-shadow backup the review of the reload change had predicted.

- **~~`ui_gtk` and `ui_win32` do not yet replace the untouched `new 1` on open.~~ Landed on both (Phase 5) — and on Win32 it replaced a whole mechanism rather than adding a call.** `Shell::open_file_replacing_scratch` is cross-platform and needs only a call-site swap plus a three-send helper (`SCI_GETLENGTH == 0 && !SCI_CANUNDO && !SCI_CANREDO`) on each backend — see `ui_cocoa::editor_is_pristine`, and note the redo half is load-bearing rather than defensive (typing one character and undoing it leaves `SCI_CANUNDO == 0` but `SCI_CANREDO == 1`, measured).

  The call sites are named here so the follow-up is unambiguous, because picking the wrong one is the way to get this subtly wrong: only *user-initiated* opens should opt in. On GTK that is `menu::open_paths` (`crates/ui_gtk/src/menu.rs`), which serves File→Open and drag-and-drop — and only its first iteration can match, since after one open the workspace is no longer a lone tab. Session restore (`lib.rs`, two sites) and the Find-in-Files jump (`fif.rs`) must keep the plain `open_file`: a restore is replaying a saved workspace, and a FIF jump is navigation, neither of which should consume a buffer. Win32 has around eight call sites and needs the same triage.

  **The GTK half landed on a Linux host, driven end to end rather than reasoned about**, which is what the paragraph above was waiting for. `crate::editor_is_pristine` is the three-send helper and `menu::open_paths` is the single opt-in call site — it serves File → Open, drag-and-drop, the recent-files region and the workspace tree, which is exactly the set `ui_cocoa::open_path` covers, so the two backends agree by construction rather than by inspection. Session restore and the Find-in-Files jump keep the plain `open_file`, as this entry always specified.

  Verified against the real app under a scratch `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, reading the tab list back out of the `session.xml` written at quit — an observation that does not depend on a screenshot. Opening a file into the startup workspace leaves **one** tab; typing one character into `new 1`, undoing it, then opening leaves **two**, with the untitled buffer's backup intact. Both mutation-verified, and the pair is the point: dropping the `SCI_CANREDO` term collapses the second case to one tab — i.e. it silently discards a buffer the user had been working in — while reverting the call site to `open_file` restores two tabs in the first. Neither mutation is visible to a unit test, because the property is "what the editor measures", which is why this waited for a host that could run it.

  **The Win32 half is not the call-site swap this entry predicted, because Win32 already had its own answer to the same problem and it was a different one.** `discard_sole_empty_untitled` *closed* the scratch tab before the open — a hand-rolled 130-line helper called from eight sites — where the shell gate *retargets* it. Both end at one tab, so the two are indistinguishable from the tab strip; what differs is everything underneath. Retargeting keeps the tab's `id` (so a buffer id a plugin cached stays valid) and keeps its Scintilla document (filled by the load through `doc_needs_text`), and it never produces the zero-tab window a close-then-open leaves — which is why `handle_load_session` could drop the `ensure_one_tab` call it made on *every* exit path, the parse-failure one included, purely to heal that window. So the helper is gone rather than kept alongside.

  It was also **measuring the wrong thing**: `SCI_GETLENGTH == 0` alone, so a buffer typed into and erased back to empty was discarded outright, with the tab's undo history going with it. Driving the pre-change build confirms it — type one character, undo it, open a file, and the buffer is gone. That is the case the redo term exists for, and Win32 never had it.

  The triage this entry asked for, settled: File → Open, drag-and-drop, all three recent-files handlers, the workspace-tree double-click and the workspace context menu's Open opt in, through one `open_user_path`; startup session restore (two sites in `run`) and the Find-in-Files result jump keep the plain `open_file`, matching `ui_gtk::fif` and `ui_cocoa::fif`. File → Load Session opts in through a new `editor_is_pristine` parameter on `Shell::load_npp_session` rather than through the helper, because its open loop lives in the shell — so GTK inherited the same behaviour from the same parameter, which is the one place all three backends cannot drift on it.

  **Win32's measurement carries one term the other two do not, and it is the surviving half of the deleted helper.** `active_buffer_is_pristine` confirms the view is actually bound to the tab the shell is about to judge (`SCI_GETDOCPOINTER` against `Tab::scintilla_doc`) before trusting the three-send read, because this backend binds lazily and defers rebinds — `handle_tab_selchange` and `apply_load_result` — so `active_tab` can move a step ahead of `SCI_SETDOCPOINTER`. A mismatch reports **not pristine**, which keeps the buffer; the old helper bailed the same way, and the code review's suggestion of a `debug_assert` instead was declined for the reason the direction matters: an assert aborts `cargo run`, while failing closed costs at worst a scratch tab that survives an open. No path is known that reaches the gate with a stale binding today, but that invariant is spread across three call sites and enforced by nothing.

  **Three clauses were added to the shared gate itself**, so all three backends inherit them. `!t.pinned` is a real fix carried over from the deleted helper, which had it — a pinned scratch is the same "keep this across automatic close operations" signal `CloseMultiKind::AllButPinned` honours, and GTK and Cocoa had been ignoring it since the gate landed. `!t.doc_needs_text` and `!t.shadow_unsaved` came out of the security audit and are defence rather than a fix: a tab whose real content sits in `Tab::text` awaiting `bind_and_fill` has a document that legitimately reads empty, and it is the one thing the UI's measurement cannot stand in for. Every setter of either is gated on something a lone *active* scratch cannot be, so nothing reaches it today; the failure it would produce is silent and unrecoverable, so the gate states it rather than resting on that.

  **Verified by driving the real Win32 app against a pre-change build of the same tree**, under a scratch `APPDATA` with a seeded `recent_files.xml`, reading the tab count back off `SysTabControl32`. Opening into a pristine `new 1` leaves **one** tab on both builds. Typing one character and undoing it leaves **two** on the fixed build and **one** on the pre-change build — the data-loss case, reproduced. Typing a character and keeping it leaves two on both. The editor's own terms are reported alongside each run, and the middle case reads `len=0 canundo=0 canredo=1` on Win32 exactly as it does on the other two backends, which is the measurement this entry recorded as load-bearing. File → Load Session was driven end to end through the real file dialog: a two-file session into a pristine workspace gives two tabs (not three), and an all-filtered session gives one — and both builds agree, which is the point, since the old close-then-reseed reached the same end state by a different route.

  Six source-scan guards pin what a headless test cannot see — which callers keep the plain `open_file`, that both gate sites measure rather than pass a constant, the three terms of the pristine test, the binding check and its fail-closed direction, and that Load Session neither passes a constant nor re-seeds a tab — all mutation-verified. One of them was **fake on its first write**: the fail-closed guard matched a bare `return false;`, which the function's *other* early return already satisfies, so flipping the mismatch arm to `return true` — the data-loss direction — passed it. Caught by mutating it rather than by reading it, and it now matches the whole arm. The same shape as the m3c guard that matched its own comment and the m4d guard satisfied by a different call site: **a source scan must match the construct, not the identifier.**

- **~~`ui_gtk`'s Unfold All ceilings do not bound anything, because the final reveal re-enters the lazy loader.~~ Landed (Phase 5, GTK parity batch).** `tick_unfold`'s completion arm now calls a ported `expand_populated` — expanding only folders the walk actually read and leaving the rest collapsed and still lazily expandable — with a source-scan guard banning `expand_all(` outright. Original entry follows. Found by the macOS m4d gate while porting the same walk. `tick_unfold`'s completion arm calls `expand_all()` unconditionally, under a comment asserting every row is already populated — which holds when the queue *drained* and not when `UNFOLD_MAX_FOLDERS` or `UNFOLD_MAX_ROWS` stopped it. `GtkTreeView::expand_all` walks every row and fires `row-expanded`, which is where `populate_children` hangs, so every folder the ceiling declined gets read anyway: recursively, synchronously, in one unbatched call. That is strictly worse than no ceiling, since the batching is what kept the UI responsive.

  Measured on the Cocoa side, which has the identical shape: on a 340-directory tree with the folder ceiling lowered to 5, the recursive reveal populated all 341 directories against the correct 20. The fix is `ui_cocoa::workspace::expand_populated` — expand only folders already read, leaving the rest collapsed and still lazily expandable, which is what the ceilings' own documentation always claimed happened. Not fixed on GTK here because there is no Linux runner on the macOS development host, and the failure mode is a UI hang on a pathological tree, which is exactly the thing not to land unexercised.

- **~~`ui_gtk::style_config` puts an unsanitized font name into its font combo, and `apply_default_style` ignores its own documented transparency floor on the two non-Windows backends.~~ Landed (Phase 5, GTK parity batch).** (1) The font combo now carries `sanitize_str_for_display` labels with a `font_faces` side table read back by index — the same value-vs-label split `ui_cocoa::style_config` uses, so a hostile face name renders substituted while `read_styles` returns the real one. (2) The floor moved into `Styles::clamp` in `core` rather than being clamped per backend, so it is inherited by all three and by both the `load_from_xml` read path and the `set_styles` write path; it warns when the on-disk value did not survive verbatim, which is the Win32 behaviour this was meant to match. `TRANSPARENCY_PERCENT_MIN`/`MAX` back the remaining GTK/Cocoa range asserts as defence in depth. Original entry follows. Both found by the macOS m4g audit.

  (1) `combo.append_text(current)` inserts a face read from `styles.xml` verbatim, so a name carrying bidi controls renders reordered — the same class as the three incidents §7.4 already records. The fix is not a plain sanitize: the combo's text is *also* the value read back by `read_styles`, so substituting would rewrite the user's stored font. `ui_cocoa::style_config` shows the shape — sanitized titles plus a side table of real faces read back by index.

  (2) `apply_default_style` clamps `Transparency::percent` to `0..=100` in `ui_gtk` (`platform.rs`) and `ui_cocoa` (`platform.rs`), while the field's own doc states a 20% floor that "the dialog's slider enforces". A hand-edited `styles.xml` with `percent="0"` therefore yields a fully transparent — still focusable, still clickable — main window, with no in-app way to see what to click.

  **Win32 is not affected and is the reference for what the other two should do.** An earlier revision of this entry said all three backends ignored the floor; that was wrong in two ways, and the correction is worth recording because it changes the shape of the fix. Win32 does not apply transparency from `apply_default_style` at all — it has a separate `apply_window_transparency` (`ui_win32/src/lib.rs`), which clamps to `20..=100` and additionally emits a `tracing::warn!` so a log reader can tell the on-disk value was snapped. So this is a two-backend port, not a three-backend one, and the behaviour to port includes the warning rather than just the bound.

  `Styles::clamp` bounds the font name's length and nothing else (verified: it calls `truncate_font_name` and returns). The floor belongs there, so every consumer inherits it rather than each backend remembering — and `clamp` already runs on both the `load_from_xml` read path and the shell's `set_styles` write path, which is exactly the coverage this needs. Doing it in `core` is a change that wants all three backends exercised, and it would let the two non-Windows clamps drop to a plain range assert.

  Neither is fixed here because there is no Linux or Windows runner on the macOS development host, and (2) is worth landing in one go rather than leaving the two non-Windows backends disagreeing with Win32 about their own floor.

- **~~A plugin that sends `SCI_*` from its own thread reaches Scintilla off the main thread on both non-Windows backends.~~ Landed on both (Phase 5); the Cocoa half is described at the end of this entry.** Found by the macOS m5 audit, and it is a characteristic of the SDK's non-Windows transport rather than of either backend. On Windows, `SendMessage` from a plugin's worker thread is marshaled by the OS onto the thread that owns the window, so a plugin written against that semantics is safe by construction. Off Windows the SDK forwards straight to the host callback on whatever thread called it: `NPPM_*` degrades safely (the state is a `thread_local`, so the dispatcher finds nothing and returns "declined") but `SCI_*` reaches `objc_msgSend` on a `ScintillaView` — or `scintilla_send_message` on a `GtkWidget` — from the wrong thread, which both toolkits document as undefined. That branch deliberately bypasses `with_state`, and must, so it has no backstop.

  **Decided (Phase 5): marshal. Landed on GTK first, then on Cocoa.** The two candidates were refusing (return 0, as the unknown-handle branch already does) and marshaling onto the UI thread. Refusing is three lines and trivially safe, and it is the worse failure: a query — `SCI_GETLENGTH`, `SCI_GETCURRENTPOS` — comes back 0, which is a *plausible* answer rather than an obviously wrong one, and a mutation becomes a silent no-op, so the plugin appears to work while its edits vanish. That is the silently-half-working shape this document keeps recording as the one to avoid.

  The deadlock objection turns out not to distinguish the two, which is why it did not decide it. A cross-thread `SendMessage` on Win32 *also* blocks until the target thread next pumps, and *also* deadlocks if that thread is meanwhile waiting on the sender — so marshaling inherits Win32's hazard rather than introducing one, which is the entire point of reproducing the semantics a ported plugin was written against.

  `ui_gtk::plugin` now records the UI thread's `ThreadId` in `discover` and routes the `SCI_*` branch through `send_sci_on_main`: a `MainContext::invoke` carrying an explicitly-`Send` pointer wrapper, plus a `sync_channel` the calling thread parks on. **No timeout, deliberately** — a bounded wait would have to invent a return value on expiry and the only one available is 0, converting a visible stall into exactly the silent wrong answer the paragraph above rejects. The identity check runs *before* the affinity check, so an unknown handle is still refused rather than marshaled.

  One claim in the first draft of that code was wrong and is recorded because it was plausible: the same-thread fast path was documented as required for *correctness*, on the reasoning that the main thread does not own the context while sitting outside an iteration and would therefore queue and then block on the loop that would have run it. Measured by calling `send_sci_on_main` directly from the UI thread — it returns Scintilla's real answer immediately. `g_main_context_invoke_full` acquires the context when it can and dispatches inline. The branch earns its place by avoiding a channel allocation and by naming which thread a call is on, not by averting a hang.

  Covered by a display-gated scenario that drives the real `plugin_dispatch` against a real Scintilla widget from a real spawned thread, asserting the call is still outstanding after 200 ms of *not* running the main loop and answers correctly once it does — mutation-verified against an affinity check hard-wired to "main". What that does not cover is the SDK's own forwarding, which is unchanged here; the app-level check is that the four in-tree plugins still load lazily on first menu open and `example_hello`'s `SCI_INSERTTEXT` still reaches the buffer.

  **One accepted risk is recorded rather than closed**, per this section's usual practice. The wait is unbounded, so a plugin that calls `SCI_*` from a thread it never lets finish parks that thread until the process exits. That is a leaked thread and not a visible hang — nothing joins plugin worker threads, and `exit` does not wait on threads nobody joined — but it *becomes* a hang the moment host code waits for plugin threads to quiesce during teardown. Any future shutdown path that does so must not block on plugin threads; the alternative is a bounded wait, which means solving the invented-return-value problem rather than ignoring it. Noted at `send_sci_on_main` as well as here.

  **The Cocoa half (landed).** `ui_cocoa::plugin::send_sci_on_main` is `DispatchQueue::main().exec_sync` — `dispatch_sync` onto the main queue — behind the same identity-then-affinity ordering, with the result coming back through a borrowed slot rather than a channel, since `exec_sync` requires `Send` but not `'static` and the caller is parked for the block's whole lifetime by construction. Two things are Cocoa-shaped rather than ported. The affinity check needs **no arming step**: `MainThreadMarker::new()` is `pthread_main_np`, and macOS fixes the main thread at process start, where GTK's "UI thread" is whichever thread called `gtk::init` and so has to be *recorded* in `discover`. And the same-thread fast path is **load-bearing rather than an optimisation** — `dispatch_sync` from the main thread onto the main queue would park the only thread that could ever run the block, and libdispatch answers that by aborting the process — so `send_sci_on_main` carries an unconditional `assert` that it is never entered from the main thread, and the fast path's rationale in the source is "avoids a crash" rather than GTK's "avoids a channel allocation". The hop runs inside libdispatch's own C frames, which the calling thread's `catch_unwind` cannot cover, so it carries its own `at_callback_boundary`; a panic there logs, leaves the slot unset, and the caller gets 0. The shutdown risk is the same accepted one as GTK's: `terminate:` calls `exit()` without joining, so a worker parked here at quit is a leaked thread, and it becomes a hang only if a future teardown path waits on plugin threads.

  **The scenario lives in the `harness = false` smoke binary, not in the crate's test module, and that is forced.** It drives the real `plugin_dispatch` against a real `ScintillaView` from a real spawned thread, which needs the process main thread — the thing libtest never yields. The two private items it needs are re-exported through a `#[doc(hidden)] smoke_support` module rather than the scenario moving in-crate — compiled only under `debug_assertions`, with its arming function `unsafe`, because it rewrites the very trust anchor the identity check reads and "nothing else calls it" is a fact about today's tree rather than a guarantee (the security audit's one finding). It asserts the three things the GTK scenario asserts — same-thread answered directly (the control, which also proves the check is armed), cross-thread still outstanding after 200 ms of not pumping, then the real answer once `runMode:beforeDate:` on the main run loop drains the queue (measured to do so: the main queue is serviced only in a *common* mode, which the default mode is) — plus a bogus handle from a worker refused rather than marshaled. Mutation-verified: with the affinity check hard-wired to "main", the two earlier scenarios pass and this one fails on exactly the "answered without the main queue being drained" assertion. A source scan pins the shape on every runner: the direct send inside `if on_main_thread()`, the marshal in its `else`, `exec_sync` in the hop, no `with_state` in either the hop or the affinity check, the assert being unconditional, and the test surface being `cfg(debug_assertions)` and `unsafe` — mutation-verified against removing the gate and against removing the assert.

  Two things worth recording from running it. The smoke binary launched from a non-interactive shell spends about four minutes inside `NSApplication::sharedApplication` before the first scenario starts and then completes all three in about a second — the stall precedes every line of test code, and DEVELOPMENT.md §4.5 now says so. And the GTK entry's "measured, and false" note about the fast path being merely an optimisation does **not** carry over — though not in the shape this entry predicted either. It said `dispatch_sync` from the main queue to itself "deadlocks outright"; hard-wiring the check to "remote" and running the smoke binary shows the control **crashing** instead, exit 133 with libdispatch's *"BUG IN CLIENT OF LIBDISPATCH: dispatch_sync called on queue already owned by current thread"* in the crash report. libdispatch detects the self-wait and aborts rather than parking. The consequence for a plugin is the same as the one predicted — the fast path is the only thing between a same-thread `SCI_*` and a dead process — but the mechanism is recorded as measured, and the first draft of the source comments, which repeated the deadlock claim, was corrected before landing.

- **~~`ui_gtk`'s Plugin Manager does not freeze the drain while its modal is up.~~ Landed (Phase 5, GTK parity batch).** `show_plugin_manager`'s `dialog.run()` is now wrapped in a `DrainFreeze`, with a flush after, mirroring `ui_cocoa::plugin::show_plugin_manager`. Original entry follows. Found by the macOS m5 review while comparing the two. `ui_gtk::plugin::show_plugin_manager` calls `dialog.run()`, which spins a nested GTK main loop, with no equivalent of the `DrainFreeze` guard `ui_cocoa` holds around its own `runModal` — so a worker wake dispatched into that loop can `drain` the shell mid-dialog. Same class as the close-prompt race §7.4 already records in the other direction (there GTK was the stricter of the two and Win32 the laxer, until Win32 landed its own `DrainFreeze`). Lower severity than that one, because the manager's snapshot is index-keyed against a registry that nothing worker-driven mutates — the realistic worst case is a stale row rather than a wrong plugin being disabled — but the argument for it being unreachable is again about what a *user* can do, which is the premise that stops holding the moment a plugin or a timer can touch the shell. Not fixed here because there is no Linux runner on the macOS development host; the fix is one RAII guard, mirroring `ui_cocoa::plugin::show_plugin_manager`.
