- **~~Plugin menu-item shortcuts are not implemented on *any* backend — Win32 included.~~ Landed on all three (Phase 5), the Notepad++ way: a persisted `shortcuts.xml` cache.** `FuncItem._pShKey` previously had **no reader anywhere in the workspace** — `populate_plugin_menu` consumed `item_name`, `p_func` and `cmd_id` only, no accelerator was installed, and Win32 carried only *half* the machinery (`NPPM_GETSHORTCUTBYCMDID`/`NPPM_REMOVESHORTCUTBYCMDID` introspected/filtered the HACCEL, but nothing ever *added* a plugin binding to it). So §6.4's third lazy-load trigger ("fires a hotkey owned by the plugin") did not exist anywhere.

  **The circularity and how the cache breaks it.** The only source of a `ShortcutKey` is `getFuncsArray`, which runs at *load* — so under §6.4's lazy loading, a hotkey cannot trigger the load that would have taught the host the hotkey. Notepad++ escapes this by caching plugin shortcuts across sessions, and Code++ now does the same. New `core::shortcuts` (`crates/core/src/shortcuts.rs`) reads/writes `<config_dir>/shortcuts.xml` in N++'s own `<PluginCommands>` shape (`moduleName`/`internalID`/`Ctrl`/`Alt`/`Shift`/`Key`), so a migrating user can copy their file over. The persisted key is **`(module filename, FuncItem index)`**, never the runtime `cmd_id` — cmd ids are assigned in load order and shift when the plugin set changes, so they cannot be a durable key (the same reason `disabled.txt` keys on the basename). The cache holds the *effective* shortcut per command: on load, `Shell::ensure_plugins_loaded` folds each plugin's `_pShKey` defaults in via `absorb_plugin_shortcut_defaults`, and an existing entry always wins over a default, which is what makes a hand-edited remap stick. `PluginShortcuts::clamp` bounds it on read (drops zero-key / out-of-range-index / oversized-module entries, dedupes, caps the count), same discipline as `Styles::clamp`.

  **The shell owns policy; backends own mechanism.** `Shell::startup_plugin_chords` returns the registrable, discovered, first-wins-deduped chord set a backend installs at startup; `Shell::match_plugin_chord` resolves a pressed chord to a `(module_key, internalID)` identity at fire time (so a live `NPPM_REMOVESHORTCUTBYCMDID` takes effect on the next keypress); `Shell::resolve_plugin_command` turns that identity into the loaded `(cmd_id, PluginCmd)`. `core::shortcuts::is_registrable_chord` refuses bare/Shift-only non-function keys (they would swallow typing) and the Scintilla-native chords (Ctrl+X/C/V/Z/Y/A) the accelerator table has always deliberately omitted. The `HostServices` bridge routes plugin-band `NPPM_GET/REMOVESHORTCUTBYCMDID` through the cache, so the introspection Win32 already had now sees plugin bindings too, on every backend.

  **Cost and the shared authority.** `startup_plugin_chords` (registration list) and `match_plugin_chord` (fire-time lookup) both read a **memoized `PluginChordIndex`** on `Shell` — a `Vec` plus a `HashMap<(ctrl,alt,shift,key),(module,id)>` built in one pass, filtered to discovered + registrable + first-wins-deduped. It is rebuilt only when the cache or discovered set changes (`absorb`, `remove`, `discover`, `set_disabled` each invalidate it), never per call. That is not a micro-optimization: `shortcuts.xml` is hand-editable to a megabyte, and the pre-memoization recompute (an O(n²) dedupe) ran on the cold-start path, on every Plugins-menu rebuild, and on every ⌘/⌥ keystroke on Cocoa — a large file turned that into a multi-hundred-millisecond freeze (found by the security audit). `PluginShortcuts::clamp` likewise dedupes with a `HashSet`, so parsing a maximally-packed file is O(n).

  Per-backend firing:
  * **Win32** — the initial `HACCEL` is built with the cached chords merged in. A not-yet-loaded plugin's chord installs under a **shim id** (`ID_PLUGIN_SHORTCUT_SHIM_BASE..=…_END`, in the free band between the last host id and N++'s `IDM_BASE`); firing it lazy-loads the plugin, resolves the real `cmd_id`, rebuilds the table so the chord binds to the real id thereafter (`refresh_plugin_accels`, which preserves host entries so a prior removal survives), and posts the real `WM_COMMAND`. A chord whose plugin is loaded but whose `internalID` does not resolve (a bogus hand-edited entry, or a separator slot) is **not registered**, so it can't consume a key and do nothing. Menu labels get a sanitized-name `\t<chord>` suffix.
  * **GTK** — a dedicated `AccelGroup`, rebuilt from `startup_plugin_chords` at startup (after `discover()`) and after every load (`rebuild_plugin_accel_group`, the analogue of Win32's `refresh_plugin_accels`), so a newly-absorbed default goes live this session. Each closure fires by *chord*, re-checking the live cache via `match_plugin_chord`, and returns "not handled" (letting the key reach the editor) when the chord was removed or does not resolve. Menu items show the hint via a display-only accel group (the `FILE_HINT_ACCEL` pattern).
  * **macOS** — an always-on `NSEvent` keyDown monitor is the **sole** firing authority; plugin menu items carry **no** `NSMenuItem` key equivalent (their chord is shown in the item title with macOS glyphs). This is deliberate: m4f established AppKit does not call `menuNeedsUpdate:` during key-equivalent search (so a menu key equivalent could never be the pre-load trigger), and a key equivalent would keep firing a chord the plugin later removed until the menu was rebuilt. For a *live* cached chord the monitor asks `mainMenu.performKeyEquivalent:` first — which now matches only host items — so a colliding host command wins (⌘S stays Save); otherwise it lazy-loads and fires, swallowing the event **only when a command actually ran** (so an unresolved chord still reaches the editor). `is_ctrl` maps to ⌘. The candidate gate is `core::shortcuts::is_registrable_chord`, not a "must hold ⌘/⌥" test — that admits bare/Shift function keys (NppExec's classic `F6`), which a modifier gate would have made unfireable pre-load.

  **Host-vs-plugin collisions resolve to the host on every backend** — a plugin claiming ⌘S/Ctrl+S does not steal Save. Win32 merges plugin accels *after* the host defaults in the table (so `TranslateAccelerator` finds the host entry first, preserved across `refresh_plugin_accels`); GTK attaches the plugin accel group *after* the main one (activation is by attach order); macOS defers to `mainMenu.performKeyEquivalent:` before the monitor fires. `core::shortcuts::is_registrable_chord` additionally refuses the Scintilla-native chords (Ctrl+X/C/V/Z/Y/A) outright. This matches Notepad++'s "plugins may request any chord" model — honoured only where nothing higher claims it. (A hand-edited chord for a *non*-Scintilla-reserved editor key, e.g. word-nav, can still shadow that key while Code++ is focused, recoverable by editing the file; the backends decline to *swallow* such a key when it fails to resolve, but do not widen the reserved set to Scintilla's whole default table — a config-file exposure this shares with N++.)

  **The one cross-platform semantic to know:** `is_ctrl` is ⌘ on macOS and literal Ctrl on Win32/GTK, and the physical key is matched by *character/keyval* on GTK/macOS versus *virtual-key* on Win32 — the same physical/layout split every backend's own shortcuts already have. Keys with no faithful cross-platform identity (layout-dependent OEM punctuation) are declined by `core::shortcuts::portable_key` and simply don't register off Windows; on macOS a Shift-produced punctuation glyph (`⌘⇧3` → `#`) is likewise unmapped, a narrow known gap. `docs/nppm-coverage.md` is updated: the plugin-command half of `NPPM_GETSHORTCUTBYCMDID`/`NPPM_REMOVESHORTCUTBYCMDID` now works on all three backends.

  Testing: `core::shortcuts` carries the round-trip, clamp, registrability, and `module_key`/`portable_key` unit tests (including the `.dll`-keeps-`lib` carve-out and the alias hazard it prevents); `codepp-shell` has the chord-pipeline tests (`startup_plugin_chords` filtering + first-wins dedupe, live `match_plugin_chord` after an invalidation, `resolve_plugin_command` requiring a loaded plugin) and `ui_win32` tests `build_plugin_accel_entries`'s shim assignment against a real `Shell`; each backend unit-tests its key-mapping helpers. The end-to-end "a real plugin declares a shortcut, the key fires it" path is a manual demo item (no in-tree plugin declares a `_pShKey`).

- **~~`ui_gtk`'s GTK signal closures carry no `catch_unwind` boundary — including the two that run plugin code.~~ Landed (Phase 5), crate-wide rather than for the plugin pair, as the entry asked.** `ui_gtk` now has its own `at_callback_boundary`, the same shape as `ui_cocoa`'s, and every GLib/GTK-invoked closure in the crate begins with it — 112 sites: every `connect_*` handler, the four timers and idles, the §5.4 wake's `MainContext::invoke` closure, and the cross-thread `SCI_*` marshal hop. `plugin_dispatch` and the plugin-command call were converted from their bare `catch_unwind` so the crate has one mechanism; the command call, previously a silent `let _ =`, now logs the panic it swallows. Fallbacks are chosen per entry rather than uniformly: `Propagation::Proceed` for an event handler so the key or click still reaches the widget, `ControlFlow::Continue` for the auto-save timer so one bad save does not silence it for the session, `Break` for the workspace unfold tick so a panicking walk stops rather than spins. Three `build` functions crossed clippy's 100-line limit from the extra lines and had their signal wiring split into helpers.

  **Pinned by a source scan that matches the construct, not the identifier** — the lesson §7.2 records three times over. The closure literal passed to a `connect_*` / timer / `invoke` call must begin with the boundary call, and a bare function argument (`menu.connect_show(rebuild_recent_region)`), which the boundary cannot wrap, is refused outright. Mutation-verified both ways: stripping one wrapper and passing one bare function each fail it. Writing it exposed that the crate's existing scan helper cut every file at its *first* `#[cfg(test)]`; several test modules sit mid-file with production code after them, so `menu.rs` was half-scanned and the single-view guard was reading less than it claimed. The helper now excises each test module by brace matching, and both guards share it.

  **The pre-commit gate found three things, all closed here.** The guard's first version checked only that a closure *began* with the boundary call, so a statement appended after it — the easy mistake, a stray log line or a cleanup step — would run outside the `catch_unwind` while the site still looked guarded; the reviewer demonstrated it with a decoy call followed by real work. The scan now requires the boundary call to be the closure's *only* statement (its matching `)`, an optional `;`, then the body's own terminator), with the decoy in its sample test and a third real-code mutation killing it. Second, `delete-event` ran the session save and `main_quit` under one boundary, so a panicking save would have closed the window and left the loop running with nothing to quit it from; the save now has its own inner boundary and the quit is unconditional. Third — the security audit's one Medium, and the interesting one — five re-entrancy flags (`PRESENTING`, `menu::REFRESHING_MARKS`, `REFRESHING_EDIT_MENU_MARKS`, both `SYNCING`s) were bare `set(true)…set(false)` pairs. That was harmless while a panic between them aborted the process; catching the panic and continuing would have left the flag stuck for the session, silently disabling dialogs, menu marks or a panel toggle — exactly the failure `tabs::SuppressGuard` and `DrainFreeze` already exist to prevent. They now go through one `FlagGuard` RAII type, and `WINDOW_RESET_SETTLING`, which a timer clears, arms that timer *before* the work it covers. So the boundary made a latent class of bug reachable and the same commit closes it; worth knowing for any future backend that adopts the pattern.

  **Verified live rather than by reading, with a control.** An env-gated `panic!` placed inside the `configure-event` handler and inside the auto-save timer, run against the real app: with the boundary, each is logged as `panic caught at a GTK callback boundary` (three configure events at window show, then the timer at 7 s) and the editor keeps running until killed; with the wrapper removed from the handler, the first configure event aborts the process with `panic in a function that cannot unwind` — the defined-abort behaviour the original entry predicted, now measured rather than cited. Original entry follows.

  Found by the security audit of the merge that brought the plugin-shortcut work (`16f8307`) together with the GTK cross-thread marshal (`ec6ba93`), and deferred with sign-off because it is a crate-wide pre-existing pattern rather than anything either commit introduced. `ui_gtk` has **zero** `catch_unwind` outside `plugin.rs`, across ~117 `connect_*` closures; inside `plugin.rs` the boundaries sit only where control enters *from* plugin C code (`plugin_dispatch`, the marshal's `MainContext::invoke` closure, and the `cmd()` call in `on_plugin_command`). The Plugins-menu `connect_activate` closure and the accel-group closure behind `fire_plugin_chord` — both of which lazy-load plugins and resolve commands through the shell *before* reaching that innermost guard — have none, so a panic in that pre/post bookkeeping (a malformed `shortcuts.xml` edge case, a plugin returning malformed `FuncItem`s during the load) reaches glib's `extern "C"` trampoline. The m3c narrative in §7.2 stated that `ui_gtk`'s signal handlers already had boundaries; that was wrong and is corrected there.

  **Severity is lower than the equivalent Cocoa finding, and the reason is worth recording so it is not overstated next time.** On the pinned Rust 1.95 an unwind out of an `extern "C"` function is not undefined behaviour — it is a defined abort (`panic in a function that cannot unwind`), and the release profile is `panic = "abort"` regardless. So the practical difference a boundary makes here is *swallow-and-continue* versus *abort-with-message* in dev and test builds — a robustness choice, not a memory-safety one. `ui_cocoa`'s `on_sci_notify` was the sharper case because it is the host's own `extern "C"` callback rather than a toolkit trampoline.

  When it is closed, close it for the whole crate rather than for the two plugin closures alone: a single `at_callback_boundary` helper as `ui_cocoa` has, applied at every GTK-invoked entry point, so the two backends express the rule the same way. Wrapping just the plugin pair would leave 115 closures on a different convention and invite the next audit to rediscover this.

- **~~`NPPM_DMMUPDATEDISPINFO` and `DMN_CLOSE` were stubs; the docking-manager messages were all deferred together.~~ Both landed (Phase 5, Win32); `NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` is unblocked but is its own milestone.** The three were tracked in `docs/nppm-coverage.md` rather than here, and they turned out to be three different jobs rather than one.

  **The delivery contract for `DMN_CLOSE` was wrong in `Docking.h`, and that is the finding worth recording.** The header declared DMN_\* as codes "the host sends to the plugin's `beNotified`", with `nmhdr.idFrom` carrying `tTbData.dlgID`. Upstream does neither: `DockingCont::doClose` sends `DMN_CLOSE` as a plain `WM_NOTIFY` **to the plugin's own `h_client` dialog**, with `wParam` 0, `nmhdr.hwndFrom` = the container window and `nmhdr.idFrom` = **0**, and it sends it *before* hiding. A plugin built against the public headers listens in its dialog proc for exactly that shape, so the documented Code++ contract would have produced a notification nobody hears — §6.1's binary-compat promise, missed by a design decision rather than by an omission. Nothing had shipped on the old contract (the notification was never sent at all), so correcting the header breaks no plugin.

  The reasoning that settles it needs no appeal to authority: upstream's own base class compares `nmhdr.hwndFrom` against the *main* window while `doClose` sends the *container*, so any plugin that works on real Notepad++ cannot be relying on that comparison. Matching upstream field-for-field is therefore the compatible choice by construction.

  **On provenance and on how far this is verified**, since a reviewer rightly asked both. The contract was established by reading upstream's public implementation for *interface facts only* — which window receives the message, which Win32 message carries it, the four field values, the ordering — and nothing was copied; that is the same basis on which this project already states message numbers and struct layouts, and §1 of every header in `plugins/nppcompat-headers/` records why those facts are not copyrightable. What is **not** yet done is the other half of Phase 3's "critical second test": no third-party docking-capable plugin (NppExec's console is the obvious one) has been run against this. The in-tree demo proves the host and a plugin agree with each other, which is a weaker claim than agreeing with Notepad++. That check is worth doing before the docking-manager milestone builds on top of it. The send is now one arm of `dock_frame_wnd_proc`, needs no notification machinery at all — `idFrom` being 0 means nothing has to be looked up — and holds no `WindowState` borrow, so a plugin may call `NPPM_*` straight back from its handler.

  *(Corrected in Phase 5, by the measurement this paragraph said was still owed. A probe plugin — `example-hello` with every `WM_NOTIFY` it receives logged — was loaded unchanged into a portable copy of Notepad++ 8.9.6 and into Code++, and the two logs compared field by field. The reading above got the shape right — a `WM_NOTIFY` to `h_client`, `wParam` 0, `idFrom` 0, sent before hiding — and two facts wrong. `hwndFrom` is the **main window**, not the container; the container sends a different family (`DMN_SWITCHIN` and its siblings). So "the reasoning that settles it" above inverted what upstream does, and Code++'s `DMN_CLOSE` came from a window the docking-dialog template ignores. And `DMN_FIRST` is **1050**, not `0x1000`, so the code number itself was one no plugin recognises. Both are fixed; the `DMN_DOCK` / `DMN_FLOAT` entry below has the measurement. The lesson is the one this document keeps recording: reading an implementation is not measuring it, and an in-tree demo agreeing with the host proved only that the two shared a mistake.)*

  **`NPPM_DMMUPDATEDISPINFO` was a stub for a concrete reason: the dispatcher decoded the plugin's wide strings into owned `String`s and dropped the `tTbData` pointer, so by the time the message arrived there was nothing left to re-read.** The registration now retains that pointer (`DockDialogParams::tb_data`, re-walked through the new `read_dock_disp_info`), which is a lifetime contract on the plugin — the struct and its buffers must outlive the registration. That is what upstream requires too, and it is no stronger than the `h_client` contract the host already accepts without being able to verify; `Docking.h` now states it instead of implying the opposite.

  Two things fell out of doing it. The refreshed `pszName` is *also* the `NPPM_DMMGETPLUGINHWNDBYNAME` key, so the value/label split every other plugin-supplied string in this codebase makes applies here: `DockEntry::name` keeps the raw string for matching and `dock_frame_title` sanitizes for the caption. That closed a pre-existing hole — the *registration* path had been writing `psz_name` into `CreateWindowExW`'s caption unsanitized since Phase 4, the same class as the `NPPM_SETSTATUSBAR` and workspace-tree incidents this section already records, and the fourth instance of it. And `dock_frame_wnd_proc` gained the `catch_unwind` boundary every other window proc in the crate has and this one never had — newly load-bearing, since `WM_CLOSE` now hands control to plugin code.

  **`NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` stays 🟡, and the reason changed.** It was blocked on there being no docking manager; there is one now (`core::dock`, on all three backends), so the work is no longer "build the thing" but "move plugin dock dialogs off the standalone floating-frame path onto the shared model", after which the message is "activate the named panel's tab in its group" — something the model already does. What makes it a milestone rather than a patch is that `DockPanel` is a fixed two-variant `Copy` enum, deliberately exhaustive so a new panel is a compile error rather than a panel that builds and never draws; plugin panels are dynamic and arbitrary in number, so it needs a `DockPanel::Plugin(id)` arm with an interned key (a `String` in a `Copy` enum does not work), `ALL` stops being a const array, and each backend's exhaustive host match grows a dynamic arm. The payoff is wider than one row: the `u_mask` `DWS_DF_CONT_*` bits and `i_prev_cont` that are snapshotted and ignored today become live, `DMN_DOCK` / `DMN_FLOAT` become sendable, and a plugin panel survives a restart — for which `DockRememberSession` is already the right shape, keyed on `(module, name)` and applied when `REGASDCKDLG` arrives, which is also the answer to a session restoring a panel whose plugin has not been lazily loaded yet.

  **The security audit found one High, and it was introduced by this change rather than inherited.** `DMN_CLOSE` goes out on `SendMessageW`, which on Win32 is a same-thread call straight into the plugin's window procedure — and the plugin learns its own frame's HWND from `nmhdr.hwndFrom` in the very notification it is handling. Sending `WM_CLOSE` back at that frame re-enters the proc, which sends `DMN_CLOSE` again, with nothing bounding the depth. That is four ABI-legal lines in a plugin, and it ends in a stack overflow — a *hardware exception*, so neither this proc's `catch_unwind` nor the plugin's own catches it and the host dies outright. Before this milestone `WM_CLOSE` never called into plugin code at all, so the hazard did not exist to be guarded.

  Closed with a thread-local latch of the shape `PluginCallGuard` and `DrainFreeze` already establish: a close nested inside any other close skips its notification and just hides. A flag rather than a depth count and per-thread rather than per-frame, both deliberately the coarse direction — a per-frame flag still admits an A→B→A cycle, a depth cap needs an arbitrary number, and what the coarseness costs is that a plugin closing a *second* panel from inside the first one's handler sees that one hide without a notification, which is what every close did before this commit.

  What the latch does **not** bound is how long a single delivery takes: a plugin whose `DMN_CLOSE` handler never returns hangs the UI thread on the click that closed its panel. That is §6.5's already-accepted "a synchronous plugin call can hang the host, and so it can in Notepad++" rather than anything new in kind — but it is a new *instance* of it, at a call site that until now never entered plugin code at all, and is recorded rather than left implicit.

  Verified by making `example-hello` hostile for one run — its `DMN_CLOSE` handler sending `WM_CLOSE` straight back at `nmhdr.hwndFrom` — and driving the same demo. Unguarded, the host is gone by the time the probe looks, exit code `0xC000041D` (`STATUS_FATAL_USER_CALLBACK_EXCEPTION`, the stack-overflow-inside-a-window-callback signature). Guarded, the same plugin gets exactly one `DMN_CLOSE`, the panel hides, the process is alive and a re-show still works. The latch is also pinned by a source scan, since nothing about the code's shape says it is load-bearing.

  Worth recording one instrument failure from that run, because it produced a *false negative* on a fix that worked: restoring the guarded file with `Copy-Item` carried the backup's older timestamp across, cargo judged the source older than its own output, skipped the rebuild, and the "fixed" run crashed identically to the broken one. Touching the file changed the verdict. A build that reports `Finished` without a `Compiling` line is not a build.

  The audit's Medium was a documentation-accuracy one and is fixed as stated: the retained `tTbData` pointer was described as "no stronger than the contract already accepted for `h_client`", and it is weaker. `h_client` is an opaque handle with `IsWindow` as a real OS-backed liveness gate, so a stale one degrades to a decline; there is no such test for a raw pointer, so a plugin that registers a stack temporary crashes the host instead. The ABI leaves no room for a stronger guarantee — upstream retains the same pointer — so the fix is to say so where a plugin author reads it rather than to pretend otherwise.

  **Demoed against the real app, with a control**, because until now nothing in-tree registered a docking dialog — all four bundled plugins are buffer-text transforms — so §7.1 had no re-runnable demo for any of this. `example-hello` grew a real one: a child window it creates itself, a `tTbData` held in a `static` (which is what the lifetime contract demands, and the demo is also the documentation of it), and two menu commands. Driving the real Win32 binary under a scratch `APPDATA`: the frame appears with caption `Example Hello Panel`, "Rename Dock Panel" moves it to `Example Hello Panel (renamed)` and a second click moves it *back* — which is what distinguishes a re-read from a one-shot — closing the frame logs `plugin set status bar … "Example Hello: panel closed (DMN_CLOSE received)"` from inside the plugin's own dialog proc, and a re-show brings back the same single frame, so the registration survived. Against a build with both host changes mutated away, the caption never moves and the `DMN_CLOSE` line never appears. Five unit and source-scan guards cover the caption's fallback chain, its sanitization, and the two `DMN_CLOSE` facts that are ABI rather than taste (the notify-before-hide ordering, and `idFrom = 0` with the send aimed at `h_client`); all four mutations kill the guard that owns them.

  **Two instrument failures, one of which is this section's recurring lesson in a new costume.** The probe's class-name helper was named `Cls` — which is PowerShell's built-in alias for `Clear-Host`, and aliases beat functions in command resolution — so it silently invoked `Clear-Host`, returned nothing, and reported *zero dock frames* while the feature was working correctly. It looked exactly like the feature being broken. And two attempts to mutate the `WM_CLOSE` ordering landed in the wrong function, because both `GetWindowLongPtrW(hwnd, GWLP_USERDATA)` and `WM_CLOSE =>` occur several times in `ui_win32/src/lib.rs`; each produced a passing test that proved nothing. A mutation that silently edits the wrong code is indistinguishable from a guard that works.

  **Found while demoing, and fixed in the commit after this one: `ui_win32` never called `stage_bundled_plugins`.** `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` both did, and the function's own doc says it exists "so a freshly-built (or shipped) Code++ finds them with no manual step" — but on Windows, the primary platform, a fresh profile discovered **zero** plugins until someone laid out `<config_dir>/plugins/<name>/<name>.dll` by hand. An omission by history rather than a decision: staging was born in the GTK plugin-host milestone, where Linux had no manual install path at all, and was ported to Cocoa in macOS m5; this backend's plugin host predates the mechanism and never adopted it. Every Win32 plugin demo this document records was run against a hand-staged directory, which is exactly why it went unnoticed for two phases.

  Driving a completely fresh `%APPDATA%`: before, the Plugins menu opens to nothing but its two static entries and the log reads `count=0`; after, four plugins are staged and discovered and the menu carries all four plugins' command sets. The ordering — staging before discovery — is invisible at the call site and compiles either way, so it is pinned by a mutation-verified source scan; reversed, a first launch would stage the DLLs and then find none, leaving an empty menu until the *second* launch, which reads as a slow install rather than a bug.

  **The security audit turned up two Mediums in `plugin_stage.rs` — pre-existing, shared with the other two backends, and fixed here because this change is what makes them reachable on Windows.** Both are the same bug class the project had already found and fixed elsewhere, which is what makes them worth recording: the function's own comment claimed to match "the write-to-temp-then-rename discipline used for session/FIF writes elsewhere" while implementing the *pre-fix* version of it.

  The copy went to `<dest>.staging.<pid>` — fully predictable, since the four plugin names are public constants and a pid is visible to any same-user process — and `std::fs::copy` follows a link at the path it opens. Anyone able to write to the plugin directory could pre-place that name and have the copy write through it. The temp is now `tempfile`-named and `O_EXCL`-created, exactly as `codepp_shell::fif::atomic_write` and `core::session` already do. On Windows the sharp version of that attack is a **hard link**, not a symlink, because `mklink /H` needs no privilege at all — so the regression test uses one and runs on every runner rather than being `#[ignore]`d behind developer mode.

  And `create_dir_all` succeeds silently when the path already resolves *through* a planted symlink or NTFS junction. That one needs no race at all: the per-plugin directory is created exactly once, on a profile's first run, so an attacker plants and waits — and a junction likewise needs no privilege. `stage_one` now refuses a parent that is a link or any other reparse point, failing closed on a stat error too. The check is deliberately on the *per-plugin leaf* directory rather than the config root, so a user who has legitimately relocated their whole configuration with a link is unaffected.

  Both are bounded — the bytes written are always the legitimate bundled plugin, and the attacker needs same-user write access to a directory where they could already do worse — which is why they are Mediums rather than worse. They are fixed anyway because the fix costs nothing and the codebase already holds itself to it. Both mutation-verified: reverting the temp to the predictable name fails the planted-link test, and deleting the directory check fails the junction test, while the straight-line staging test keeps the guards from passing by refusing everything.

  **§8's lazy-load constraint was re-checked rather than assumed**, because this is the first time a default Windows profile has any plugins in it at all, so "zero plugins loaded until first user interaction" only becomes observable here now. Launched on a fresh profile and left alone with the Plugins menu never opened: four staged, four discovered, and **zero of the four DLLs mapped into the process** — read off the live process's own module list, not inferred from the log.

  **§8 re-measured, and the first-run case is worth stating plainly.** Release build, one empty buffer, `session.xml` cleared, four runs each. Baseline (no staging, nothing to discover) **61.7 / 65.6 / 65.9 / 70.0 ms**. Steady state, destinations already current, **69.0 / 69.5 / 73.7 / 74.5 ms** — and the pre-change binary with the same four plugins already in place measures **64.2 / 65.1 / 69.2 / 71.5 ms**, so staging's own steady-state cost is the few milliseconds of four `stat` pairs and a `symlink_metadata` each, and the rest is discovery having something to find. All inside the 80 ms budget.

  The **first** launch after an install or a rebuild copies four DLLs and measures **74.4 / 74.8 / 80.8 / 84.8 ms**, i.e. over budget on two runs of four. That is the same position the other two backends already take — GTK's call site says in as many words that the copy "does not weigh on the warm-cache cold-start budget" — and it is consistent with how this section already treats a cold file cache, discarding a 140.8 ms Win32 first run as outside §8's warm-cache scope. Recorded rather than buried: the overage is real, it is once per install, and the lever if it ever matters is to move the copy off the startup path, which is not free because discovery feeds the accelerator table and a plugin hotkey must work before any menu is opened.

  **A side note on the baseline itself.** These numbers are 62–70 ms against the ~94 ms §8's Win32 row records, measured under the condition §8 states and this document had to learn the hard way on macOS — `session.xml` deleted first, so it really is one empty buffer. Whether the older figure was taken on other hardware or with a session restored is not knowable from here, so the row is left alone rather than rewritten; but anyone repeating it should clear the session first, and on this machine Win32 cold start is inside its budget.

- **~~Six Notepad++ message numbers were wrong, and `NPPM_GETNPPVERSION` reported a version no real plugin accepts.~~ Both landed (Phase 5).** Found by trying to load the real, unmodified NppExec, which refused with *"This version of NppExec requires Notepad++ ver. 5.1 or higher"*.

  **The version constant was wrong for a reason worth recording, because the reasoning was written down and was internally consistent.** It reported `0.1`, deliberately below every real Notepad++ release, so that a plugin's *feature* gate — `if (NPPM_GETNPPVERSION() >= 0x00080000)` — would fail closed on features Code++ does not implement. That is a real shape and the logic holds for it. It misses the commoner one: a **minimum-version floor**, "I require N++ ≥ X or I refuse to run", against which a version below every release fails *every* check rather than none. §6.3 had always said the value should be "range-compatible with Notepad++ for plugin gating", and `0.1` is not range-compatible — it is below the whole range. It now reports **8.4.1**, the newest release whose plugin-facing API Code++ actually implements (the one that introduced `NPPM_ISDARKMODEENABLED` and `NPPM_GETDARKMODECOLORS`), and honours the `ADD_ZERO_PADDING` `wParam` it had been ignoring — a padded compare is what orders versions correctly across differing digit counts, so answering one form for both is wrong half the time.

  **Verifying that turned up something larger: six message numbers disagreed with upstream, and three of them collided with real messages.** `NPPM_ISDARKMODEENABLED` sat at `NPPMSG + 110`, which is upstream's `NPPM_CREATELEXER`; `NPPM_GETDARKMODECOLORS` at `+111`, which is upstream's `NPPM_GETBOOKMARKID`; and our `NPPM_GETBOOKMARKID` at `+101`, which is upstream's `NPPM_ADDTOOLBARICON_FORDARKMODE`. `NPPM_GETNPPDIRECTORY` and `NPPM_GETNPPFULLFILEPATH` were at `RUNCOMMAND_USER + 23` and `+ 42` against the real `+ 7` and `+ 10` — invented offsets, so those two had never been reachable by any plugin compiled against the public header. And `NPPM_GETZOOMLEVEL` is not a Notepad++ message at all: Code++ invented it and put it at `NPPMSG + 102`, which is upstream's `NPPM_DOCLISTDISABLEPATHCOLUMN`, so a plugin asking Notepad++ to drop the document list's path column would have been answered with a zoom level. It moves to `CODEPPM_GETZOOMLEVEL` in the extension band, which exists precisely so an invention cannot alias anything upstream may add.

  The collisions are the sharp part, because a wrong number is not a message that goes unanswered — it is a *different* message answered confidently. `NPPM_GETBOOKMARKID(0, 0)` reaching a handler that expects `wParam = cbSize` and `lParam = NppDarkMode::Colors*` is the shape to worry about; it is null-guarded today, but that is luck rather than design.

  **The mechanism is the actual fix, and its absence is the lesson.** Both declaration sites — the Rust dispatcher and the C header plugin authors include — are hand-transcribed, and the in-repo lock test (`nppmsg_constants_match_header`) pins the Rust constants against *the same hand-written literals*, so it agreed with itself and caught nothing for two phases. New `tools/npp-abi-check/check.py` fetches Notepad++'s published header and diffs both sites against it, resolving upstream's symbolic offsets (`RUNCOMMAND_USER + NPP_DIRECTORY`) and separating genuine mismatches from the eleven messages upstream has renamed while keeping the number, where Code++'s older spelling is still correct. It vendors nothing: the tree carries no upstream source, and what the tool prints is a name and an integer — the same not-copyrightable ABI surface the clean-room headers already rest on. `docs/nppm-coverage.md` and DEVELOPMENT.md now make running it a step when a constant is added or changed, and the corrected numbers are additionally pinned in the lock test so a *regression* fails without needing the network.

  **This did not fix NppExec on its own, and the reason is a separate bug** — see the next entry, which landed straight after and does. Recorded plainly because a fix that addresses the reported error message without addressing the reported failure is exactly the kind of thing that gets called done.

- **~~A plugin cannot query the host from `setInfo`, so every real plugin that checks the host version at init is locked out.~~ Landed (Phase 5), on all three backends. This was what actually blocked NppExec.** §7.4 already recorded the window — "the lazy load remains the one window where a plugin cannot re-enter … plugins that query the host from READY (version, config directory) still read 0 there" — but recorded it as a limitation affecting `NPPN_READY`. It is worse than that: it affects `setInfo`, which is the first thing a plugin runs, and the first thing NppExec does there is ask the host its version.

  Measured rather than inferred, by logging every message that reaches the Win32 `NPPM_*` arm along with whether the state was reachable. NppExec's very first message is `NPPMSG + 50` (`NPPM_GETNPPVERSION`), inside the `plugin_load` span, with `reachable = false` — and so are the eleven that follow it during load, including `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` (so its Console panel never registers as a dock either) and `NPPM_SETSTATUSBAR`. It reads 0 for the version, concludes it is older than 5.1, and refuses. Correcting `NPPM_GETNPPVERSION` to report 8.4.1 changes nothing for it, which was confirmed by re-running against the real DLL: the same dialog appears.

  The cause is structural rather than a missing arm. Each backend's load call site holds `&mut WindowState` (or the `with_state` equivalent) across `PluginHost::load`, and arms `PluginCallGuard` so that a re-entrant `state_from_hwnd` is *declined* rather than aliasing that borrow. Declining is the correct behaviour given the borrow; the bug is holding the borrow at all while foreign code runs.

  **The fix is the one §7.4 already prescribes for notifications, applied one step earlier**, and the pieces are in place: `load_inner` is already a pure-FFI function that touches no host state. Split `PluginHost::load` the way `Shell::begin_close_active_tab` / `close_announced_tab` splits a close — take the path and cmd-id base under the borrow, run `LoadLibraryW` / `setInfo` / `getFuncsArray` with **no borrow held**, then commit the result and deliver `NPPN_READY` / `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` under a fresh one. `Shell::ensure_plugins_loaded` needs the same split for the same reason, and all three backends' call sites change. `PluginCallGuard` then stays only where a plugin genuinely runs inside a borrow that cannot be dropped, which after this is the `WM_DESTROY` shutdown pair and the dark-mode re-broadcast.

  **What shipped is that split.** `PluginHost::load` is gone, replaced by `next_pending_load` → `execute_load` → `commit_load`, with `Shell` exposing the same three as `next_plugin_to_load` / `commit_plugin_load` / `after_plugin_loads`. The loop lives in each backend, because a backend is the only thing that can drop its own state borrow; each phase takes a borrow that ends with its own statement, and the plugin's entry points run between them with none held. `PluginReady::deliver` carries `NPPN_READY` / `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` out to be fired the same way *(since changed: they now go out as one `LoadNotifications` batch per load pass, after every plugin in it has loaded and its menu exists, in Notepad++'s measured order — see the lifecycle entry further down)*, closing the half of this §7.4 already described.

  **The security audit caught the half of that I had only done on two backends.** `PluginCallGuard` was doing two jobs on Win32: declining the plugin's re-entrant `NPPM_*` — the bug — and, as a side effect, making `state_from_hwnd` answer `None` inside the `WM_APP_WAKE` arm, which deferred any worker drain for the span of the load. Dropping the guard drops both, and I had replaced the second only on GTK and Cocoa. A plugin's `setInfo` may pump the message queue — a `MessageBoxW`, an init dialog, a nested loop, and NppExec creates windows at init — so a wake landing there would have drained the shell *inside the loading plugin's own call stack*. Win32 takes the same `DrainFreeze` now, pinned by its own scan. The audit's other finding is closed too: `PendingLoad` must be public to cross the borrow gap, so a caller could hand-build one whose `idx` and `path` disagree and file one library under another plugin's entry; `commit_load` now rejects that for the cost of a string compare.

  Two things the split creates rather than inherits. A nested pass is now *reachable* — the load runs unborrowed, so a plugin's `setInfo` can send a `WM_COMMAND` that walks back into the loader — and without a latch the nested pass would hand out a second `PendingLoad` carrying the **same** `cmd_id_base`, because the base only advances at commit; two plugins would then claim one command-id range and a menu click would fire the wrong command. `PluginHost` holds that latch, so all three backends inherit it. And on GTK and Cocoa the borrow was what made a `DrainFreeze` unnecessary — a wake landing mid-load found the state borrowed and deferred itself. The Cocoa call site's own comment predicted this in as many words ("if this is ever split so the borrow is dropped between steps, it needs the explicit guard"); both now take one.

  `PluginHost::load_blocking` remains for callers with no UI state to alias, which in practice is the test harnesses. Its doc says plainly that it holds the borrow across the plugin's entry points and must never be used from a backend, and a source scan pins that `ui_win32` does not.

  **Verified against the real, unmodified NppExec**, which is what started this: it now loads with no dialog and takes its place in the Plugins menu, where before the fix it put up its initialization error every time. Three source-scan guards pin the parts a diff cannot show — that the loop arms no `PluginCallGuard`, that the foreign call sits between the two borrows and that neither is *bound* (the binding form compiles, reads naturally, and is exactly the bug), and that both lazy-load triggers go through the one loop and `load_blocking` is not among them. All mutation-verified, and the second needed two attempts: the first version counted borrows and passed against a collapsed single-borrow loop, which is the precise shape of the original defect.

  The other two backends are changed identically but verified by build and test only — there is no GTK or Cocoa runner on this host — which is the same footing as the Phase 5 m2b rebind.

- **~~Loading a real plugin's docked panel showed two window frames, and quitting with one loaded crashed the host.~~ Both landed (Phase 5).** Reported from hands-on testing once NppExec could load at all, and they are unrelated to each other beyond both being things only a real third-party plugin surfaces.

  **The double frame is what `SetParent` does not do.** `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` re-parented the plugin's dialog into a host-owned frame and left its *styles* alone — and a plugin creates that dialog as the top-level window it is until a host adopts it, so it arrives carrying `WS_POPUP | WS_CAPTION | WS_SYSMENU | WS_THICKFRAME`. The user sees a window inside a window with two close buttons, the inner one drawn in the plain non-client style because it is no longer top-level. The fix is what a docking host is for: `WS_CHILD` on, every frame bit off, including the extended ones (`WS_EX_DLGMODALFRAME` and the edge styles each draw a border *inside* the frame the host already painted), then `SetWindowPos` with `SWP_FRAMECHANGED`, without which the non-client area keeps its cached size and the caption stays on screen. Measured on the live window: NppExec's console went from `0x94CC004C` (`WS_POPUP|WS_CAPTION|WS_SYSMENU|WS_THICKFRAME|WS_VISIBLE`) to `WS_CHILD|WS_VISIBLE` with the extended edge bits cleared. The transform is a pure function with unit tests — including that it does **not** strip `WS_VISIBLE`, which is the other way to get this wrong and leaves the panel blank — and the ordering is a mutation-verified source scan, because restyling before the reparent is undone by it and both orders read fine.

  **The crash was `FreeLibrary`.** Quitting with NppExec loaded exited `0xC0000005` (`STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION`) every time; the four in-tree plugins never triggered it, and neither did NppExec if the Plugins menu was never opened — i.e. it needed a real plugin actually loaded. Established by elimination rather than by reading: skipping the `FreeLibrary` in `DynLib`'s drop, changing nothing else, made it exit `0`. `PluginHost` now deliberately leaks its libraries — see §6.4, which this is the one deviation from.

  Worth recording that our own dock frames make one instance of that hazard *certain* rather than merely likely: they are owned by the main window, so Win32 destroys them after the main window's `WM_DESTROY` returns, which is after the host has been dropped — and destroying a frame destroys the plugin's client window with it, calling a `WNDPROC` in a library that is no longer mapped. But NppExec crashes without registering a dock at all, so fixing the ordering would have fixed one case and left the class open. That is the argument for not unloading rather than for unloading more carefully.

  **Still not fixed, and it is the milestone rather than a bug:** a plugin's panel does not integrate with Code++'s own dock panels. Dragging it to an edge offers Windows' snap layouts, because the host frame is a real top-level `WS_OVERLAPPEDWINDOW` — floating-only mode, exactly as `docs/nppm-coverage.md` describes. Making a plugin panel a peer of the workspace tree and the document map is the `NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` entry above: `core::dock` has the group/tab model now, and what it needs is a dynamic `DockPanel::Plugin(id)` arm.

- **~~`DMN_DOCK` and `DMN_FLOAT` are never sent, and a real plugin's saved panel state may depend on them.~~ Landed (Phase 5, Win32), together with the correction of every `DMN_*` number — and the hypothesis that brought them forward is refuted.** Both go out with upstream's shape, measured rather than read: a plain `WM_NOTIFY` to the plugin's `h_client`, `wParam` 0, `idFrom` 0, `hwndFrom` the **main window**, and `code` = `MAKELONG(DMN_DOCK or DMN_FLOAT, container)`. `hwndFrom` is the field that matters: Notepad++'s docking-dialog template — the base most panel-owning plugins are built on — drops any `WM_NOTIFY` whose `hwndFrom` is not the main window it was initialised with.

  **What "container" means was the design question, and the answer is the side.** Upstream has exactly one container per side plus one per floating window, and numbers them `CONT_LEFT`…`CONT_BOTTOM` then 4 and up. Code++'s model is finer — a side can hold several stacked groups — so `core::dock::DockContainer` maps a docked panel to its *side* and a floating one to its group, and `DockLayout::container_of` answers for a hidden panel too, with the container the next `show` would put it in. That last part is what makes the registration-time notification possible (upstream sends one from `createDockableDlg`, before anything is on screen), and it is pinned against `show` itself by a test that walks every branch of `show`'s precedence, since a disagreement would tell a plugin one container and land it in another. The numbering on the way out is pinned against `DWS_DF_CONT_*` decoding on the way in, which is the same numbering.

  The sameness rule has one non-obvious case. A hidden panel whose remembered spot is floating has no group — it gets a fresh one, with a fresh id, on `show` — so it is `Floating(None)`, which matches any floating container. Without that, a hide-then-show of a floating panel would read as a move, where upstream hides a panel inside its container and says nothing.

  **Where it is sent from.** The last phase of `apply_dock_layout`, which every dock change already funnels through, and which already runs with no state borrow held. Each `DockEntry` carries the container its plugin was last told; the reconcile diffs against it, **records the new value before sending**, then sends with no borrow. Recording first stops a transition being told twice: a handler that shows or hides a panel reconciles again from inside the delivery, and that nested pass finds the transition already recorded, because no `NPPM_*` moves an *existing* panel between containers. A source scan pins that order.

  **Recording first was presented as the whole bound, and the security audit showed it is not — the same recursion class as `DMN_CLOSE`'s High, in a heavier form.** A handler that *registers a new panel* hands the nested pass an entry nothing has been told about, so the nested pass sends to it — synchronously, from inside the first handler — and that panel's handler can register another. The chain stops only at the 64-panel registration cap, each link is a full trip through the main window procedure plus whatever stack the plugin chooses to spend, and a stack overflow is a hardware exception nothing in the host catches. The first draft's comment and this entry both said "depth one, no latch needed" in the same breath as describing the registration-time notice, which is precisely the case that breaks it.

  The fix is a queue, not a latch. `DmnCloseGuard` *skips* a nested `DMN_CLOSE`; doing that here would lose notices outright, because the record is already written when a notice is raised, so a panel registered from inside a handler would never learn its container at all. Instead a notice raised while a delivery is running on the thread is appended to a thread-local FIFO and the call returns; the outermost delivery drains it. The window-procedure nesting this path can cause is one level whatever the plugin does, and nothing is dropped. The cost is timing in exactly that nested case — such a panel is told its container after the handler it was registered from returns, rather than before its own `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` returns — and `Docking.h` says so to plugin authors. FIFO order also closes the audit's informational note on staleness: a panel whose older notice is still queued when a nested reconcile records a newer container receives both, in record order, and ends on the latest. Pinned by a test that drives real `SendMessageW` into a real window whose handler raises a new notice every time it receives one — the registration chain in miniature — and asserts all twelve arrive, in order, at a nesting depth of one; with the queue removed the same test sees a depth of twelve.

  **Verified** by unit tests over the policy (registration, float, dock, side change; and no notice for a hide, a show, a re-activation or a same-side restack) and by driving the real app with the real NppExec: its console's registration now produces `DMN_DOCK` with container 3, the bottom, which is where `DWS_DF_CONT_BOTTOM` asked for. `example-hello` reports both codes on the status bar, and accepts them only from the main window the way the template does, so its demo tests the field rather than only the code: run 1 docks its panel and logs `panel docked (DMN_DOCK, container 3)`; with `session.xml` then hand-edited to float that group, run 2 logs `panel floating (DMN_FLOAT, container 4)` at registration. Against a build that sends a null `hwndFrom` instead, the same notifications go out and the plugin is silent — which is what a template-built plugin would have been.

  **Measured against the real Notepad++, and the measurement found the whole `DMN_*` family misnumbered.** The code review asked for evidence behind the `hwndFrom` claim beyond `example-hello` mirroring it. The way to get it without touching the user's installation was a **probe**: `example-hello` renamed and built outside the repo, logging every `WM_NOTIFY`, command and `NPPN_*` it receives, loaded unchanged into a portable copy of Notepad++ 8.9.6 (`doLocalConf.xml`, `-multiInst`, no updater, no other plugins) and into Code++. Notepad++ sent its registration notice as `code = 0x0003041c`: `hwndFrom`, `idFrom`, `wParam` and the container half all as designed — and the low word, `0x41c`, is **1052**, where Code++'s `DMN_DOCK` was `0x1002`. Upstream's published `dockingResource.h` confirms `DMN_FIRST` is 1050. So every `DMN_CLOSE` Code++ had ever sent, and every notice from this change until then, carried a number no plugin built against the upstream headers recognises. `example-hello` "worked" only because it compiles against the same wrong SDK constant — the in-tree demo agreeing with the host rather than with Notepad++.

  The same run measured the rest. Floating sends `0x0004041d` (`DMN_FLOAT`, container 4) from the main window. Closing sends `DMN_CLOSE` (1051) from the **main window** too, which corrects the earlier `DMN_CLOSE` entry: it had the container as the sender, from a reading of upstream's source. The container itself sends 1054–1056 (`DMN_SWITCHIN`, `DMN_SWITCHOFF`, `DMN_FLOATDROPPED`) as tabs switch; Code++ declares those and sends none of them yet. After the fix, the same probe DLL under Code++ logs `0x0003041c` at registration and `0x0004041d` for a restored floating panel — the values Notepad++ sent. `DMN_CLOSE` could not be driven in Code++ without moving the user's cursor, because the caption ✕ resolves a click from the live cursor position; its sender is pinned by a source guard instead.

  One upstream behaviour is deliberately not copied. Docking a floating panel back by double-clicking its caption, Notepad++ sends `DMN_FLOAT` again, with the docked side's number (`0x0003041d`). A plugin cannot usefully act on being told it floats when it is docked, and the template sets its docked side from `DMN_DOCK`, so Code++ sends `DMN_DOCK` whenever the panel ends up docked. `Docking.h` says so.

  **Why nothing caught it, and what does now.** The ABI checker diffed `Notepad_plus_msgs.h` only. `DMN_*` lives in `dockingResource.h` and `DWS_*`/`CONT_*` in `Docking.h`, so the family was never compared. It is now: a second pass evaluates every `DMN_*`, `DWS_*`, `CONT_*` and `DOCKCONT_*` definition in `Docking.h`, `ffi.rs` and the SDK to an integer — through an AST whitelist rather than `eval`, since half its input comes off the internet — and diffs them against both upstream headers. Run against the tree as it was before this change, it reports five mismatches in each of `Docking.h` and `ffi.rs` and one in the SDK: the four `DMN_*` numbers, plus **`DWS_USEOWNDARKMODE`**, which was `0x01000000` against upstream's `0x00000008`. Nothing acts on that flag yet, so no plugin saw a difference, but a future dark-mode implementation would have read the wrong bit. The same reading corrected two field contracts in `Docking.h`. `pszModuleName` is the plugin's DLL file name **including `.dll`** — upstream's header is explicit that Notepad++ persists it and finds the plugin again by it at startup — where ours said "without extension". And `dlgID` is the index of the `FuncItem` that opens the panel, where ours called it a dialog id carried in `DMN_*`. Both matter to the next entry.

  **It did not change what NppExec saves, and measuring why found the actual mechanism.** Two scenarios under Code++ show the flag never moves at runtime in either direction: shown from the menu with its check mark set, NppExec writes `Visible=0`; seeded `1`, then hidden from the menu, it writes `1`. Watching the file through quit shows exactly one write each time. So the question became what Notepad++ does instead, and the real one was run — `-multiInst` and a scratch `-settingsDir`, so the user's own instance and configuration were untouched — against the same NppExec build (0.8.12.1):

  * On the user's own Notepad++, where the console does come back every launch, NppExec's ini says **`Visible=0`**.
  * In the scratch profile, NppExec's ini **never exists at all** (its config directory was absent, so its write failed silently), and the console still comes back, with its menu check set.
  * What Notepad++ writes is its own record: `<PluginDlg pluginName="NppExec.dll" id="5" curr="3" prev="-1" isVisible="yes" />` in `config.xml`. `id` is `tTbData.dlgID`, which upstream's header defines as the index of the plugin's `FuncItem` that starts the dialog — and NppExec's menu, dumped in order, has "Show NppExec Console" at index 5.

  So Notepad++ restores a plugin panel by **re-running the plugin's own command** at startup: for each panel recorded as visible, it calls `FuncItem[dlgID]` of that module, and the plugin opens its panel exactly as if the user had clicked the menu. The plugin's own flag plays no part, which is why it can round-trip indefinitely without anyone noticing. With `-nosession` the relaunch does not restore, so upstream ties this to session loading. The next entry is the Code++ half of it.

  **How firm that is.** The command run is strongly indicated — NppExec's console comes back with no ini at all, its menu has "Show" at exactly the recorded index, and upstream's header says Notepad++ uses `pszModuleName` to re-open the panel at the next start — but it has not been *observed*. The DMN probe's relaunch adds one fact and leaves that one open. Its two panels came back purely through registration: the probe registers them itself at `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, and Notepad++ applied the saved "bottom, visible" state at that moment and ran no command, so an eager registrant needs no command run at all. What the probe could not show is the run itself, because its `pszModuleName` lacks the `.dll` Notepad++ matches against. `example-hello`'s lacks it too, and its `dlgID`s break the other half of the convention: its first panel declares `dlg_id` 0, which is "Insert Hello", so a Notepad++ that did run its command would type into the user's buffer at startup.

  **Settled since, and one conclusion here reversed.** A probe carrying the `.dll` name and registering its panel only from its own command shows the run directly: Notepad++ calls `FuncItem[dlgID]` after `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` and before `NPPN_BUFFERACTIVATED` and `NPPN_READY`. The same probe registering eagerly at `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` gets its command run as well — so the relaunch above ran no command because its module name did not match, not because the panel was already registered, and "an eager registrant needs no command run" is wrong. The next entry was written from the earlier reading and recommended the opposite of what Notepad++ does; it records the correction.

- **~~A plugin panel whose plugin only registers on demand does not come back across a restart, because Code++ never runs the command that opens it.~~ Landed (Phase 5, Win32): the host restores a plugin panel the way Notepad++ does, by running the plugin's own command for it.** The mechanism is in the entry above: Notepad++ persists `(module, dlgID, container, visible)` per panel and, at the next start, runs `FuncItem[dlgID]` for each panel that was visible. Code++ now does the same, in the same slot.

  **Where it runs, measured.** The restore probe — one panel, registered only from its "Show Dock Panel" command, logging every notification and every command with whether `NPPN_READY` has arrived yet — was driven through the same two runs against a portable Notepad++ 8.9.6 and against Code++: open the panel, quit, relaunch and touch nothing. Both hosts log the identical sequence on the relaunch: `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, then the command (`NPPN_READY` not yet seen), then `NPPN_BUFFERACTIVATED`, then `NPPN_READY`. With the probe registering its panel itself at `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, both still run the command, in the same place.

  **What that reverses in this entry's old plan.** It said the command should run only for a restored panel still without content, so that a *toggle* would not hide a panel the host had already shown. Notepad++ does not do that, and a host that differs in exactly this sequence is the one plugins have not been tested against: a plugin written for Notepad++ has always had its command run for a panel it may have registered a moment earlier, and copes — typically by keeping its own "is my panel open" flag, which starts false, so the first run of a toggle shows. Running it unconditionally is also what leaves the plugin agreeing with what is on screen: the command is where a plugin sets its own state for the panel — that flag, its menu check — so a panel restored without it is one the plugin does not know is open.

  **What ships.** `core::dock` records each plugin panel's command index (`DockLayout::set_open_command`, re-seeded at every registration so the plugin's current `dlgID` wins over a stale one) and persists it as `cmd="…"` on the `<panel>` element, where a hand-edited negative or non-plugin value is dropped on read. `LoadNotifications::deliver` grew a slot between `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` and `NPPN_BUFFERACTIVATED`, with the backend supplying what goes in it — a panic there is contained, because it is host code and must not cost every plugin its `NPPN_READY`. On Win32, `load_plugins_where` notes the open plugin panels its plugins own, and every group's front tab, under the bookkeeping borrow before any plugin is notified; in the slot, `restore_plugin_panels` resolves each panel's command through `Shell::panel_open_command_id` and sends it as the `WM_COMMAND` a click on that menu item sends, with no borrow held, then puts each group's front tab back — each show brings its panel forward, and Notepad++ re-applies the saved front tabs after its restore too. After `NPPN_READY`, a restored panel whose plugin never supplied a window is closed rather than left as an empty band; the layout remembers where it was. One command opening several panels is run once, since a second run of a toggle would undo the first.

  GTK and Cocoa pass an empty restore: they host no plugin panel, and drop any a Windows-written session names at restore. *(GTK has since hosted plugin panels and restores them by the same command run — see the GTK plugin-panel entry below. Cocoa is as described.)*

  `example-hello` now follows upstream's contract, which the restore depends on: `pszModuleName` is `example_hello.dll`, and its two `dlgID`s name its two show commands (1 and 3, named constants next to the array they index, with a test that each names the command it must) — its first panel used to declare 0, which is "Insert Hello", and would now have typed into the user's buffer at every start. Its second panel is deliberately registered only from its own command, so the demo covers both kinds of plugin.

  **Verified by driving the real app.** `example-hello`: open both panels, put the first in front, quit — `session.xml` carries `cmd="1"` and `cmd="3"` — then relaunch and touch nothing: both panels are back, the lazily-registered second one included, the first is in front, and only `example_hello.dll` is mapped into the process, so §8's lazy load still holds for the rest. The real, unmodified NppExec: show the console, quit, relaunch — the console is back and "Show NppExec Console" is checked, with `cmd="5"` persisted, which is the index Notepad++ records for it. Headless coverage: the model's round trip, filtering and front-tab tests on every platform; the delivery slot's position, its panic containment and its absence on an empty pass; the shell mapping against the real `example_hello.dll`, staged under a name of its own because discovery reads the real profile's `disabled.txt`; and three source guards on the Win32 ordering a diff cannot show. Seventeen mutations, each killed — one needed a second test case, because "impose the saved front tab wherever the panel went" passed until a test moved a panel into another group, and one first named a message constant the crate does not import, so it failed to compile and proved nothing until redone.

  **The pre-commit gate found two things worth more than the change that surfaced them, and both are fixed here.**

  *A plugin's name could identify two plugins.* Discovery accepts both `plugins/X.dll` and `plugins/X/X.dll`, so two copies of one plugin — or two unrelated plugins sharing a file name — can be installed side by side, and the development machine this was built on has exactly that for all four in-tree plugins: old flat copies beside the staged ones. The restore resolved a panel by name to the first loaded match, so it could run one copy's command for a panel the other had registered. `Shell::panel_owner` now resolves a panel to the single installed plugin its name identifies, the restore refuses a name several answer to — with a warning naming the collision — and the command is looked up in that one plugin's own `FuncItem`s rather than through the first-match `resolve_plugin_command`. Verified with two real copies of `example_hello.dll`, both loaded: in a unit test, and in the running app with a flat copy placed beside the staged one before a relaunch, where nothing restores, no command runs, the log names both panels with `owners=2`, and the layout record survives the session so the panels return once the duplicate goes. The loading itself is untouched — both copies still load — which is pre-existing and has its own entry below. *(Since closed at the source: discovery loads only Notepad++'s layout and refuses a second plugin answering to a registered name, so a name identifies at most one plugin and the refusal described here is gone with the ambiguity it guarded against — see that entry.)*

  *A restored panel's name reached the chrome unsanitized.* Pre-existing since plugin panels became dock panels: registration sanitizes a plugin's panel name before interning it, but a restore interned the key read from `session.xml` raw, and that name is what every caption and tab draws — so a hand-edited file could put a bidi override in the chrome, the class this document records closing four times before, through a route with no way to be cleared short of editing the file again. The display classifier moved from `shell` down to `core::display`, and `intern_plugin_panel` refuses any text it rejects: one check at the one place identities are made, covering both routes, and nothing legitimate is lost because registration's substitute character passes. The shell's substitution helpers wrap the same function, so the list is still single-sourced.

  Also closed: persisted text may create at most half the interning table's identities (`MAX_RESTORED_PLUGIN_PANELS`), charged to one process-wide budget under the table's own lock, with open panels admitted before remembered ones — so a crafted session can no longer fill the table and lock every real plugin's registration out for the life of the process. Pre-existing, and the audit's one Low that had a fix. The first version bounded each *read* instead, and was sound only because every read at startup parses the same session; both the delta review and the delta audit pointed out that nothing enforced that, so the bound is now on the process and a test reads a second, different session against a spent budget. GTK and Cocoa still intern a Windows-written session's plugin panels before dropping them, since neither can host one; that is now bounded by the same cap and left as it is. *(GTK hosts them since, and keeps them; only Cocoa still drops them.)* And `DockLayout::panels_to_restore`, which the code review found had no production caller while the Win32 path re-derived the same rule beside it, is gone: `open_plugin_panels` replaces it and is what the Win32 capture calls, so the rule lives once. One review suggestion was checked and does not hold. It described an eagerly registered panel whose persisted command no longer resolves as left hidden in its group; but the reconciler shows every registered panel that is its group's active tab whether or not `NPPM_DMMSHOW` arrives, and a registration re-records the command before the restore reads it, so the persisted index is never the one used for such a panel. Eight more mutations across the fixes, each killed.

  **One thing a crafted `session.xml` can now do, stated so it is not discovered.** It can name, for any installed plugin, which of its menu commands the host runs at startup. That is the same lever Notepad++'s `config.xml` hands anyone who can write it, and it is bounded the same way the eager load above it is: only plugins already discovered on disk, only commands they publish in their own menu — nothing a click could not do — and only as many panels as the restore bound admits. Anyone able to write that file can write the plugins directory, which is strictly more capability — though the audit's refinement is worth keeping: a small text file the application rewrites routinely is a quieter thing to change than a new DLL, so this is a lever for someone who could already do more, not no lever at all. A plugin whose menu has a command that acts without asking (NppExec's "Execute Previous NppExec Script" is the obvious one) is what makes it worth stating. Accepted by the maintainer at the pre-commit gate, as Notepad++ parity.

  The delta audit found the same lever reachable through the ABI, and it is accepted on the same terms. A plugin's `tTbData.pszModuleName` is whatever the plugin says, and nothing ties it to the plugin that sent the registration — `SendMessage` carries no caller — so a plugin can register a panel under another installed plugin's name with a `dlgID` of its choosing, and that other plugin's command then runs at every start, even after the first plugin is gone. §6.5 is the reason no check is added: a plugin is in-process code with the host's own privileges, able to write `session.xml` directly, so nothing here can stop a hostile one — and Notepad++ stores the declared name and finds the plugin by it at startup in the same way. What a check *could* do is catch a plugin that declares the wrong name by mistake, by comparing the declared name with the plugin whose notification or menu command the host is running when the registration arrives; that was offered and declined for now, and is the shape to reach for if a real plugin is ever found making that mistake.

  **Both levers are guarded since, behind Preferences → Security, on by default** — see the entry on the guard below. That check is half of it. The other half is that Code++ now signs the commands it records, so a saved command it did not sign, edited or copied, does not run. Turning the guard off restores exactly the behaviour described here, which is Notepad++'s.

  **~~Still open.~~ Both closed since — see the two entries below.** A restored panel whose plugin fails to load, or is no longer installed, sat as an empty group for the session: the new close step covers only plugins that loaded and then did not register. Closing those too would record them as closed for the next session as well, which is wrong for a plugin that is only temporarily missing; it wanted a "restorable but not shown" state in the model rather than a hide, and has one now. A panel whose name several installed plugins answered to was in the same position; discovery no longer registers two plugins under one name.

- **~~Discovery loads a plugin twice when both `plugins/X.dll` and `plugins/X/X.dll` exist.~~ Landed (Phase 5): discovery loads Notepad++'s layout and nothing else, and at most one plugin per name.** Measured first, as this entry asked, because refusing a layout stops every plugin installed that way from loading. One probe DLL, copied into every layout discovery had ever accepted, was loaded into a portable Notepad++ 8.9.6 and the process's module list read back. Notepad++ loaded `plugins\X\X.dll`, and `plugins\X\x.dll` differing only in case, because it builds the path from the folder name and lets the file system resolve it. It loaded nothing else: not the flat `plugins\X.dll`, not `plugins\X\x64\X.dll`, not `plugins\X\Y.dll`, and not `plugins\Config\Config.dll`, because it skips its settings folder by name. The `x64` layout is worth singling out. Code++ had accepted it since Phase 3 as "the `NppExec` / `ComparePlus` 64-bit layout" on nobody's measurement, and neither plugin installs that way — the real `NppExec` on the development machine sits at `plugins\NppExec\NppExec.dll`.

  `PluginHost::discover` now does exactly what Notepad++ does. It looks for the file named after each folder (the exact spelling first, then, on a case-sensitive file system, one matching regardless of ASCII case), and skips the `config` folder, which is also where Code++ points `NPPM_GETPLUGINSCONFIGDIR`. A DLL left directly in the plugins folder is logged at `warn` rather than loaded, so someone whose plugin has stopped loading can find out why. Nothing below a plugin's folder is looked at, which is also what keeps `ComparePlus`'s own `libs\git2.dll` out of the registry.

  **A second plugin answering to a name already registered is refused**, with a warning, and the first keeps it. That makes "one name, one plugin" a property of the registry rather than a check every consumer has to remember. `disabled.txt`, `shortcuts.xml` and a dock panel's module name all key on the file name through `module_key`. On Windows the layout alone makes a collision unreachable, since folder names are unique there regardless of case. A case-sensitive file system can hold `Foo/` beside `foo/`, though, and on Linux and macOS `module_key` reads `libfoo.so` and `foo.so` as one name. Folders are visited sorted by upper-cased name, the order NTFS already lists them in, so which one wins does not depend on the file system. That also replaces ext4's hash order on Linux, where plugin load order, command ids and the Plugins menu had followed whatever the directory returned. With a name unable to mean two plugins, the panel restore's refusal of an ambiguous name, added a commit earlier, had nothing left to refuse and is gone: `Shell::panel_owner` returns the one plugin, or none.

  **That guarantee holds only if every lookup normalises a name the way discovery does, and the security audit found one that did not.** Discovery keyed a plugin through a lossy conversion of its file name, while the shell's lookups went through `PluginInfo::filename()`, which turned a name that is not valid Unicode into an empty string. Such a name cannot be registered today, because discovery requires a folder name that is valid Unicode, but the invariant rested on that accident. Now one conversion produces both the name and the identity, and every comparison goes through `PluginInfo::module_key()`. A Unix-only test pins it with a file name that is not valid UTF-8. Two mutations, each reverting one half to the old conversion, are killed on Linux. The audit also flagged the `discover` doc's old "Phase 5 will need to validate resolved paths" note on symlinks. It now states the decision instead. Links are followed deliberately: a link in the plugins folder takes the same write access as a DLL there, so refusing links would stop nothing an attacker needs, and it would break the ordinary way to develop a plugin, which is to link its build output into the folder. The second audit round added two points the doc now makes too. The load resolves the recorded path afresh, possibly much later because loading is lazy, so whatever sits there by then is what loads; swapping it takes the same write access. And all of it assumes the plugins folder belongs to the user running Code++; a folder writable across a trust boundary would need its own analysis.

  **Verified by driving the real app against a pre-change build of the same tree**, on one profile holding the four staged plugins plus flat copies of two of them (the shape the development machine's own profile had), a plugin one level too deep, and a DLL in `config\`. The pre-change build maps eight DLLs and lists Export, Example Hello and Restore Probe twice each. One of those two probes is the DLL in the config folder: a file in the directory plugins are given for their settings, loaded and run as a plugin. The fixed build maps the four staged plugins and logs the two flat files it skipped. The discovery tests moved out of the Windows-only module into one that runs on every platform with each platform's own extension, which closes the "parametrise the `.dll` fixtures" follow-up that module carried. They cover every refused layout, the config folder, the flat copy beside a staged one, first-wins for a contested name, the fixed order (tested on an in-memory list too, because NTFS would pass a test of the file-system order whether or not the sort ran), and on Linux the case twin and the `lib` twin. The four plugin-host integration tests and the shell and Win32 tests that staged flat now stage in the Notepad++ layout.

  *Original entry:* Found by the security audit of the restore-by-command change, and pre-existing. `is_plugin_dll` accepts any DLL at the top of the plugins directory, and one inside a folder whose name matches it, so a profile holding an old flat copy beside the staged one loads both: two submenus, two sets of load-time notifications, and every record keyed on a plugin's file name — `shortcuts.xml`, `disabled.txt`, a dock panel's module — answering to both. The development machine this was found on has exactly that for all four in-tree plugins, from a layout that predates staging. The panel restore now refuses such a name; nothing else does. Notepad++'s own layout is `plugins\X\X.dll`; whether it also loads the flat form has not been measured, and should be before discovery changes, since refusing the flat form would stop loading any plugin a user installed that way.

- **~~A restored plugin panel whose plugin fails to load, or is no longer installed, sits as an empty group for the session.~~ Landed (Phase 5, Win32): such a panel is parked — not shown, and saved exactly where it was.** Measured against Notepad++ 8.9.6 before choosing, with the restore probe. Its panel was docked at the bottom, then the plugin was removed. On the next start Notepad++ shows no band at all: a `PrintWindow` capture shows the full editor. It writes the panel's `<PluginDlg … isVisible="yes">` record back unchanged, and after the plugin is reinstalled the next start runs its command and the panel is back. The old "Still open" note had the right diagnosis. Leaving the group costs a caption with nothing under it for the whole session. Closing it records it closed, so the panel would not come back with its plugin. What was missing was a state in the model for "open, but not shown".

  **The model.** `DockLayout::park` takes open plugin panels out of their groups into a `parked` list. It records, for each panel, the group it was in, where that group was and its place in the group list, its tab index, and whether it was the front tab together with which tab took the front when it left.
