Because a parked panel is in no group, nothing presents it: no backend needed to learn about it, and a group it had to itself simply is not carved. `to_session` puts parked panels back on a copy of the groups, in the reverse of the order they were parked. That undoes the parking exactly when nothing else has touched the layout. A property test pins it: every subset of six panels, placed every way the fixture can place them, parked in both orders, must save byte-for-byte the session that was loaded, and must restore it again when unparked.

  When the layout does change around a parked panel, what is saved follows the panel's group:
  * A group the user moves takes the parked panel with it.
  * If the group dissolves, the panel keeps the place the group last had.
  * A group dragged into another carries its parked panels with its tabs.
  * A parked panel takes the front back only while the tab that replaced it is still in front, so a tab the user brings forward in the meantime stays in front.

  `show` unparks first, `hide` closes a parked panel where it belongs, and `move_panel` moves one from there. `container_of` answers for a parked panel exactly as `show` will place it, and the precedence test walks both cases. A group that comes back to stay gets a fresh id, and every panel still parked from it follows it there.

  **The first `unpark` was wrong whenever panels came back at different times, and the code review found it.** A parked record places its panel relative to its group as the group was when the panel left. That is only right once the panels parked after it are back: the reverse of the order they were parked, which is how `to_session` restores them. The first `unpark` put back only the panels it was given. Two plugins whose panels shared a group, re-enabled in separate load passes, each came back into a group still missing the other. The reviewer reproduced two tabs coming back reversed, and a group whose last tab was in front coming back with its first in front. A group that had gone could come back in another group's place in the stack the same way. The property test above had not seen it, because it put each parked subset back in one call.

  Every change to a parked panel now goes through the whole layout, whether it brings the panel back, closes it or moves it. Every parked panel is put back, last parked first, then the change is made, then the rest are parked again. Each record then describes the layout as it now is, so the result does not depend on the order panels come back in. Parking the rest again would hand each group's front to whichever neighbour the removal rule picks, so a group on screen keeps the tab it had in front unless a panel coming back is owed it. A second property test puts every subset back one panel at a time, in every order, from both parking orders. That is 4,562 sequences across the six-panel fixture and a second fixture built for these cases: three tabs with the last in front, and single-panel groups stacked around a host panel's group. At every step the saved session must not change, and nothing on screen may change except the panel being added, which takes the front only when it is owed it. Closing or moving a parked panel must save exactly what the same close or move would have made with nothing parked. Fourteen mutations, each killed, among them the old `unpark` restored verbatim. The first run let one survive, the removal of the front-keeping step, because the check accepted any returning panel in front. It now requires the returning panel to be its group's front in the layout with nothing parked.

  **The security audit of that fix found a cost it added, and an older hole under it.** Putting every parked panel back meant every group that had gone came back for the length of each call, and each came back under a fresh id: up to one per parked panel, on every call. Group ids count up and wrap past `u32::MAX`, and `alloc_id` handed out whatever came next without asking whether it was still in use. The audit measured the wrap as reachable in about four minutes of a plugin showing and hiding one panel in a tight loop, which predates this change. An id handed out twice lets an operation aimed at one group land on another, and a new group given the id a parked record names would take that panel in when it came back. Both are closed. A group that comes back for the call alone keeps the id its records name, and only a group that stays gets a fresh one, so a call spends what the first `unpark` spent. `alloc_id` skips any id a live group holds or a parked record names. And `debug_assert_invariants` now checks that no two groups share an id, so every test that changes a layout checks it too. Both reviews asked for the front-tab repair's claim that one record at most can owe the front to a tab to be checked rather than stated; it is a `debug_assert` now, which the 4,562 sequences exercise. Eight more mutations, each killed.

  **Win32.** At the end of every load pass, after `close_unregistered_restored_panels`, each open plugin panel with no registered window whose plugin is not loaded is parked: the plugin is not installed, disabled, failed to load, or was never attempted. `Shell::panels_without_a_loaded_plugin` answers which. It only ever finds something after the startup pass, because every other open plugin panel was opened by its plugin registering it. A panel with a window is never parked, whichever plugin its name belongs to. At the start of the next pass that loads the panel's plugin, it is put back before the pass notes what to restore, so the ordinary restore runs its command. That is reachable mid-session: a plugin disabled at startup can be re-enabled in the Plugin Manager and loaded by the next menu open. Registering a parked panel unparks it too, which is how Notepad++ treats a panel it saved open. A restored panel whose plugin *did* load and ran its command but never registered is still closed, as before. Re-running a command that did not produce the panel at every start is the wrong default when the likeliest cause is a plugin update that renumbered its menu.

  **Verified by driving the real app against a pre-change build**, in five sessions with the restore probe, a plugin staging does not reinstall:
  1. Open its panel.
  2. Uninstall it.
  3. Corrupt its DLL, so `LoadLibraryW` fails.
  4. Disable it in `disabled.txt`.
  5. Restore it.

  In sessions 2–4 the pre-change build shows a visible 1262×160 group with no panel in it, captured as an empty "Restore Probe Panel" band. The fixed build shows no group and logs the parking, and `session.xml` still holds the panel as open, `cmd` included. In session 5 the panel is back, with its command run between `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` and `NPPN_READY`. The mid-session path was driven as well: a session started with the probe disabled has the panel parked. The Plugin Manager's checkbox was then set cross-process through `LVM_SETITEMSTATE` with an `LVITEMW` written into Code++'s own memory, so the dialog got the `LVN_ITEMCHANGED` a click sends and the user's mouse was never touched. That updated `disabled.txt`, and opening the Plugins menu loaded the probe and brought the panel back through the same restore. The review's case was driven the same way, against the first version of this change: example-hello's panel and the restore probe's shared the bottom group with the probe's in front, both plugins started disabled, and the two were re-enabled and loaded one at a time. That version saved the group with its tabs reversed and the other tab in front; this one saves it exactly as it was.

  GTK and Cocoa are unchanged. They still drop plugin panels at restore, because they cannot host one. *(GTK has since parked such a panel exactly as Win32 does — see the GTK plugin-panel entry below. Cocoa is as described.)*

- **~~A hand-edited `session.xml`, or a plugin declaring another plugin's name, can choose which command Code++ runs at startup.~~ Guarded (Phase 5, Win32), behind a new Preferences → Security page, on by default.** These are the two risks the restore-by-command entry above accepted as Notepad++ parity. They are closed here without giving up the restore.

  **What is checked.** Code++ signs each plugin panel's startup command when it records it. The signature is an HMAC-SHA256 over a fixed label, the panel's persisted key and the command index (`core::dock::restore_command_message`). The key is 32 random bytes generated on first use and kept next to `session.xml` as `panel-restore.key`, encrypted with DPAPI for the Windows account. The signature is saved beside the command as `seal="…"`. With the guard on, a saved command runs only if its seal checks out. A record whose seal does not check out no longer makes its plugin load at startup either, which also closes the related lever in the eager-load entry above. The MAC and the randomness are Windows' own CNG, so no crypto crate is added. Both libraries are loaded the first time a key is needed, from System32 only, rather than imported. Imported, they are mapped at every start, and a release build importing them measured about 2 ms slower to first paint (median of ten alternating runs), in sessions that never sign or check anything.

  **The key file.** A key file this account can read but not use — copied from elsewhere, damaged, not a key at all — is replaced, since nothing could sign with it. One that cannot be read for a moment, such as while a scanner holds it, is left alone and read on the next attempt. Replacing it there would cost every panel its signature over a passing lock, and the first version did just that, which the code review found. Two instances never read, create or replace the key at the same moment. Each holds a lock beside it while it does: `panel-restore.key.lock`, opened with no sharing, which the system releases for a process that dies holding it. The security audit found that without the lock, an instance could lose a replacement race and go on signing with a key no longer on disk. The other instance waits its turn for up to half a second, kept short because the wait runs on the UI thread. After a failure, Code++ leaves the key alone for ten seconds before trying again. The audit of the lock found why that is needed: any process running as the user can hold the lock file open. Retrying at once would then wait out the lock on the UI thread once per restored panel, about a minute of frozen window at startup. With the pause, a held lock costs one short wait per ten seconds. The earlier measures stay as a second line for a writer that ignores the lock: creation never overwrites, a replacement goes ahead only over the bytes judged unusable, and the key used is the one read back. A link planted at the key's path is not followed. What it points at is never read, and the link is replaced like any other file that is not a key; a planted hard link is replaced without writing through to the file it shares data with. Nothing is ever written to the lock file either, so a link planted there is opened but never written through. The symbolic-link case is tested, but that test needs Developer Mode, like the find-in-files link tests (DEVELOPMENT.md §2.6), and has not run on the development machine, which lacks it.

  **When a command gets signed.** Only when the plugin registering the panel is the plugin the panel is named for. `SendMessage` carries no sender, so the host marks, per thread, which plugin it is calling: its load entry points, every notification, its menu commands and inter-plugin messages (`plugin_host::caller`, `PluginCommand::run`). `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` carries the mark in `DockDialogParams::caller`. A registration under another plugin's name is recorded unsigned, with a warning naming both plugins. So is one from outside any host call: a plugin's own window procedure, a timer, another thread. `Docking.h` tells plugin authors this. A command is signed whether or not the guard is on, so turning the guard on later costs nothing.

  **What a withheld command costs.** The panel is parked where it was rather than closed, because nothing is wrong with it. It comes back the moment its plugin registers it, which for most plugins means the user clicking its menu item. That registration is signed, so the next start restores the panel normally. An unsigned record whose plugin is loaded some other way, such as the Plugins menu, stays parked through that pass rather than flashing an empty group (`PanelRestore::held`). It is restored after all if the plugin registers it from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`. A session saved before this change carries no seals, so each open plugin panel needs opening by hand once after upgrading. No migration is added, because trusting an unsigned record once is exactly the hole: anyone could write unsigned records.

  **What it does not stop, stated plainly.** Code running as the user can ask DPAPI for the key exactly as Code++ does, and so can sign anything. It could as easily drop a DLL into the plugins directory (§6.5). The line is drawn where one can be: an edited, copied or handed-over file cannot choose what runs, while a program running as the user still can, as it always could. The attribution is a check, not a boundary. A plugin whose timer fires inside another plugin's modal loop is taken for that plugin, and an in-process plugin can read the key from memory. A registration also still takes whatever identity it names, as it always could. A plugin that registers another plugin's panel name first holds that panel for the session. Its unsigned record replaces the signed one, so that panel is not restored by command at the next start until it is opened again. That costs a restore; it never runs a command. It is enough to catch a plugin declaring another's name by mistake, and to make doing it on purpose take effort. A session synced between two machines checks out on neither, because DPAPI keys do not travel between them. Each panel then needs reopening after switching machines, or the guard turned off.

  **Verified by driving the real app** under a scratch `APPDATA`. The plugin is the restore probe, which registers its panel only from its "Show Dock Panel" command. A switch added to it registers the panel under `example_hello.dll`'s name, with example-hello's "Insert Hello" as the command.
  * The probe's own panel, opened and quit, is saved with a seal, and a 308-byte DPAPI blob is created. The restart runs its command between `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` and `NPPN_READY`, as before.
  * That session with `cmd` edited from 1 to 0 ("Insert Hello"), under the same key: with the guard on, no command runs, the probe is not even mapped, and the panel is parked with a log line naming Preferences → Security. With the guard off, the probe logs `cmd[0] Insert Hello ran`.
  * The probe claiming example-hello's panel: the registration is logged as not signed and saved with `cmd="0"` and no seal. With the guard on at the restart, example-hello is not loaded and the buffer is untouched. With the guard off, example-hello is loaded at startup and its "Insert Hello" types 17 characters into the user's buffer. That is the attack the guard exists for.
  * The signed session copied into a fresh profile checks against another key, so the panel is parked and the probe not loaded. Opening the panel from the menu signs it again with the new key, and the next start restores it.
  * Preferences → Security: the category list has two rows. Picking Security hides the Recent Files controls and shows the checkbox, checked. Unchecking it and closing writes `<verify-panel-commands>false</verify-panel-commands>` to `config.xml`.

  One detail, recorded so it is not chased again: with the guard off, the probe's startup "Insert Hello" ran but its text did not survive. The restored untitled tab's backup landed afterwards and replaced the document; clicking the command by hand inserts normally. Restore commands run before a session's buffers are filled, in Notepad++ too, so example-hello's text surviving in the claim run is not something to rely on.

  Headless coverage: the seal's wire form and message in `core::dock`; RFC 4231's HMAC vectors and the key file's life (created, read back, replaced when it does not decrypt, left alone when it cannot be read, taken in turns under the lock, a held lock costing one wait per interval, a planted hard link replaced at the key and left unwritten at the lock) in `platform::panel_key`; the per-thread mark through nesting, panics and threads, at every call site, in `plugin_host`; and the shell's policy with a stand-in signer, down to a registration through the real dispatcher. The Win32 wiring is pinned by source checks. 35 mutations across the guard, each killed; the two that would follow a planted symbolic link need that ignored test, and are not among them.

  **Windows only.** GTK and Cocoa host no plugin panels. They keep the setting in `config.xml` but show no control for it. Their plugin commands run through `PluginCommand::run` all the same, so a backend that later hosts panels inherits the attribution. *(GTK has since done exactly that: it hosts plugin panels, shows the Security checkbox, and keeps its key in an owner-only file, as the GTK plugin-panel entry below describes. Cocoa is as described.)*

- **~~Code++ told a plugin `NPPN_READY` before `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, one plugin at a time, before its menu existed.~~ Landed (Phase 5): the load-time lifecycle now follows Notepad++'s, on all three backends.** Measured, not assumed — a probe plugin loaded twice (so a batch is visible) into a portable Notepad++ 8.9.6 and into Code++, logging every entry point, every `NPPN_*` and whether its own menu items existed at each notification, and ticking one item from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, one from `NPPN_READY`, and asking for a third to start checked through `FuncItem._init2Check`:

  | | Notepad++ | Code++ before |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Entry points, per plugin | `isUnicode`, `getName`, `setInfo`, `getFuncsArray` | `setInfo`, `getName`, `getFuncsArray` |
  | When the next plugin loads | before any plugin is notified | after the previous one was notified |
  | Notifications | `TBMODIFICATION` to all, `BUFFERACTIVATED` to all, `READY` to all | per plugin: `READY`, then `TBMODIFICATION` |
  | Own menu items at either notification | present | absent — built after the pass |
  | Ticks from `TBMODIFICATION` / `READY` survive | both | neither |
  | `_init2Check` item starts checked | yes | no — the field was read nowhere |
  | `isUnicode` returns FALSE | refused before any other entry point, load-error dialog | never called |

  Every row now matches. `codepp_plugin_host::LoadNotifications` owns the notification order, so the three backends cannot drift on it: each load loop pushes the plugins it commits and delivers once, after the loop, with no borrow held. On Win32 the menus are installed between the two — incrementally, in registry order above the Plugins popup's fixed tail (Plugin Manager, Open Plugin Folder, now present from startup), never by rebuilding, which would throw away the ticks earlier plugins set — and the accelerator table is rebuilt in the same step, so a plugin's chords fire by the time it hears READY. That also means a plugin loaded by a hotkey or a restored panel has its submenu straight away, where it used to appear only once the user opened the menu.

  **The one thing Code++ adds is the `NPPN_BUFFERACTIVATED`.** Under Notepad++ a plugin always sees the session's buffer activated between `TBMODIFICATION` and `READY`, because the session opens after plugins load; under lazy loading that activation happened before the plugin existed. The pass announces the buffer active *now*, to the plugins it loaded only, which gives a plugin that keys per-buffer state on that notification the state it would have had. "Now" is read afresh for each plugin, at delivery, not snapshotted before the broadcast — the security audit's catch: the handlers run with no borrow held, so a plugin that closes the active tab from its `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` would otherwise have every plugin after it told about a buffer that is gone. A test pins the re-read.

  **Verified with the real NppExec as well as the probe.** With its console restored from the last session, NppExec ticks "Show NppExec Console" from `NPPN_READY`; before this change the item came up unchecked beside a visible console, and now it comes up checked. `example-hello`, which registers its dock panels from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, is unaffected. A two-pass run (example-hello loaded at startup for its restored panel, the rest on the first menu open) yields the same popup as a single pass: registry order, fixed tail last. Pinned by a recorder test of the broadcast order, a source scan of the entry-point order, real-menu tests of `_init2Check` and of registry order across passes (including a plugin's own item already in the popup, which is why a submenu's position is found by looking rather than counted), and a guard that the menus and accelerators are in place, and their borrow ended, before `deliver` — all six targeted mutations killed.

  **Two more things the pre-commit gate found, both closed here.** The plugin's own name — `getName`, the submenu's label — reached the Plugins menu unfiltered: pre-existing, carried through the rewrite of that exact line, and the class this document records closing four times before (a bidi override reorders the entry a user decides to click on; a `\t` forges a shortcut column). It now goes through the same `sanitize_menu_label` as the item labels, with a real-menu test. And on GTK and Cocoa a plugin refused as ANSI was handed the host's message-routing callback *before* the check refused it — no capability it lacked, since its `DllMain` has run by then, but a failed plugin should not keep a live route into the host; the callback now goes in only once `isUnicode` has passed. A refused plugin's library is freed, as on every failed load and as Notepad++ frees it — the "never unload" rule of §6.4 is about a plugin that initialised, and this one has run nothing but `DllMain` and `isUnicode`.

  **What stays different, recorded rather than folded in.** GTK and Cocoa deliver in the same order but still build their plugin menus after the notifications and never apply a tick — `NPPM_SETMENUITEMCHECK` is a no-op there and `NPPM_GETMENUHANDLE` answers NULL, so no plugin can observe the difference, and `_init2Check` is not honoured on either. *(GTK applies marks since: it keeps each plugin's marks per command and paints them whenever the menu is built, which is what makes its build order unobservable, and it honours `_init2Check`. `NPPM_GETMENUHANDLE` still answers NULL there. See the GTK plugin-panel entry below. Cocoa is as described.)* Notepad++ also relays its own window messages (`WM_SIZE`, `WM_MOVE`, …) to every plugin's `messageProc`, which the same probe logged throughout startup; Code++ relays none. And Notepad++ refused the ANSI plugin with a load-error dialog at startup, which Code++ replaces with the failure reason it records for every failed load, shown in the Plugin Manager — a modal at a lazy load would interrupt whatever the user just did.

- **~~The `HWND`-shaped `UiPlatform` methods keep their trait defaults on both non-Windows backends, so a plugin cannot dock a panel or add a toolbar icon there.~~ Half landed (Phase 5): GTK hosts plugin dock panels — see the next entry — and its answer to the design question below needs nothing Cocoa could not use unchanged.** Still defaulted on GTK: `create_plugin_scintilla`, `register_modeless_dialog` and `add_toolbar_icon`. On Cocoa: every method listed below. The question was what a plugin-owned panel *is* when the handle it is given is not message-addressable, and it turned out to need no new ABI. `hClient` is the toolkit's own widget, which the host adopts rather than creating anything; the `DMN_*` that Windows sends to the panel's window procedure go to the plugin's `messageProc` export instead, with `wParam` the panel's `hClient` to say which panel. On Cocoa an `NSView*` would play the widget's part, and a Cocoa implementation should take this shape rather than invent a third. Original entry follows. `create_plugin_scintilla`, `register_dock_dialog` (which does a literal `SetParent` on Win32), `show_dock_dialog`, `hide_dock_dialog`, `dock_hwnd_by_name`, `register_modeless_dialog` and `add_toolbar_icon` are all defaulted on GTK and, since macOS m5, on Cocoa too. A plugin that only transforms buffer text — which is all four in-tree ones, and most of the useful third-party ones — is unaffected; a plugin with its own docked panel degrades to "the menu item does nothing visible". This is a design question rather than an implementation gap: the two backends need a shared answer to what a plugin-owned panel *is* when the handle it is given is not message-addressable, and inventing one per backend is how the two drift. Recorded here so the m5 completion is not read as full plugin parity.

- **Plugin dock panels and plugin menu check marks on GTK (landed, Phase 5).** Over Phase 5 the Win32 backend gained plugin panels that are ordinary dock panels, their restore at startup by running each panel's own command, the signature that guards that restore, the `DMN_*` notifications, and plugin menu ticks. On GTK a plugin's "show panel" command did nothing visible, `NPPM_SETMENUITEMCHECK` was a no-op, and the plugins were never told the host was shutting down. GTK now does all of it, and does it the Win32 way wherever the toolkit allows. Nothing moved backend-specific into `core`: the shared rules moved *down*, so the two backends call one copy. The decoding of `u_mask`'s container nibble, the container numbering and the `DMN_*` code packing are in `codepp_plugin_host::docking`. The panel identity and its caption are in `codepp_shell::intern_plugin_dock_panel` and `plugin_dock_title`, and whom a panel's notification goes to is `Shell::plugin_panel_message_target`. `ui_win32` calls the identity and caption helpers too, and a test pins that its registration interns through them; the router is GTK's alone, since Win32 sends a panel's notifications to the panel's own window.

  **What `hClient` is here.** A `GtkWidget*` the plugin made and has not put in a container or made a window of. The host takes its own reference, sinking a floating one as a container's `add` does. It never destroys the widget — the rule the GTK dock has kept since it landed, because destroying a container destroys what is in it. A plugin that destroys its own widget ends the registration: a `destroy` handler marks the entry gone at once, so nothing puts a disposed widget back in a container, and closes the panel from an idle, since `destroy` can fire inside a reconcile holding the dock borrow.

  **The widget goes in a scrolled container of the host's, and that was found by looking.** The first version handed the plugin's widget to the dock as it was. `example-hello`'s panel docked in an 80 px left band then drew its label across the splitter and into the editor: a label that cannot wrap asks for its whole width, and `GtkLayout` allocates a child no smaller than its minimum. The Dockable-panels entry above had predicted exactly this for "a future plugin panel", and it arrived with the first one. A real plugin's row of buttons would spill much further. So the dock now shows and moves a `GtkScrolledWindow` around the widget (through a frameless viewport, unless the widget scrolls by itself), which asks for next to nothing; a panel smaller than the widget scrolls. A display scenario pins that the container asks for less than the narrowest band across and down, whatever the widget asks for. Setting either scroll direction to `Never` fails it, reporting 437 px across or 228 px down. Its first version measured hidden fixtures, and GTK 3 gives a hidden widget no size at all, so every assertion held vacuously until the fixtures and containers were shown. The wrapper cost one thing that had to be handled: a destroyed widget used to leave its container by itself, while the wrapper stays where the dock put it, so dropping a registration now takes the wrapper out too. With two panels tabbed in one group and the first one's widget destroyed through a temporary change to `example-hello`, the group shows only the second panel, filling it. With that one line removed, the empty wrapper keeps half the group. The host now shows the plugin's widget once, at registration, where it used to show and hide the widget itself with the panel. That is the one property of the widget it sets, and a plugin asks `gtk_widget_is_visible` or `gtk_widget_get_mapped` whether its panel is on screen. Registration refuses the npp handle and the main Scintilla view by identity. It then refuses anything GObject's type check does not call a widget, a toplevel, and a widget that already has a parent, which covers every host widget and a second registration of the same one. §6.5's reasoning applies unchanged: these catch mistakes, not malice. A garbage pointer faults inside the type check rather than being declined, because memory has no `IsWindow`. `hIconTab` is a `GdkPixbuf*`, honoured under `DWS_ICONTAB`; anything else gets the generic glyph.

  **Why `DMN_*` go to `messageProc`.** A widget has no window procedure, and `messageProc` is the plugin's only other message entry point. The `NMHDR` is Win32's field for field (`hwndFrom` the npp handle, `idFrom` 0, `code` = `MAKELONG(DMN_xxx, container)`), so a plugin's decoding is shared between its two builds. `wParam` carries the panel's `hClient`, which is all a plugin with several panels needs to tell them apart. The send runs the plugin's `messageProc` *as* that plugin (`PluginMessageProc::send` sets the caller mark). A panel registered from inside a `DMN_*` handler is therefore attributed, and signed, exactly as one registered from a notification. `NPPM_MSGTOPLUGIN` goes through the same send, where it used to have its own copy. GTK carries its own `DMN_CLOSE` re-entry latch and `DMN_DOCK` / `DMN_FLOAT` queue, copies of Win32's `DmnCloseGuard` and `deliver_container_notices`, for the reasons given there.

  **Borrow discipline.** Every `UiPlatform` method runs inside the NPPM dispatch's `with_state` borrow, and the dock model lives in its own thread-local. So a `DMM*` handler only changes the model and marks it dirty, and `dispatch_nppm` reconciles once its borrow has ended. That is the shape of Win32's `dock_dirty`, arrived at for the same reason: a reconcile moves widgets, moving a widget runs signal handlers synchronously, and those handlers need the state.

  **Menu check marks.** GTK rebuilds the Plugins menu every time it opens, so a mark cannot live on a widget. A record per command id is the source of truth: `NPPM_SETMENUITEMCHECK` writes it, `FuncItem._init2Check` seeds it at load, every build paints from it, and a menu that is up is updated at once. The loaded plugins' commands are taken in after each load pass and before its notifications, so a tick set from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` or `NPPN_READY` is kept. That makes the order of loading and menu-building unobservable, which the lifecycle entry above had recorded as the one difference no plugin could see. An item becomes a check item the first time its plugin marks it. GTK 3 gets in the way twice. A `CheckMenuItem` toggles itself in its class handler, before any `activate` handler runs, where a Notepad++ item changes only when its plugin says so; so the click's toggle is undone after the command runs, leaving whatever mark the command set. And `set_active` re-emits `activate`, so a guard flag keeps a programmatic mark from running the command again. `NPPM_GETMENUHANDLE` still answers NULL: a `GtkMenu*` is not an `HMENU`.

  **Startup restore and shutdown.** `restore_panel_plugins` runs once, after the window is shown and before the main loop. It loads exactly the plugins a restored-open panel names (only signed records, with the guard on) and runs the load pass the lazy triggers run. That pass follows Win32's `load_plugins_where` step for step, one function per step so the two read side by side. Panel commands run in `LoadNotifications`' slot between `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` and `NPPN_BUFFERACTIVATED`; each group's front tab is put back afterwards. After `NPPN_READY`, a panel whose plugin loaded and still registered nothing is closed, one whose command the guard withheld is parked, and one no loaded plugin can supply is parked. `apply_saved` keeps plugin panels rather than dropping them, since the backend can now host them. At exit, the window's close and File → Exit both go through one `quit`. It captures the layout to save and pins it, so a plugin that hides its panel while shutting down does not change what is saved. Then it sends `NPPN_BEFORESHUTDOWN` and `NPPN_SHUTDOWN` while the panels still exist, then saves. The notifications go out with no borrow held, so a plugin saving its settings can ask where (`NPPM_GETPLUGINSCONFIGDIR`) and be answered — which Win32's teardown, unable to drop its borrow first, declines. A dialog a handler queues is dropped rather than shown on a window that is going.

  **The key on Linux.** `panel-restore.key` holds the 32 bytes themselves; there is no DPAPI, and a keyring daemon is neither guaranteed nor something an editor should start. The file protects it instead. It is created owner-only, through the same create-without-overwrite path as on Windows. It is read with `O_NOFOLLOW | O_NONBLOCK`, and trusted only if it is a regular file this account owns that no other account can read or write, holding exactly 32 bytes. Anything else is unusable, and replaced. The randomness is the kernel's `getrandom`. The HMAC is GLib's `GHmac`, reached through `glib-sys`: a crate already in the tree under GTK, so `platform` gains a dependency edge and no new crate, and a test pins it against RFC 4231's vectors. Everything that is not platform work moved into a shared core (`panel_key/mod.rs`), so Linux inherits Windows' lock, its retry pause and its rule of replacing only the bytes it judged unusable. What the file cannot match is DPAPI's refusal to travel. A whole config directory copied *with* its key carries the key along, and the session it signed checks out on the other side. A session file edited, or handed over on its own, still cannot choose what runs. Preferences on GTK gained the Security frame, with the Win32 pane's wording. *(Since moved to a page of its own: GTK's Preferences now has Win32's layout, a category list on the left picking the page on the right. It also reads back a number typed into a field and left uncommitted by Esc or the window's close, which it used to drop; Win32 always read it.)*

  **`example-hello` on Linux** grew a GTK panel module on raw `gtk-3` / `gdk-3` FFI, with its two panels, their ticks and unticks, and the rename. It creates nothing unless a display is open. The headless end-to-end test found why that matters: a test harness delivers `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` with no display, and creating a widget there aborts the whole process from inside GTK.

  **Verified by driving the real app** on a nested X server (Xephyr, with metacity as its window manager) under a scratch `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`:
  * The first panel's registration docks it at the bottom and sends `DMN_DOCK` with container 3.
  * The second panel tabs into the same group, both tabs showing the plugin glyph.
  * "Show" ticks its item; a plain click on a ticked item leaves it ticked.
  * `NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` switches tabs.
  * The caption ✕ sends `DMN_CLOSE`, and the plugin unticks its item.
  * Tearing a panel off sends `DMN_FLOAT` with container 4.
  * A rename moves the lookup name and leaves the caption.
  * At quit, `session.xml` carries `cmd="1"` and `cmd="3"` with their seals, and the key and its lock are owner-only, 32 and 0 bytes.
  * Relaunched and left alone, both panels are back with their items ticked. Of the four staged plugins only `example_hello.so` is mapped, read from `/proc/<pid>/maps`.
  * With one record's `cmd` edited to 0 ("Insert Hello") under the old seal, the command is refused with the Preferences → Security log line, the buffer stays empty, and the panel is parked and written back unchanged.
  * With the guard switched off through the new checkbox, `config.xml` records `false`, and the next start runs "Insert Hello" and types "Hello from plugin" into the user's buffer — the attack the guard exists for. The panel that command never registers is closed.

  Headless coverage: the `docking` helpers; the caller mark on `messageProc`; the caption's fallback and sanitization; interning; notification routing against the real `example_hello.so`; the check-mark record; the Linux key's permission, owner, length, symlink, FIFO and lock cases; and the plugin-host end-to-end test against the real library. Display-gated: the scrolled container's minimum, above.

  **What the pre-commit gate found.** The security audit's sharpest finding was a real gap, and older than this change. The table of plugin-panel identities is process-wide, capped at 64, and never gives a slot back: `DockPanel` is `Copy` over a leaked identity, so a slot cannot be reused. A plugin that registers its panel under a fresh name each time, and destroys the old one, fills it, and after that no plugin can register a panel until the process ends. That needs no hostile plugin — one that titles its panel after the current file and registers anew on every switch would do it — and it was reachable on Win32 before this change made it reachable on Linux. `core::dock` now caps the identities one module's registrations may create at `MAX_PLUGIN_PANELS_PER_MODULE` (8), so such a plugin runs out of names of its own and nobody else's. Identities a restored session created are not charged: a crafted `session.xml` already has `MAX_RESTORED_PLUGIN_PANELS` for a bound, and must not be able to spend a real plugin's allowance. The count is a pure function with a unit test; the rule through the real table is an integration test, `crates/core/tests/plugin_panel_quota.rs`, in a process of its own because the table is shared by every test in a binary. Both mutations — no quota, and a restore charged as a registration — fail it. The audit's other finding, a refused registration logged with the plugin's raw strings where the Win32 code before the move logged the sanitized caption, turned out milder than reported: the default log format Debug-escapes string fields (read in `tracing-subscriber`'s source), so no control character ever reached the log. The fields are sanitized again all the same, for a structured sink that escapes less, with a source guard on each backend.

  The review found five more. `PluginMessageProc::send` wrapped the call in `catch_unwind` and said it contained a plugin's panic; it cannot, because a panic cannot unwind out of a plain `extern "C"` function and aborts inside the plugin — what the Win32 code in this same change already said. The wrapper, carried over from the old `NPPM_MSGTOPLUGIN` path where it was just as dead, is gone, and the doc says why. Moving the container-number test into `codepp_plugin_host` had turned its `3` into `CONT_BOTTOM`, a comparison of the constant with itself; the numbers are pinned to Notepad++'s literals again, and a wrong `CONT_BOTTOM` now fails three tests. A plugin that destroyed its panel's widget and registered a replacement under the same name before the host's idle cleanup ran would have had the replacement closed by that cleanup. GTK had no guard on the ordering that bounds the `DMN_DOCK` / `DMN_FLOAT` round trip, which Win32 pins with a source scan; it has one now. And the two tests that need an unreadable key file skip under root, where `cargo test` hides the skip; they now fail when `CI` is set, and DEVELOPMENT.md §3.4 asks for a runner that is not root — checked both ways under a user namespace mapping this account to root. One Low the audit raised was not borne out: that an ACL could grant another account the key while the mode check passed. Checked with `setfacl`: granting an account read raises the group bits the check rejects, and taking them away again leaves that entry with no effective access, because on a file with an extended ACL the group bits are its mask. NFSv4 ACLs do not work that way and are not read; `sys_linux.rs` says so.

  **What is not done.** No third-party plugin registers a panel on Linux: `NppExec`, the obvious one, is Windows-only. So `example-hello` agreeing with the host is agreement with ourselves — the trap that let the `DMN_*` numbering stay wrong on Windows for two phases. The difference is that the Linux contract is Code++'s own, with no Notepad++ on Linux to measure against, so the missing check is a plugin author reading `Docking.h` and building against it. `create_plugin_scintilla`, `register_modeless_dialog` and `add_toolbar_icon` are still defaulted on GTK, and `DMN_SWITCHIN` / `DMN_SWITCHOFF` / `DMN_FLOATDROPPED` are sent on neither backend. Cocoa has none of this, and sends no shutdown notification either. On Win32 a dock registration outlives the window it was made for — nothing removes it when the plugin destroys its window — so a plugin that destroys its panel and registers a new one under the same name is refused as already registered for the rest of the session; GTK drops a registration when its widget is destroyed.


- **`plugin-host`'s other `catch_unwind` wrappers around a plugin's entry points cannot catch anything, and §6.5 says otherwise.** The host wraps its calls into a plugin in `catch_unwind` — `isUnicode` and `getName` at load, the load-time `beNotified`, and `notify_all`'s — and §6.5 states it as a rule that this keeps a Rust-written plugin's panic from unwinding across the FFI. It cannot: each entry point is a plain `extern "C"` function, and a panic that tries to unwind out of one aborts the process inside the plugin, before control returns to the wrapper. The GTK plugin-panel change found this for `PluginMessageProc::send`, removed that wrapper and corrected its docs; the others predate it and were left alone rather than widen that change into the load path, with the maintainer's sign-off. Two of them also guard host code — the panel-restore callback in `LoadNotifications::deliver`, and the string copy after `getName` — and should keep a wrapper around that code, with a comment that says what it covers. The rest should go, and §6.5's bullet should say what is true: a plugin's panic takes the process down, as a C++ plugin's uncaught exception does in Notepad++.

- **The config folder is created with the default mode, so other local accounts can list what is in it.** Every writer that creates `<config_dir>` does so with `create_dir_all` and no mode, which under a usual umask is 0755. The files in it are created owner-only — `session.xml`, `config.xml`, the panel-restore key and its lock are all 0600 — so no content is exposed, but their names and sizes are: that the key exists, how large a session is. Raised by the security audit of the GTK plugin-panel change as a project-wide pattern rather than anything that change introduced, and deferred with the maintainer's sign-off. The fix is one place that creates the folder 0700 on Unix, used by every writer, rather than a mode at each call site.

- **On Win32 a dock registration outlives the window it was made for.** Nothing removes a `DockEntry` when the plugin destroys its window, so a plugin that destroys its panel and registers a new window under the same name is refused as already registered for the rest of the session, and the dead entry keeps its registration slot. GTK drops a registration when its widget is destroyed and, since the pre-commit gate of its plugin-panel change, keeps a panel a plugin rebuilt under the same name. Win32 needs the same, driven on a Windows machine: a `WM_DESTROY`-time cleanup (or an `IsWindow` sweep before a registration is refused as a duplicate) and the same keep-the-replacement rule. Deferred with the maintainer's sign-off, because this host cannot verify a Win32 change.

  Noted with it, and smaller: `flock` does not give real mutual exclusion over NFS, so two instances on two machines sharing one home directory could race on the panel-restore key — bounded, because a replacement goes ahead only over the bytes it judged unusable; a lock file planted with looser permissions before Code++ first creates it keeps them, which costs nothing because nothing is ever written to it; and `cargo deny` warns that seven of `deny.toml`'s gtk3-rs advisory ignores no longer match any advisory, which is housekeeping for whoever next touches that file.
- **Host-side dark mode is unimplemented on every backend; the plugin ABI for it is wired and honestly answers "off".** `NPPM_ISDARKMODEENABLED` and `NPPM_GETDARKMODECOLORS` dispatch through `UiPlatform::is_dark_mode_enabled` / `dark_mode_colors`, whose trait defaults return `false` — and no backend overrides them, because no backend renders dark chrome. (Win32 carried its own hardcoded-`false` overrides from the Phase 4 dispatcher bring-up until Phase 5; they were removed as exact duplicates of the defaults, so all three backends now express the state the same way and an audit reading "only Win32 implements it" cannot recur.) Win32 additionally re-broadcasts the system theme flip as `NPPN_DARKMODECHANGED` (from `WM_SETTINGCHANGE` / `"ImmersiveColorSet"`), so a dark-mode-aware plugin hears the flip, re-queries the host, and correctly keeps its dark-mode path off. When host-side dark mode lands, the work is: a dark palette for the chrome on each backend; live implementations of both trait methods (Win32 from its own theme setting, GTK from `gtk-application-prefer-dark-theme` or Code++'s own setting, Cocoa from `effectiveAppearance` or Code++'s own setting); the 12-`COLORREF` palette behind `NPPM_GETDARKMODECOLORS`; and firing `NPPN_DARKMODECHANGED` on the host's own toggle — which GTK and Cocoa fire nowhere today, so those two also need the system-flip re-broadcast Win32 already has. The comments in `plugin-host` (dispatcher, FFI struct docs), the `UiPlatform` trait docs, and the `docs/nppm-coverage.md` rows for these three messages all point at this entry.

- **~~`ui_gtk` styles UDL buffers but offers no way to select one, and the comment explaining why is stale.~~ Landed (both halves).** The menu half landed with the §7.4 GTK-parity batch: `rebuild_udl_rows` + `udl::language_rows` splice loaded UDLs flat into the Language menu on `connect_show`, and the stale `build_udl_submenu` rationale is gone. The *"nothing detects them by extension either"* half landed with the Markdown-auto-apply change: `Shell::detect_lang(path, &udl_registry)` now layers UDL `ext=` associations on top of `LangType::from_path` (built-in lexers win; a UDL only fills an `L_TEXT` gap), so opening a `.md` file auto-applies the preinstalled Markdown UDL — shell-level, so all three backends inherit it. The original text is kept below for context. Found by the macOS m4e port. `ui_gtk::udl` is complete — `apply_lang` routes to the container lexer, `on_style_needed` answers `SCN_STYLENEEDED`, `resolve_lang_label` names the buffer — but `build_language_menu` appends only `build_udl_submenu` and never enumerates `registry.entries()`. UDL ids live in a dynamic space above the built-in constants and are absent from `LANG_TABLE`, so nothing detects them by extension either; the only route into GTK's UDL styling is a `tab.lang` restored from a `session.xml` written on another platform, or hand-edited.

  `build_udl_submenu`'s own doc comment states the reason as *"GTK's `apply_lang` does not style UDL buffers (it logs and falls through — see `platform.rs`) … They land when UDL styling does"*. That stopped being true when `ui_gtk/src/udl.rs` landed; `platform.rs` now calls it and returns early on a UDL id. So the condition the comment sets has been met and the rows were never added.

  The fix is `ui_cocoa::menu::rebuild_udl_rows` ported to GTK's `connect_show` handler, which already exists for the language marks — plus deleting the stale rationale. Not done here because there is no Linux runner on the macOS development host, and the milestone's own bug (a tagless submenu child colliding with `L_TEXT`'s id 0 and ticking the wrong row) is exactly the kind that wants a hands-on look at the menu it changes. Note GTK's mark path is `CheckMenuItem`-based rather than tag-based, so it does not inherit that specific hazard.

- **~~A persisted UDL language id can resolve to a *different* UDL after the `userDefineLangs/` set changes.~~ Landed (Phase 5): `session.xml` persists a UDL by name.** `core::session::Tab` gained `@udl`, carrying the `<UserLang name>`, and a UDL's scan-order id no longer reaches disk at all — `Shell::persisted_lang_fields` splits a tab's language into "a built-in lexer by id, a UDL by name", and `Shell::resolve_persisted_lang` reads the pair back with the name authoritative and `@lang` not consulted when one is present, so the two cannot disagree about which language a tab restores under. A name the registry no longer knows resolves to nothing and the tab falls back to extension detection — the honest degradation, where resolving a stale id is the bug. A bare UDL-range id in `@lang` is a pre-split session and is **declined**, which the first version of this change got wrong: it honoured the id once, "as a migration", and that is the one branch that could still restore a buffer under the wrong UDL's rules. The deciding fact is *when* it would fire — `Shell::new` runs `copy_preinstalled_udls` immediately before `UdlRegistry::scan_dir`, so a release that bundles one more preinstalled UDL renumbers every id that sorts after it **on the very boot that would perform the migration**. The hazard is correlated with the upgrade rather than independent of it, which is the opposite of what a one-shot migration assumes. Validating the id against the registry does not rescue it either, and that is worth stating because it is the obvious repair: a renumbered id resolves to a live entry — the wrong one — so the check passes. The cost of declining is that an explicit pre-split UDL choice reverts to extension detection once and the user re-picks it; that is visible and two clicks, where being styled by a different UDL is neither.

  **The entry predicted this would touch all three backends, and it touches none of them**, which is worth recording because the prediction was about the wrong layer. The three UI crates never see `session.xml`; they see `SessionRestoreEntry`, whose `lang` is an `Option<i32>` they pass straight back to the shell. Resolving inside `load_session_entries` — where the registry already lives — means that field now carries an id the registry has vouched for rather than a raw stored one, and the backends are correct without being edited. One resolution site is also the only thing that stops the three drifting on it, which is the failure this section keeps recording in other forms.

  Two adjacent holes were closed with it. `load_npp_session` upserts by path into the same `session.xml` records, and its `lang` comes from an N++ session file, where the attribute is a built-in language *name* and can never name a UDL — so a record it overwrote would have kept a `@udl` from the previous Code++ session, and that stale name outranks `@lang` on the way back. It clears the field now. And `UdlEntry`'s own "ID stability caveat" doc, which described the fix as deferred, now states the rule instead: the id is ephemeral and nothing that outlives the process may store it.

  Seven mutations, all killed: the save path writing the id instead of the name (fails the renumbering test *and* the field-split test), the load path consulting `@lang` first, a missing name falling through to the stale id, the migration branch honouring a bare id again, `existing.udl = None` deleted from the N++ upsert, `@udl` never reaching the wire, and `find_by_name` matching by position rather than by name. The renumbering test is the one that matters and it is built from two real scans of two real directories differing by one file. Its precondition — that the id actually moved and somebody else took the vacated slot — is asserted first, so it cannot pass vacuously. The last two of those mutations exist because the pre-commit review found them missing: nothing exercised the N++ upsert's cleared field, and the migration branch had no test that would notice it being restored.

  **Verified by driving the real Win32 app against a pre-change build of the same tree**, under a scratch `APPDATA` with a seeded `session.xml`, posting the UDL's `WM_COMMAND` id and reading the `session.xml` written at quit. On the fixed build: picking the Markdown UDL writes `udl="Markdown (preinstalled)"` and no `@lang`; adding a UDL whose filename sorts first and relaunching brings the tab back as Markdown and re-persists it as Markdown; a hand-written `lang="1024"` against that same directory is declined with the warning and the attribute is dropped; and a `udl="Deleted Lang"` falls back to extension detection with its own warning. On the pre-change build the same two steps write `lang="1024"` and then leave it unchanged — the number round-trips intact while the language it names has become the other UDL, which is the bug, reproduced.

- **~~Two of `ui_gtk`'s three display-gated tests failed on every run, so the print-export path had no live coverage while appearing to have a test.~~ Fixed (Phase 5).** Found while adding the cross-thread `SCI_*` scenario, and worth recording because the failure was in the *convention*, not in any one test.

  `platform.rs`, `print.rs` and the new `plugin.rs` scenario each built a real Scintilla widget, and each carried a doc comment instructing `cargo test -p codepp-ui-gtk -- --ignored --test-threads=1` on the stated reasoning that GDK must not be touched from two threads at once. **`--test-threads=1` does not do that.** It serialises tests without pinning them to one thread — libtest still runs each `#[test]` on its own spawned worker — and `gtk::init()` records the thread that first called it and *panics* on a call from any other. So the second and third scenarios did not race intermittently; they failed deterministically, every run, with `Attempted to initialize GTK from two different threads`. Confirmed on a clean tree before any of this milestone's changes: one pass, one failure.

  The three scenarios are now `pub(crate) fn`s driven by a single `#[test]` in `crate::display_tests`, which is what the doc comments had always claimed was happening. All three pass. The alternative — `harness = false` integration tests, as `ui_cocoa`'s `cocoa_smoke` uses — is right for AppKit's stricter *first*-thread requirement but wrong here: GTK needs only one thread, and these scenarios reach private items an integration test could not see.

  The general lesson is the one this section keeps restating in other forms: an instruction in a doc comment is not a mechanism. A `#[test]` that has never been observed passing is indistinguishable from one that has.

- **~~`ui_gtk`'s Find/Replace has two gaps the Cocoa port fixed: stale chrome after a replace, and an unsanitized query seed.~~ Landed (Phase 5, GTK parity batch).** `do_replace_one` / `do_replace_all` each call `refresh_chrome()` after their borrow returns, and a new `seedable_query` refuses to seed a display-hostile or multiline selection into the query field rather than substituting into it. Original entry follows. Both were found by the macOS m4a gate while comparing against `ui_gtk::search` as the reference, and both are in that reference rather than in the port. (1) `do_replace_one` / `do_replace_all` mutate the buffer inside a `with_state` closure, and GTK's `sci-notify` is emitted synchronously from inside the edit — so the notification re-enters while the borrow is held, `with_state` declines, and the tab's dirty marker and the status bar stay stale until an unrelated event repaints them. `ui_gtk/src/lib.rs` documents this exact contract; these two call sites do not honour it. The Cocoa fix is one `refresh_chrome()` after each borrow returns. (2) `open_dialog` seeds the query field from the selection with no display-sanitization check, while the Find-in-Files *directory* field in the same function is explicitly guarded with a written rationale — so the omission on the query field reads as an oversight rather than a considered exception. The Cocoa fix refuses to seed a display-hostile selection rather than substituting into it, because the field's contents are a search parameter and a substitution would search for the wrong thing. Neither is fixed on GTK here because there is no Linux runner on the macOS development host; both are one-line changes mirroring `ui_cocoa::search`.

- **~~macOS scrollbars.~~ Fixed on the third attempt (Phase 5), and the two failed ones are recorded because each looked correct.** The cause is in vendored code: `SCIScrollView::tile` calls `[super tile]` and then shifts the content view right by the line-number margin's width and shrinks it by the same, with no allowance for scrollers that *take space* rather than float over the content. `ScintillaCocoa::SetScrollingSize` re-assigns `hasVerticalScroller`/`hasHorizontalScroller` on every call, so any document resize re-tiles.

  Attempt one forced `NSScrollerStyle::Legacy` for a permanently visible bar, which is what the other two backends have. It worked until the first line longer than the viewport: the re-tile then left the clip view covering the scrollers and **both** disappeared. Measured — the clip went from 1019×531 to 1036×548 inside a 1080×548 scroll view while the scroller frames stayed put. `ScintillaView.mm:1474` carries `//[scrollView setScrollerStyle:NSScrollerStyleLegacy];`, commented out, so upstream met this too.

  Attempt two reverted to AppKit's overlay default and called `flashScrollers()` whenever the scroll position changed. That is the documented way to show overlay scrollers for programmatically scrolled content, and it is *not enough here*: Scintilla scrolls its own content, and the flash is a brief fade the user reported never seeing at all. Instrumenting showed the flash firing exactly once, at startup.

  What works is to force Legacy **and repair the geometry `tile` should have produced** — clip width = view width − gutter − vertical bar, height = view height − horizontal bar — re-asserted after every `SCN_PAINTED`, since that is what follows a re-tile. Host-side rather than a patch to the vendored tree, the same shape as `clamp_scroll_width_to_viewport` and for the same reason (§4.1). It is idempotent, so a paint where nothing moved costs one rect comparison. Verified by driving the app: the bug reproduces on the long line, the repair restores 1019×531, and a second repair changes nothing. The arithmetic is a pure function with unit tests including a transposed-axis case, on this project's usual reasoning that off-by-one rect maths is what a hands-on demo is worst at catching.

  A fourth report followed, and it was the *other* half of the same floor. `clamp_scroll_width_to_viewport` only ever raises `scrollWidth`, which is right while the window keeps its size — the floor is what stops the blank area right of short lines being unclickable — but the raised value outlives the viewport that justified it. Maximising and un-maximising a three-line file therefore left the document as wide as the old clip and a horizontal scrollbar appeared for content that was not there; it cleared on the next maximise or tab switch, both of which re-seed.

  **The obvious fix is wrong, and the pre-commit gate caught it before it shipped.** Re-seeding on a viewport change — resetting the width to 1 so Scintilla's tracking recomputes it from actual content — reads correctly and passed the test written for it. But `SCI_SETSCROLLWIDTH` also zeroes `lineWidthMaxSeen`, and tracking only re-raises during a subsequent `Paint`, so reading the width back in the *same* pass and flooring it at the viewport pinned it there. Measured after the reviewer raised it: with a 4 000-character line on screen, resizing 1500 → 900 collapsed the document from 26 525 pt to 839 pt and it did not recover, putting the rest of that line out of reach. Worth recording that the first attempt to reproduce it *failed* — the probe resized the window to the size it already had, so the gate never fired and it proved nothing. The finding was real; the test was not.

  What ships instead lowers the floor only when the width is *exactly the value this function last installed*, which is the precise statement of "nothing but the floor is holding it up". A content-derived width never matches that except by coincidence, and a coincidence is harmless because tracking re-raises it on the next paint while the line is still on screen. No window exists in which the width is 1, so the document view cannot collapse. Verified in both directions: a short-line file's document follows the clip through grow and shrink with nothing scrollable, and a long line on screen keeps its 26 525 pt range across the same resize.

  Two ordering details are load-bearing and easy to get wrong. The repair must run *before* `clamp_scroll_width_to_viewport` in the same notification arm, because that reads the clip's width and would otherwise measure the wrong, scroller-covering one and raise a floor that only ever rises. And the style must be set before the first repair, which is a no-op while the scrollers are still overlay.

- **macOS misses two of the three §8 budgets — cold start and keystroke latency. Memory passes, and the reported miss was a measurement error.** *(Same weight caveat as the Win32 entry below: §8 calls these budgets non-negotiable, so this deserves separate triage rather than sitting in a polish list.)* Measured at Phase 5 m1, release build, one empty buffer: `phys_footprint` **73.0 MB against 25 MB**, and cold start **~111 ms against 80 ms**. The full numbers and the partial attribution are in §8. The memory miss is the larger and the less understood of the two: `vmmap` shows the process mapping WebCore, JavaScriptCore, SwiftUI, GeoServices and ANECompiler — none of which Code++ links — but those are clean COW text pages that `phys_footprint` should not be counting, so they are a lead rather than the answer. What needs explaining is 68.5 MB of *resident writable* memory of which only 16.9 MB was ever written. Next step is `malloc_history` / `heap` against a live process; a second `vmmap --summary` will not add anything.

  **Both figures moved by m4b's re-measurement, and the cold-start one is now a different — worse, and better-understood — problem.** `phys_footprint` is ~77 MB, roughly unchanged. Cold start is **~235 ms against 80 ms, about 3×**, and the earlier ~108–122 ms figures are retracted: they closed the interval before `-[NSApplication run]`, so they measured neither a first draw nor the same span the other two backends measure. §8 carries the per-phase attribution; the two suspects this entry previously named — the toolbar's PNG decoding and the menu tree — were measured and are **both refuted**, at 6.9 ms and 6.7 ms.

  What the attribution leaves is one dominant item and two ordinary ones. **`NSApplication::sharedApplication` costs 55–61 ms on its own**, i.e. 70–76% of the whole budget is gone before Code++ constructs anything, and putting the binary in an `.app` bundle does not change it (61–69 ms bundled — tested, because it was the obvious hypothesis). Code++'s own share is window plus chrome construction (~37 ms) and the post-build startup work of styles, session restore and view settings (~35 ms).

  **That AppKit cost is a platform floor, not something Code++ provokes**, and a minimal-app reference now bounds the whole thing rather than just that one call. A twelve-line Objective-C program — `NSApplication`, one 1024×768 `NSWindow`, one `NSView` filling white — measures **149.7 / 182.9 / 195.0 ms from `main()` to first paint** and settles at **13.6 MB**. Win32's *entire* cold start is 94 ms, i.e. below the macOS floor for a blank window. §8 carries the full breakdown, including the 1 036 dylibs (WebKit, JavaScriptCore, WritingToolsUI among them) an empty Cocoa window ends up holding.

  **So the two budget misses are different problems, and only one of them is ours.**

  *Keystroke latency is the third miss, and unlike the other two it is now diagnosed.* p99 ≈ 20 ms against 5 ms, worse than either other backend. **Every keystroke repaints every visible line, and each line costs a fresh `CTLineCreateWithAttributedString`** — CoreText typesetting and glyph encoding, per line, per keystroke. The thread is busy for the whole interval rather than waiting for a frame (main-thread CPU equals wall time to within 0.04 ms), and latency scales with window height: p99 6.4 ms at 14 visible lines, 21.1 ms at 64. §8 has the profile, the per-phase split, and **five refuted hypotheses** — frame scheduling, the status-bar refresh, `SC_CACHE_PAGE`, `SC_PHASES_ONE`, and this backend's own `SCN_PAINTED` hooks — none of which should be re-tried.

  The full-bounds repaint is not something this backend chooses: `NSWindow`'s content view is unconditionally layer-backed on modern macOS (169 of 169 views measured), and the layer redraw hands Scintilla the whole rect. **So the one real fix is to cache `CTLine` objects per (text, style) run inside Scintilla's Cocoa `SurfaceImpl`** — turning a repaint of an unchanged line from a typeset into a draw. That is a patch to the vendored tree, which §4.1 deliberately keeps unforked, so it needs a decision rather than a commit: either carry the patch (and own it across Scintilla bumps, alongside the two host-side workarounds §7.4 already records for the same backend), or accept that macOS types at ~20 ms p99 on a full-height window. Neither other backend has the problem, because neither re-typesets per paint — Win32 goes through GDI/DirectWrite and GTK through Pango, both of which cache.

  *Cold start is the platform's.* Code++'s own share is the ~40–85 ms above the floor; driving it to zero still lands at ~160 ms against an 80 ms budget. The open question is no longer "what is slow" but "what should the target be" — whether §8 should give macOS its own documented cold-start figure the way it already gives each platform its own *memory* metric, with the honest justification that a 150 ms platform tax is not something an application can architect away. Worth one run each on Intel and on an older OS version first (the harness is a dozen lines, and the WebKit/WritingTools names suggest this is recent).

  **Memory is no longer a miss at all — 18.5 MB against 25 MB — and the reported miss was a measurement error.** §8 has the full correction: every earlier figure was taken by launching the binary and sampling it, which restores the previous session, so what was measured was whatever files the developer last had open rather than §8's stated "one empty buffer". Clearing `session.xml` first gives 18.5 MB; the accumulated session that produced the 76 MB reading held five files and a crash backup. Keep the sRGB pin — the per-surface halving was observed directly — but its headline "35% saving" is retracted along with the rest, since re-measured clean it is 18.9 → 18.5 MB. The paragraph below is the superseded reasoning, kept because the mechanism it describes is still right and only the magnitudes were wrong.

  **That 18.5 MB did not reproduce at m4c; the discrepancy is now closed, in favour of the smaller figure.** *(Superseded — see the m5 block in §8: 19.4 MB, confirmed by two independent instruments. The paragraph below is the m4c-era reasoning, kept because the question it raised was the right one.)* Same machine, same release profile, same "delete `session.xml` first" protocol: **47.1–47.4 MB**, settling there from 57 MB over the first eight seconds. Measured against the milestone's own stashed baseline it is flat, so it is not m4c's — it is either a pre-existing drift across m4b→m4c or a difference in how the two measurements were taken that has not been identified. Attribution at rest: IOSurface 20.5 MB, `MALLOC_SMALL` 14.4 MB dirty. Two candidates were ruled out cheaply — a stale crash-recovery backup (the directory held one empty file) and the sampling instant (the figure is stable from ~8 s). Whoever picks this up should establish which of the two numbers is the honest one *before* treating either as a budget verdict, because 18.5 MB passes §8 and 47 MB does not.

  *Memory was misattributed twice before that, and the second attribution stands as a mechanism.* This entry first said the ~73 MB looked like an AppKit-established floor because it did not move across milestones; it does not move because its dominant term is the window's compositing surfaces, which depend on window size. The follow-up lead — the scroll-edge-effect `NSVisualEffectView` — was then **tested and refuted**, saving nothing. The actual cause was the surfaces' *pixel format*: CoreAnimation gives a window on a wide-gamut display half-float RGBA (`RGhA`, 8 bytes per pixel). **Pinning the window to sRGB drops that to `BGRA` and the footprint from ~77 MB to ~50 MB — a 35% saving, landed.** §8 has the measurement and the pixel-level check that it changes nothing on screen.

  What remains is ~50 MB against a 25 MB budget, and it is compositing buffers sized by the window the user opened, not heap — a 400×300 window is already 27.6 MB. So the memory question is now the same shape as the cold-start one: not "what is leaking" but "is a budget written against Win32's private working set meaningful for a metric that counts GPU-shared window buffers". Three further levers exist and are untried: whether the three buffers can be reduced to two, whether the surfaces have to cover the whole window, and — the cheapest — **pinning the secondary windows too**. Only the main window is pinned today; the Find/Replace `NSPanel`, the alerts and the open/save panels still get wide-gamut backing while they are on screen. They are small and transient so no saving is claimed, but the split means two windows in the same app behave differently, which is the kind of asymmetry this document has been bitten by before. One shared helper at each construction site, and a measurement to say whether it is worth anything.

  It also means the cross-platform comparison was never like-for-like: on Linux those compositing buffers live in the X server, not in the editor's PSS. Whatever is decided about the target, `phys_footprint` and PSS counting different things needs to be stated in §8 rather than left implicit.

  Two things worth deciding alongside it rather than after: whether any of those frameworks can be kept unmapped at all (an `.app` bundle with a restrictive `Info.plist`, or avoiding whichever AppKit call pulls SwiftUI in, are the obvious things to test), and whether m1's numbers should gate m2 or simply be tracked. Recording them now is deliberate — §7.4's own post-mortem on the Linux memory drift concluded that measuring once at the end is exactly how a regression goes unnoticed, so every macOS milestone from here should re-record both figures.

- **~~`ui_win32::show_error_dialog` renders `ShellError` text unsanitized.~~ Landed (Phase 5), as a parameter type rather than the one-liner this entry predicted.** Five sites were composing error text into the body raw — a `ShellError`'s `Display` at both Save paths, Save As and the Save All summary, and an `SHFileOperationW` status string at the Recycle Bin failure — not the "at least two" recorded below; the other eight of the thirteen call sites were static text, already-sanitized parts, or the shell-composed `PendingDialog::Error`. `show_error_dialog` now takes a `DialogText` (`crates/ui_win32/src/dialog_text.rs`), whose field is private and whose only constructors run `sanitize_str_for_display`, so a caller cannot hand the dialog raw text: the omission that recurred three times as call-site discipline is a compile error here. The title is sanitized inside the function, since one caller receives it from the shell.

  **The one-liner would have been wrong, and the reason is why the type has three constructors.** Sanitizing inside the function substitutes `\n`, and three callers build their body with deliberate blank lines between a sentence, the path and the OS error. A flat `&str` cannot tell a caller-authored line break from one embedded in an error string — and the embedded one is precisely the fake-line injection the GTK comment cited below warns about — so the choice was destroy the structure or preserve the injection. `DialogText::paragraphs` and `DialogText::lines` sanitize each part *before* joining, which keeps both properties, and the unit tests pin exactly that: the caller's blank lines survive while a `\n` inside the error text does not. `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` reach the same result by sanitizing per part at each site, and every current site there does; they keep that discipline rather than the type, so this backend is the stricter of the three here. The security audit's one informational finding is closed in the same change rather than tracked: the shell's "Open failed" message sanitized its path but not the load error's text, and `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` present that message with no pass of their own — nothing attacker-influenced reaches that text today, but both halves are now sanitized at the shell, which is the one place all three backends inherit from.

  **Two sibling sinks were found by the same survey and are left open**, both pre-existing and neither the item this entry tracks. The Replace-in-Files confirmation (`handle_fif_replace_in_files`) formats the query, the replacement and the directory into a `MessageBoxW` raw, where `ui_gtk::search` and `ui_cocoa::search` sanitize all three for their prompt — and the query is seeded from the selection by `prefill_from_selection`, which refuses a newline but not a bidi override or a zero-width run, so buffer text can reach that prompt; that is the seed gap the m4a work closed on the other two backends. And the UDL editor's own `show_error` renders `UdlError`'s `Display`, which embeds `path.display()` unsanitized (`crates/udl/src/lib.rs`); the path there is one the user just picked in a save dialog, so the exposure is a folder name rather than attacker-supplied content. Both are one-line fixes of the shape this entry describes.

  **Verified by driving the real app, with a control — and the live route is not the one this entry named.** A path reaches the save error only through `tempfile`'s `PathError`, whose `Display` formats the path with `{:?}` and so already escapes bidi controls, so the "file with bidi-control characters" example below was the class rather than a reachable instance. What *is* reachable is a hand-edited `session.xml`: an `encoding` attribute is preserved verbatim as `Encoding::Other`, a dirty-from-backup restore keeps it as the save target where a plain load would replace it with the detected encoding, and the next File → Save fails with `UnknownLabel` carrying the label raw. Driven with `encoding="bogus&#x202E;lebal&#10;Press OK to delete all files"` under a scratch `APPDATA`, `WM_COMMAND(ID_FILE_SAVE)` posted to the main window, and the `#32770`'s body static read back through `GetWindowTextW`: the pre-change build renders U+202E verbatim across two visual lines, the injected second line included; the fixed build renders both characters as U+FFFD on one line. The guard itself is mutation-verified — constructing the type around a raw `String` outside its module is `E0423`, and removing the sanitizer from either constructor fails the tests that pin it.

  Original entry follows. Surfaced by the Phase 5 m2 audit while checking the Cocoa equivalents. `ui_gtk::menu::on_save` runs error text through `sanitize_str_for_display` before it reaches a dialog, with a comment noting that a `ShellError`'s `Display` can carry a path and that secondary dialog text renders control characters as real lines; `ui_cocoa` now does the same. `ui_win32`'s `show_error_dialog` (called from at least two sites) does not, so a save failure naming a file with bidi-control characters reaches a `MessageBoxW` raw. Same class as the two incidents already recorded in this section (`NPPM_SETSTATUSBAR`, the workspace tree), and the fix is the same one-liner. Not fixed in the m2 commit because it is a different backend with no runner on this host; tracked here so the third instance of this pattern does not go the way of the first two.

- **~~macOS: only the text itself was clickable — the empty space to the right of a line was not.~~ Fixed (Phase 5, post-m3b).** The residual of the fix below, and reported straight after it: double-clicking in the blank area right of a line's content to select the line — an ordinary gesture that works on Win32 and GTK — did nothing.

  Same root as its predecessor, one level subtler. Scintilla's Cocoa backend sizes the `NSScrollView` document view from `scrollWidth` and clamps only the *height* to the clip rect (`cocoa/ScintillaCocoa.mm::SetScrollingSize`: *"Ensure all of clipRect covered by Scintilla drawing"*), never the width — `docWidth = Wrapping() ? clipRect.width : scrollWidth`. With width tracking on, `scrollWidth` is the longest *visible* line, so the document view ends exactly where the text ends and every click past it lands on the enclosing `NSClipView`. Neither other backend shows this, because neither sizes a view from `scrollWidth`; there the whole editor widget is the click target.

  Fixed host-side rather than by patching the vendored tree (DESIGN.md §4.1 keeps it unforked): `clamp_scroll_width_to_viewport` raises `scrollWidth` to the clip width whenever tracking leaves it lower, re-asserted on `SCN_PAINTED` (tracking recomputes during layout, so a one-shot floor would not survive) and immediately after a rebind. Tracking stays on, so a genuinely long line still scrolls and the width still shrinks back when that line is deleted — this only raises the floor. The clip width is read from the live `NSClipView` rather than computed as "view width minus margins minus scroller", so it stays correct as those change.

  Measured both directions: a short-line file gives `scrollWidth == visible == 956` with a click 800 px right of the text landing on `SCIContentView` and moving the caret; a 600-character line gives `scrollWidth 3973 > visible 956`, stable across samples, so horizontal scrolling is intact and the floor does not oscillate against tracking.

- **~~macOS: `SCI_SETSCROLLWIDTH(1)` without tracking collapsed the document view to one point wide, killing mouse input.~~ Fixed (Phase 5, post-m3b).** The third and actual cause of "the mouse stops working", after two earlier fixes that were each real but not this. Repro: mouse works at launch, dies the moment you switch to any other tab, and never recovers.

  `rebind_active_view` reset the tracking-mode horizontal scroll high-water mark with `SCI_SETSCROLLWIDTH(1)` on every document swap — ported from `ui_gtk`, but **without its companion `SCI_SETSCROLLWIDTHTRACKING(1)`**, which is what makes the reset safe by having Scintilla recompute the width from visible content. GTK survives the omission because its backend does not size a widget from `scrollWidth`. Cocoa does: `scrollWidth` drives the `NSScrollView` document view's frame, so an unpaired reset left `SCIContentView` **one point wide**.
