Hit-testing then resolved to the enclosing `NSClipView` for every click past x=1, Scintilla never received a `mouseDown:`, and the editor looked inert — while the keyboard kept working, because key events go to the first responder rather than through hit-testing. That asymmetry is what made three separate investigations all start by suspecting event delivery.

  Found by dumping the Scintilla view subtree's frames either side of a tab switch: `SCIContentView` went from `2000x652` to `1x652`, and `hitTest` from `SCIContentView` to `NSClipView`, while `SCI_POSITIONFROMPOINT` kept returning the correct offset — i.e. Scintilla's *layout* was fine and only the event never arrived. Two hypotheses were tested and refuted first, and are recorded so they are not re-tried: that the tab strip's wholesale rebuild was destroying the button mid-action (deferring the rebuild changed nothing), and that the button was involved at all (a purely programmatic `select_tab_by_id` breaks it identically).

  Both sends now live in one `seed_horizontal_scroll` helper called at startup and on every rebind, and a source scan asserts `SCI_SETSCROLLWIDTH` has exactly one call site which also enables tracking. Mutation-verified by deleting the tracking call.

- **~~macOS: opening an already-open file left the editor bound to the previous document.~~ Fixed (Phase 5, post-m3b).** Second report from the same user session as the activation bug below, and initially indistinguishable from it: "the mouse works at launch, but as soon as a file is opened it stops, and closing the file doesn't help". The mouse was never the problem. `Shell::open_file` returns `OpenFileOutcome::SwitchedToExisting` when the path is already open — it flips `active_tab` and queues no load, so no §5.4 wake fires and nothing rebinds the single Scintilla view. The Cocoa backend discarded the return value at all four call sites, so the tab strip showed the new tab while the view kept rendering the old (frequently empty) document. Clicks moved a caret in a buffer the user could not see, which reads exactly like dead mouse input.

  The enum's own doc comment states the requirement — *"the UI must issue a synchronous view rebind … otherwise the tab bar shows the switch while the editor keeps rendering the previous buffer"* — and `ui_gtk` has always honoured it. This was a porting omission, not a design gap.

  Found by instrumenting rather than reading: window state (key, active, first responder, hit-test) was healthy at every step, and a synthesized click proved the event path worked, so the plumbing was exonerated early. The tell was printing the shell's tabs alongside the bound document pointer — `active_tab` pointed at one tab while `SCI_GETDOCPOINTER` returned another's. Two follow-ups were then needed to avoid a wrong conclusion: a click immediately after the rebind still resolved to line 0, which looked like a second bug but is the view not yet having painted in that same run-loop turn — clicking 1.5 s later lands on the correct line with the correct caret.

  All four opens now route through one `open_path` helper, and a source scan asserts `shell.open_file(` appears exactly once so a future caller cannot quietly reintroduce it. Mutation-verified. The scanner cuts the file at `#[cfg(test)]` first, because `include_str!` otherwise counts the pattern quoted in the test's own assertion message — it flagged itself on the first run.

- **~~macOS: the window was never key and the app never active, so mouse input did not work.~~ Fixed (Phase 5, post-m3b).** Reported by a user testing the m3b build: typing and deleting worked, but clicking in the editor did nothing — no caret placement, no selection. The cause was ordering, not Scintilla: `makeKeyAndOrderFront` and `activate()` were called from `run()`, i.e. *before* `-[NSApplication run]`. For a process outside an `.app` bundle that silently does nothing — the window never becomes key, the application never becomes active, and the window server stops routing clicks to it.

  Diagnosed by measurement rather than inspection, because every layer looked correct: the view hierarchy, frames and first responder were all right (`SCIContentView` at the expected size, first responder as expected), and a synthesized `NSEvent` pushed through `NSApp.sendEvent` moved the caret from 0 to 20 — proving the whole in-process path worked. The discriminator was activation state: a bare `./codepp` reported `app_active=false has_key_window=false` from inside the run loop, while the *same binary* copied into a minimal `.app` and launched with `open` reported all true. Moving activation into the delegate's `applicationDidFinishLaunching:` makes the bare binary report all true as well, which matters because `cargo run` is the documented development workflow (DEVELOPMENT.md §4.5).

  **Why no test caught it, and what now does.** The failure needs a real run loop and a real window server and manifests as *absence* — no event arrives. The display-gated `cocoa_smoke` test has no run loop (it is `harness = false` so it owns the main thread, not so it pumps events), and every milestone demo to that point verified logic through `session.xml` round-trips, which never required the app to be interactive. A source-scanning guard in `ui_cocoa` now asserts that activation appears in `activate_main_window` and *not* in `run()` — the same tool `ui_gtk` uses for its single-view invariant, and chosen for the same reason: the property is unobservable at runtime in CI but trivially checkable in the source. Mutation-verified (putting `makeKeyAndOrderFront` back into `run()` fails it).

  Worth carrying forward: an `.app` bundle also fixes it, and §9.2 already schedules one for macOS distribution — but the bundle should not be the reason input works, since development happens through `cargo run`.

- **macOS m1 needs an `NSApplicationDelegate` in m2, for three things at once.** All three surfaced in m1's pre-commit audit and are deferred together because they share one piece of machinery. (1) **Closing the window leaves a menu-bar-only process** — `applicationShouldTerminateAfterLastWindowClosed:` is the fix, and Code++ is single-window by design (§10), so the answer is unconditionally yes. (2) **`perf.report()` after `app.run()` is unreachable on the standard Quit path** — `-[NSApplication terminate:]` calls `exit()`, so `run()` never returns; `applicationWillTerminate:` is the correct home for it. Harmless in m1 (no keystroke instrumentation on this backend yet, so the report would be empty, and cold start is emitted separately by `mark_first_draw`), but it must be fixed before the §8 keystroke-latency figures can be gathered on macOS at all. (3) m2 needs the delegate anyway for session save-on-quit, which is the same hook as (2).

  Also fixed in m1 rather than deferred, recorded because the pattern will recur: a programmatically constructed `NSWindow` defaults to `releasedWhenClosed == true`, which sends an unbalanced `release` on close while a Rust `Retained<NSWindow>` still owns a +1. `ui_cocoa` now calls `setReleasedWhenClosed(false)` explicitly. Every window the m3 dialogs create needs the same call — those *will* be opened and closed as ordinary control flow rather than living until `exit()`, so the latent double-free there is not latent.

- **Win32 misses both §8 latency budgets with a uniform shift, not a tail.** *(Weight: §8 calls these budgets "non-negotiable" and CLAUDE.md re-anchors that as a phase-blocker constraint. This is heavier than a Phase 5 polish item; it sits here for now because that is where every other deferred-and-tracked entry lives, but the item deserves separate triage attention.)* The first Win32 `--perf` run (this commit) confirms every hook fires — `WM_CHAR` in the pump, `SCN_MODIFIED` + `SCN_PAINTED` off `WM_NOTIFY.nmhdr`, all filtered to the main editor's `hwndFrom` — and produces 1 000-sample distributions at 7 and 20 char/s. Every row lands at p50 ≈ 6 ms, p99 ≈ 7–9 ms, max ≈ 10 ms, on both an empty buffer and a real 10 000-line file. Cold start sits at ~94 ms (empty) / ~99 ms (10k-line file), 14–19 ms over the 80 ms budget. Every phase gate through Phase 4 passed on Windows without this constraint being properly checked — `--perf` didn't exist until this commit — so a finding of this size only turned up now that the wire went live.

  The shape is what makes it interesting, and what makes it distinct from the GTK entry above: the GTK tail is a sharp cliff (p50 barely moves while p99 jumps 14× at higher rates), Win32's distribution is compact and uniformly shifted up (p50→max spread inside each row is 53–66% — not flat, but nothing like GTK's 42× p50-to-p99, and every one of 3 000 samples came in below 10.13 ms). GTK has "something stalls occasionally"; Win32 has "something costs ~5 ms every single keystroke." Doubling the input rate doesn't queue anything on Win32 either — rules out backlog. The two 7 char/s rows differ by ~20% at p99 in the *faster-with-more-content* direction; the isolation is not clean (buffer size and lexer state both changed) so this notes a candidate rather than refuting the lexer as GTK's dedicated test did.

  Next step is a profiler, not more black-box runs — the remaining suspects can't be distinguished from the outside: DWM composition scheduling (60 Hz frame timing on a non-vsync'd input path), the per-`SCN_UPDATEUI` chrome refresh (`refresh_status_dynamic_parts` issues multiple `SendMessage` per keystroke; the toolbar state refresh does more; both fire on every edit), `TranslateAcceleratorW` on the hot path, or Scintilla's Direct2D paint interacting with the themed frame chrome. Since ~5 ms per keystroke is the entire measured cost above GTK's baseline, whichever candidate accounts for it is the whole fix, not part of one.

- **~~`NPPM_SETSTATUSBAR` bypasses the display-sanitization consolidation.~~ Landed (Phase 5).** The dispatcher was reading the plugin's wide-pointer, handing the string to `Shell::set_status_bar`, and both backends piped it straight to the status bar with no call to `sanitize_display_char` / `sanitize_filename_for_display`. `\t`, `\r`, `\n`, the full `Bidi_Control=Yes` set, zero-widths, and U+2028/U+2029 all reached the chrome unfiltered on both backends — the "legitimate plugin, hostile content" scenario `ffa5246` was meant to close but missed for this one NPPM message.

  Closed shell-side rather than per-backend. New `codepp_shell::sanitize_str_for_display` — same substitute-with-U+FFFD policy as its filename/path cousins, no length cap since status text isn't a filename — runs inside `HostServices::set_status_bar` (`crates/shell/src/lib.rs`) before dispatching to whichever `UiPlatform::set_plugin_status` the current backend provides. That deliberately deviates from the audit's suggestion of a two-site fix in `ui_win32` + `ui_gtk`: the shell layer is where the policy already lives (per `sanitize_filename_for_display`'s own "don't duplicate the sanitizer, it drifts silently" rationale), one call site means the eventually-landing GTK plugin dispatcher inherits the fix without a second edit, and `set_plugin_status` has no other caller in the workspace so the additional coverage is zero. Also dropped the now-dead `text.replace('\0', "")` in `write_status_part` — U+FFFD encodes as `0xFFFD`, not zero, so it never re-triggers the `SB_SETTEXTW` truncation the strip was defending against; and every path into `write_status_part` now either passes static text or has already sanitized upstream. Verified: 170 shell tests pass (two new — pure-helper coverage runs on every runner; end-to-end via `dispatch_plugin_message` matches the existing Windows-gated dispatch-test pattern); clippy pedantic + fmt clean.

- **~~The Win32 Workspace tree renders unsanitized filenames, and one of its context-menu actions executes the file.~~ Landed (Phase 5).** `insert_tree_folder_children` used to write `entry.file_name().to_string_lossy()` straight into `TVITEMW.pszText`, so a filename carrying U+202E RIGHT-TO-LEFT OVERRIDE (`invoice\u{202E}fdp.exe`) rendered as `invoice⁩exe.pdf` — a fake PDF whose real extension the "Run by system" context-menu entry then handed to `ShellExecuteW(hwnd, "open", …)`, which for an EXE means *run it*.

  Closed by copying the FIF listview's shape: `WindowState.workspace_tree_names: HashMap<isize, String>` stores the real component name per `HTREEITEM.0`, `tree_insert_item` writes the `sanitize_filename_for_display` output into `pszText`, and `tree_item_full_path` / `tree_locate_path` read the real name from the map instead of parsing `pszText` back — the exact "functional value vs. label" split `handle_fif_listview_dblclk` already used. The map is populated on insert, drained on delete (both `tree_clear_all` and `tree_delete_all_children`, the latter walking the doomed subtree first), so its invariant is "every entry keys on a live tree handle". The existing `starts_with(&workspace_root)` defence-in-depth checks in the double-click and context-menu paths stay — they defend against a different failure mode (a hostile FS returning a component with an absolute-path prefix).

  Verified: `cargo build/clippy/fmt` clean on Windows, the tree-driving flow still needs a Windows runner to exercise end to end (no automated coverage for `SysTreeView32`-driving functions, consistent with the rest of the file). `ui_gtk` still has no workspace tree, so the same trap applies whenever one lands — the ownership model there is the map, not `pszText`-round-trip.

- **~~Tab click arm/commit should key on buffer identity, not vector index.~~ Landed (Phase 5, Win32; GTK inherited the same shape already).** The pre-fix `tab_close_armed` / `tab_pin_armed` recorded a vector index at `WM_LBUTTONDOWN` and checked it against a same-position hit-test at `WM_LBUTTONUP`. Mouse capture holds the button-down/up pair on the tab strip but does not gate keyboard input, so a `Ctrl+W` / plugin `NPPM_MENUCOMMAND` / any other keyboard-triggered `Shell::move_tab` / `close_active_tab` between DOWN and UP could reorder or shrink `Shell.tabs` while the arm was live, sliding a different logical tab into the armed visual slot. Under the old index-keyed comparison the release hit-test still matched, and the action committed on the wrong buffer.

  Ported the same shape `ui_gtk`'s `close_tab_by_id(id)` already used: both arm fields changed from `Option<usize>` to `Option<i32>` and now carry `Tab.id`. A new pure helper `resolve_tab_arm_commit(tabs, armed_id, release_id) -> Option<usize>` takes the two ids and, iff they match, returns the id's *current* vector index in `Shell.tabs` — the value that then drives `TCM_SETCURSEL` and `Shell::set_pinned`. Every reject case (arm missing, release missed every tab, ids mismatch because a different tab slid in, armed buffer was closed under the arm) collapses to `None`. `Tab.id` is `Shell::allocate_buffer_id`-assigned monotonically without reuse, so a close-and-reopen of the same file between DOWN and UP gets a fresh id and rejects too. Eight new unit tests pin every path through the helper, keeping the invariant enforceable without a live `SysTabControl32` HWND.

  This lands Win32 ahead of the Phase 5 cross-platform mouse-input abstraction the original entry anticipated — GTK is already id-keyed via its own `close_tab_by_id`, so no cross-platform abstraction was needed to unify them. When Cocoa lands it will inherit the same id-based semantics from the shell layer directly.

  Verified: 138 `ui_win32` tests pass (130 pre-existing + 8 new); clippy pedantic + fmt clean; `/review` PASS; `/security-audit` SECURE (the "clicked X, closed Y" case would have surfaced a save-modal for the wrong buffer, which is a data-loss adjacent hazard — the audit noted the fix closes it and that `Shell.active_tab` is synchronously updated to the resolved id before `ID_FILE_CLOSE` is queued, so the modal names the right buffer).

- **~~Toolbar background colour on the themed Win32 toolbar control.~~ Landed (Phase 4).** The `NM_CUSTOMDRAW` / `CDDS_PREPAINT` variant of option (a) landed in `main_wnd_proc`'s `WM_NOTIFY` arm: `FillRect` the reported `nmcd.rc` with `dialog_bg_brush()` (Code++'s established chrome color, `DIALOG_BG = 0xF9F9F9`), return `CDRF_NOTIFYITEMDRAW` so the toolbar hands each button back to UxTheme for its themed hover/pressed/disabled paint. Beats UxTheme cleanly — CustomDraw's PREPAINT stage fires before the theme background fill, so no subclass or theme drop was needed. `TBSTYLE_CUSTOMERASE` was not required; the toolbar already sends `NM_CUSTOMDRAW` unconditionally. Kept the same chrome bar-across all of Code++'s bars converge on one shade, no per-theme drift. The active-tab indicator entry below still tracks its own UxTheme cooperation battle — its fix is separate.

- **~~Active-tab indicator (orange top edge) on the themed Win32 tab strip.~~ Landed (Phase 4).** Path (a) — full owner-draw via `TCS_OWNERDRAWFIXED` + `WM_DRAWITEM` — arrived in two commits: `670bc40` shipped the owner-draw framework (save icon + close-X paint) as prerequisite scaffolding, and `ec19b1f` landed the indicator itself, `paint_tab_item`'s `if active { FillRect(strip_rc, TAB_ACTIVE_INDICATOR) }` block between the background fill and the icon blit. The indicator uses Material orange 400 (`TAB_ACTIVE_INDICATOR = 0x26A7FF`). Subsequent tuning brought the strip to `TAB_ACTIVE_INDICATOR_HEIGHT_PX_HIDPI = 8` inside a `TAB_HEIGHT_PX = 30` cell, with the 20-px icon (`TAB_ICON_DISPLAY_PX`) overwriting the strip inside its own ~20-px-wide column via `AlphaBlend` — the strip stays fully visible across the rest of the tab width, and the icon centre stays aligned with the text centre.

- **Dockable panels: shared model (`core::dock`), landed on all three backends.** The two fixed panels — "Folder as Workspace" and "Document Map" — are now *dock panels*: a panel lives in a group, a group docks to any of the four sides or floats above the main window, a group holds one or more panels as tabs, and the user rearranges everything by dragging captions and tabs. The policy/mechanism split is the same one `core::shortcuts` uses: everything decidable without a window system — group/tab bookkeeping, band carving, splitter placement, drop-target resolution, drag-hint rects, clamps, `session.xml` (de)serialisation with every numeric field bounded — lives in `crates/core/src/dock.rs` and is exercised by `cargo test` on every runner; each backend contributes only mechanism. `Shell::restored_dock_layout` is the single place the precedence lives (a persisted `<dock>` wins, else the legacy `<workspace>`/`<docmap>` fields migrate), so the three backends cannot drift on it. Win32's mechanism is `ui_win32/src/dock_panels.rs` (group container windows, owned `WS_POPUP` floats, a layered hint, a five-phase reconciler); GTK's is `ui_gtk/src/dock.rs`, and the two are kept the same shape on purpose.

  **The GTK mechanism, and the two things GTK 3 does that had to be measured.** The dock area is one `GtkLayout` whose children — the editor cell (tab strip + editor + FIF dock, moved but never reparented), each docked group's frame, the four side splitters — are placed by hand from the rects `compute_frame` carves. A `GtkLayout` rather than a `GtkFixed` because its own minimum size is zero regardless of its children (it is a scrollable), so sizing the editor cell to fill the area does not become the window's minimum and the window can still shrink; and rather than a tree of `GtkPaned`s because a rebuilt tree would reparent — and so unrealize — the main Scintilla view on every change. The first version placed children with `move_` + `set_size_request` from the area's `size-allocate` handler and relied on GTK to run another pass; driven live, nothing moved and every child sat at its minimum size in the corner, with the log showing the carve computed correctly. On GTK 3.24 a resize queued from inside `size-allocate` re-allocates only the children that queued it, at the size they already had, and never re-runs `gtk_layout_size_allocate`. What ships allocates the children directly (`WidgetExt::size_allocate`) inside the same pass, the way the class handler itself does, and every model change just `queue_resize`s the area. The second: a `GtkEventBox` creates its event window *inside* its border width, so the floating groups' resize border — a press within 6 px of an undecorated float's edge starts a WM resize drag — received nothing while it was a border width, and works as margins on the inner box. Both are recorded at the call sites.

  **Groups are rehosted, never rebuilt, and nothing in the module is ever destroyed.** A group's frame is reparented between the dock area and a floating toplevel (undecorated, transient for the main window, `Utility` type hint, never focused on map); floating toplevels are hidden and pooled rather than destroyed, and a retired group evacuates its panels to a hidden parking box before its frame is removed. The reason is the Document Map: its panel content carries the miniature Scintilla view, and `gtk_widget_destroy` on any container holding it would run `ScintillaGTK::Dispose` — which unparents the view's scrollbars — regardless of the reference `GtkUiState.docmap_sci` still holds. Reparenting, by contrast, is an unrealize/realize cycle Scintilla's GTK backend is written for (`UnRealizeThis` / `RealizeThis` recreate its `GdkWindow`s and IM context), and it was verified rather than assumed: the miniature paints and tracks the viewport after being torn out into a float, tabbed into another group, docked to a different side and restored from `session.xml` inside a float. The `single_view_source_invariant` guards keep forbidding `destroy()` / `remove(` on the two view bindings; a new `dock_reparenting_source_invariant` pins the container side — the dock module holds no `.destroy()` at all, the only `.destroy()` receivers crate-wide are transient `dialog` / `chooser` locals, and the panel modules never `hide()` / `show()` their own content (hidden means parked). All three mutation-verified, and the first needed its mutation inside an `unsafe` block, because `WidgetExtManual::destroy` is unsafe in gtk-rs 0.18 — a mutation that fails to compile looks exactly like one the guard failed to catch, as §7.2's m5 note already records. **The first version of that scan matched only `x.destroy()`, and two review rounds each found a spelling it missed** — the UFCS `WidgetExtManual::destroy(&x)`, then the raw FFI `gtk::ffi::gtk_widget_destroy(ptr)`, the latter already idiomatic in this crate for the Scintilla shim. Both compiled, destroyed the widget, and read as clean. The scanner now matches any identifier ending in `destroy` followed by `(` and names the receiver from the first argument, with a strict allowlist (`dialog` / `chooser` locals only, and that allowlist is by *name*, an accepted and documented limit), so a fourth spelling fails rather than passes; the dock module additionally admits no `unsafe` at all. Every spelling is mutation-verified in place. "Every panel has a hosted content widget" — the property the Cocoa `every_panel_is_added_to_the_content_view` guard exists for — is not a scan here: the dock state carries one field per `DockPanel` and resolves them through an exhaustive `match`, so a new variant is a compile error rather than a panel that builds and never draws.

  **Borrow discipline.** The dock model and its widgets live in a module thread-local rather than on `GtkUiState`, so a live `with_state` borrow can never decline a layout pass — the blank-panel failure the Cocoa results dock documents. Code holding the dock borrow never calls `with_state`; a `with_state` closure may take a brief read-only dock borrow. That second half reaches further than it looks: a GTK call made under the dock borrow can run a signal handler synchronously, and the one such path found by driving the app (a child's `size_allocate` → the Document Map overlay's own `size-allocate` → its refresh → `is_visible`, declined) is why the carve is snapshotted under the borrow and applied after it drops. A decline degrades — a skipped refresh, a layout pass retried from an idle — and is logged at `debug`.

  **Verified by driving the real app under a scratch `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`**, reading results back out of the `session.xml` written at quit: legacy migration (a pre-dock session comes up workspace-left / docmap-right, byte-for-byte the old arrangement), caption drag to the right edge (stacked under the map), drop onto the map's body (tabbed, bottom tab bar with icon-only inactive tabs), tab tear-out (floats at the cursor), float caption to the left edge (docks), splitter drag (band 240 → 360, persisted as `left="360"`), Escape mid-drag (nothing moves), caption ✕ (hidden, remembered on its side), main-window resize (re-carved), whole-group caption to the bottom edge (a horizontal band with the tab bar, `active` persisted), float border resize (280 → 380, persisted), and a dock-aware restore of every one of those. The drop hint is an override-redirect popup painted translucent grey on a compositing screen; it is confirmed mapped at the resolver's rect and captured by its own window id, but `import -window root` on this Muffin session does not composite override-redirect windows into the capture, so its on-screen look is the one thing here checked by geometry rather than by pixels. One `xdotool` trap worth recording: `getwindowgeometry` reported a position 10 × 40 px off the client origin on this WM, so the first drag pressed the tree instead of the caption and reported a "drag that did nothing"; `xwininfo`'s absolute upper-left is the honest figure.

  **Two follow-ups from hands-on use, initially GTK-only.** A float could be grown by its resize border but never shrunk: the frame kept the `set_size_request` the docked layout had given it, and inside a toplevel that request is the window's minimum. Rehosting into a float now replaces it with the model's own `MIN_FLOAT_W`/`MIN_FLOAT_H`, so the live window can never be dragged below the rect `set_floating_rect` persists. And a panel torn off opened at its docked size — a full-height side band nobody wants as a window — where Win32 did the same; on GTK a tear-off (caption drag of a docked group, or a tab torn out) now opens at **a third of the main window's current width and height**, floored at the model minimum, with the pointer kept at the same fraction along the caption it was grabbed at. An already-floating group being dragged keeps its size. Both pure helpers are unit-tested; both were verified live (a 300×659 float shrinks to 120×103 and no further; an 1100×750 window tears off 366×250 floats at the predicted position). Cocoa ported both with its dock module, and **Win32 has since ported the tear-off half too** — the same two pure helpers with the same unit tests, the drag-start split on the group window's floating flag — so all three backends open a tear-off at a third of the window. The shrink half was never a Win32 gap: its floating frames are `WS_THICKFRAME` popups whose `WM_GETMINMAXINFO` already floors at `MIN_FLOAT_W`/`MIN_FLOAT_H`.

  **The Cocoa mechanism (`ui_cocoa/src/dock.rs`)**, the last of the three, is kept the same shape as GTK's and differs where AppKit does. The dock area is a plain `NSView` that answers `isFlipped` YES — not stylistic: the model carves top-down, so a flipped area lets every rect `compute_frame` produces go to `setFrame:` verbatim, with no per-child conversion a Bottom band would get wrong. Nothing inside it autoresizes; the same `windowDidResize:` hook that re-lays the tab strip re-carves the area on every resize step. The editor cell (tab strip + editor + FIF dock) is an ordinary unflipped view whose children keep their springs-and-struts, so the Scintilla view is never reparented; the FIF divider now measures its drag from the *cell's* bottom edge rather than the window's, because a Bottom band can sit between the two. Groups are rehosted between the area and a floating window, never rebuilt; panel content is parked in a hidden view under the content view, and nothing is ever closed or released — floats are `orderOut`ed and pooled. **Floating groups are borderless `NSPanel`s**: `Resizable` on a borderless mask is what gives AppKit's edge-resize without a title bar, so no hand-rolled resize border is needed; they float above the main window, hide on deactivate (the utility-panel default the Find panel keeps, for the reason it records), and become key only when a view inside asks — so ordering one front on restore never steals the editor's focus. Each carries the same `makeFirstResponder:` refusal as the main window, because a floated Document Map would otherwise be a second route to typing into the shared document with no visible caret; the focus source-scan now pins both windows. Drags run in **flipped screen coordinates** (top-down, origin at the primary screen's top-left), the same space Win32 and GTK use in root coordinates, through two conversions against the primary screen's height. The gestures are synchronous `nextEventMatchingMask:` tracking loops — the tab strip's idiom — under a `DrainFreeze`, with Escape read from the same queue so a cancel needs no key monitor, and with the model mutated *after* the loop on the same decide-inside/act-outside reasoning the tab strip records: the reconcile a drop triggers rebuilds the tab bar and removes the very view whose `mouseDown:` is on the stack. The reconcile itself runs under the dock borrow and positions floating windows only after it drops, because `setFrame:display:` posts `windowDidResize:` synchronously and that delegate mirrors the rect back through the dock borrow — declined re-entrantly, it would leave the model holding the last rect the user dragged rather than the one just applied. A source scan pins that ordering, that the dock module never names either Scintilla view, and that `build_content` hands both panel containers into `DockHosts` (the m4d blank-panel lesson, now covering the hand-off the compiler cannot see — the hosting itself is an exhaustive match). Both panels' fixed-panel chrome is gone: the workspace tree's action bar carries the "Expanding folders: N" counter where the old per-panel title row did, since the title moved to the group caption, and the Document Map's container is now only the miniature and its overlay. A torn-off group opens at a third of the window, the GTK choice; Win32 has since ported the same helpers, so all three backends agree.

  **Verified by driving the real app under a scratch `HOME`**, with synthetic events posted to the queue (the m3c lesson applies: they must be posted to the window that owns the target view, and a first version that posted every event to the main window reported the float's caption and ✕ as dead). Reading the model and the live view frames back after each step: a legacy `<workspace>`/`<docmap>` session migrates to a Left band at 240 and a Right band at 160 with the editor cell between them; a caption drag of the docked map into the editor tears it off into a visible float and the cell widens by its band; a caption drag of that float onto the workspace group's body tabs it in (one group, two tabs, the float pooled); a click on the first tab switches the active panel and hides the other's content; a tab drag to the right edge tears the map back out into a Right band; Escape mid-drag leaves the model untouched where the same drag without it floats; a model-driven dock to Bottom carves a horizontal band with its splitter; a splitter drag of 60 pt grows that band 160 → 220 with the cell shrinking to match; the caption ✕ hides the map, parks its container in the hidden view, and writes `<remember kind="docmap" side="right"/>`; a window resize re-carves; a float resized through `setFrame:` mirrors its new rect into the model; and the `<dock>` element written at quit restores on the next launch, the float clamped back inside the main window's rect. **One bug was found this way and would not have been found otherwise**: a click at a tab's centre switched nothing, because the centre of an inactive tab is its `NSImageView`, which takes the mouse-down for its own drag-out behaviour and does nothing with it, so the tab's `mouseDown:` never ran. Both press targets now claim their whole area in `hitTest:` (the caption keeps its ✕ button's hit), pinned by a source scan. **The pre-commit gate then found a Critical**: neither press target held an owned reference to itself across the gesture, so the reconcile a click triggers — which rebuilds the tab bar, or dismantles the group — could free the receiver while its own `mouseDown:` was still on the stack. The decide-inside/act-outside ordering was there; the tab strip keeps *both* guarantees and records why, and now so does this, with a source scan pinning the retain. Two things this drive could not check, stated plainly: the process launched from a non-interactive shell never became active, so whether a caption press leaves the main window key is unverified (the float is built to become key only when a view asks); and AppKit's edge-resize on a borderless `Resizable` panel was exercised only through `setFrame:`, not through a real edge drag.

  **§8 re-measured against the milestone's own baseline** (release, one empty buffer, `session.xml` cleared, three runs each, the baseline built from `HEAD` in a separate worktree): `phys_footprint` **27.1 / 27.2 / 27.3 MB** against **27.3 / 27.1 / 27.3 MB**, cold start **204 / 199 ms** against **204 / 187 / 187 ms** (the milestone's first run, 259 ms, staged the bundled plugins into a fresh config directory and is discarded as the usual cold-cache outlier). Indistinguishable, which is the expected answer for a dock area, four hidden splitters and a hidden hint panel, and is recorded because "expected" is not "measured". The absolute footprint does not reproduce the 19.4 MB the m5 block in §8 records; the baseline misses it by the same amount on the same protocol, so it is pre-existing rather than this milestone's, and it is over the 25 MB budget — noted here rather than explained, on the same rule the m4c block took for its own unexplained figure.

  **Plugin dock dialogs are dock panels too (landed, Win32).** `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` used to wrap a plugin's `h_client` in a bespoke `WS_OVERLAPPEDWINDOW | WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW` frame of its own — a parallel docking implementation that could not dock, could not tab and did not persist, and which a user reported as "the window is not integrating with the existing plugin panel docks". `DockPanel` grew a third variant, `Plugin(&'static PluginPanelIdent)`, interned from `(module, sanitized pszName)`; the frame class, its window proc and its geometry helpers are gone, and a plugin panel is now an ordinary panel — it docks to any side, floats, tabs with other panels including the host's own two, reorders by drag, and round-trips through `session.xml` under a `plugin:<module>|<name>` key. That also unblocked the last two 🟡 rows in the docking section of `docs/nppm-coverage.md`: `NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` shows the named panel and makes it its group's active tab, and `DMN_CLOSE` now fires from the group caption's ✕.

  The variant stays `Copy` — the whole model is `Copy`-keyed and `DockPanel` is compared, matched and stored in vectors everywhere — by interning the identity into a leaked `&'static PluginPanelIdent` behind a `MAX_PLUGIN_PANELS` cap, so `title()` and `persist_key()` keep returning `&'static str` and the leak is bounded by a constant rather than by whatever a plugin, or a hand-edited `session.xml`, asks for. Interning is idempotent, which is what lets a restored layout and a live registration converge on one identity rather than two that compare unequal.

  **`tTbData.u_mask`'s `DWS_DF_CONT_*` nibble finally means something, and it means "the container on that side".** Upstream's docking manager has exactly one container per side, so two plugins asking for `DWS_DF_CONT_BOTTOM` become two tabs of one group rather than two stacked bands — which is also the arrangement that makes `NPPM_DMMVIEWOTHERTAB` observable at all. `DockLayout` carries it in a separate `initial_side` table rather than in `remembered`, because the two mean different things: a remembered `Side` reopens a panel in a band of its own, and it is populated by a hide and by a restored session, so consulting it first is what keeps a plugin's preference from ever overriding a position the user chose.

  Two things were found only by driving the real app. The `h_client` ownership check refused every legitimate registration — see §6.5, where the correction is recorded, because the lesson is about guards rather than about docking. And a restored plugin panel came back as an *empty* group: the layout is restored before any plugin loads, so the group is waiting for a content window that only registration supplies, and `example-hello` was registering from its menu command. It now registers from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION`, which is the moment the ABI sets aside for exactly this, and `register_dock_dialog` marks the layout dirty so the waiting group is reconciled even though the *model* did not change. The headers under `plugins/nppcompat-headers/` say so to plugin authors in as many words.

  **GTK and Cocoa cannot host a plugin panel** — `NPPM_DMMREGASDCKDLG` is one of the `HWND`-shaped `UiPlatform` methods that keep their trait defaults there (see the entry below) — and `session.xml` is portable, so a layout written on Windows can name a panel they have no content widget for. Both call `DockLayout::drop_plugin_panels` at restore, which is what lets their `panel_content` return `None` on a path that is then unreachable rather than merely unlikely. *(GTK hosts plugin panels since, and no longer drops them; only Cocoa calls `drop_plugin_panels` now. See the GTK plugin-panel entry in §7.4.)*

  **A plugin panel comes back across a restart, and two host bugs stood between it and that (landed, Win32).** Both were reported by a user testing NppExec: its console docked fine, but a restart brought back the band with nothing in it, where Notepad++ restores the console.

  **The host told plugins "shutting down" too late.** `NPPN_SHUTDOWN` fired from `WM_DESTROY`, which Windows delivers *after* it has hidden the frame — and `IsWindowVisible` is false for every descendant of a hidden window, so a plugin asking whether its own panel was open was told no. Measured at the old moment, NppExec's console reported `client_style_visible=true` (its own `WS_VISIBLE` bit was still set) alongside `IsWindowVisible == false`, because only the *parent* had gone. It now fires from `WM_CLOSE`, where the window is still up, through a latched `notify_plugins_of_shutdown` that `WM_DESTROY` also calls — `ID_FILE_EXIT` destroys the window directly and a plugin may call `DestroyWindow` itself, and a lost shutdown notification costs a plugin its saved state. The capture of the panel layout moved ahead of the notifications in the same helper, so what is persisted is the arrangement the user had rather than whatever a plugin left while tearing down.

  **And the plugin that owned a restored panel was never loaded.** The arrangement persisted correctly and the group came back, but a plugin panel's content window can only come from the plugin, and nothing loaded it until the user opened the Plugins menu — so the band sat empty, which is worse than not restoring it at all. `Shell::next_restored_panel_plugin_to_load` now loads exactly the plugins a restored-visible panel names, from `run()` after `apply_saved_dock` and before the pump. Notepad++ reaches the same end by loading everything at startup; this pays only for what the arrangement names, so **§8's lazy-load constraint still holds** — measured against the process's own module list rather than the log: a session with one plugin panel maps that one DLL and no other, a session with none maps nothing.

  Verified end to end with `example-hello`, whose first panel registers from `NPPN_TBMODIFICATION` — the moment the ABI sets aside for it, and now what the headers tell plugin authors: open the panel, quit, relaunch, and the panel is there and populated with no menu ever opened. (Its second panel now registers only from its own command, which the restore-by-command entry above covers.)

  **It is a real exception to §8's lazy-load constraint, and §8 now names it rather than leaving the table row to be read as absolute.** The security audit's framing is worth keeping: `session.xml` is hand-editable, so a crafted one can force every installed plugin the attacker can name — up to `MAX_RESTORED_PLUGIN_PANELS` (half the panel table, since the restore-by-command work below), and only ones already discovered on disk — to load at startup, running each plugin's `setInfo` / `getFuncsArray` unconditionally. That grants no capability the attacker did not already have (writing that file means writing the plugins directory), but it does turn a config file into a lever for a slow or hanging plugin init, and it defeats the "zero `LoadLibrary` at startup" check for anyone reading the trace log without knowing why. The scope is what makes it acceptable: the module set is matched by `module_key` against the discovered plugin files, so it can name nothing that is not already installed, and it is bounded by the same cap that bounds the panel table. *(Since guarded: with Preferences → Security's guard on, the default, only a panel record Code++ signed makes its plugin load at startup — see the entry on the guard.)*

  **~~The real NppExec still comes back closed~~ — closed since: the cause was the host, though not in the code this paragraph first suspected, but in a step it never took.** Its `[Console] Visible` round-trips whatever it started with: seeded `Visible=1` by hand it restores its console correctly under Code++ — registers, shows, rewrites `1` — but showing the console from either of its menu commands never makes it write `1`. Four candidates were measured and refuted, so they should not be re-tried: it is not `IsWindowVisible` (true when the host now asks), not a host query at shutdown (every `NPPM_*` it sends was logged; none reads visibility), not a difference between its "Show" and "Toggle" commands, and not `DMN_DOCK` / `DMN_FLOAT` (now sent; the flag still round-trips). The `DMN_*` entry above records what Notepad++ actually does, measured against the real one: it never reads that flag, and runs the plugin's own command instead. Code++ now does too — see the restore-by-command entry — and the console comes back, menu check and all.

  **A plugin panel without its own icon gets a glyph of its own.** The tab strip used the Document Map's artwork as a placeholder for *every* plugin panel, which a user noticed as "the same icon as the document map" — and the sharper half is that an inactive tab shows its icon and no label, so two plugin panels in one group were indistinguishable from each other. A plugin's `tTbData.h_icon_tab` is now honoured: converted once at registration (`icon_to_tab_bitmap`) into the premultiplied-BGRA bitmap the bar blits, because `DrawIconEx` into a DIB plus a premultiply pass is far too much per repaint and the `HICON` is only guaranteed live for that call. Plugins that supply none fall back to a new `assets/icons/plugin-panel` jigsaw glyph rather than the map's. Measured on the real plugins: NppExec's icon converts to a 16×16 bitmap with 9 distinct colours and its tab draws from it, while `example-hello`, which sets no `DWS_ICONTAB`, draws the fallback.

  **What is still open.** `GtkLayout` clips a band's content to the area but not to the band, so a panel whose intrinsic minimum exceeds `MIN_DOCK_BAND_PX` would overlap its neighbour — neither current panel does (a `TreeView` in a `ScrolledWindow` and a Scintilla view both request ~1 px), and a future plugin panel that does needs a `GtkScrolledWindow` around it rather than a change here. *(Measured true once plugin panels arrived, and closed that way: every plugin panel's widget sits in a `GtkScrolledWindow` of the host's — see the GTK plugin-panel entry.)*

- **GTK shows a permanent (non-functional) horizontal scrollbar where Win32 auto-hides it; a confirmed but deliberately-deferred parity gap.** With word wrap off, GTK keeps the horizontal scrollbar on-screen even when nothing overflows — its thumb correctly fills the whole track (nothing to scroll), but the bar is still present. Win32 does *not*: verified against a real Notepad++ install on Windows 11, its horizontal scrollbar is hidden until a line overflows. The cause is a Scintilla platform-backend difference, not a Code++ bug. Win32 Scintilla drives the *native* OS scrollbar via `SetScrollInfo`, and Windows auto-hides a native scrollbar whose whole range is visible — free, no Scintilla logic. GTK Scintilla packs its own `GtkScrollbar` and shows it unconditionally (`ScintillaGTK.cxx` `showSBHorizontal = horizontalScrollBarVisible && !Wrapping()` — no content-width check). The one lever to hide it host-side, `SCI_SETHSCROLLBAR`, sets the *same* `horizontalScrollBarVisible` flag that gates Scintilla's width-tracking (`Editor.cxx:1895`), which the `clamp_horizontal_overscroll` fix (`06c5acf`) depends on to keep `scrollWidth` equal to real content width — so toggling it to auto-hide would either freeze that tracking or flicker the bar on/off around edits near the viewport edge. Not worth that risk for a cosmetic, already-non-draggable bar; recorded here rather than fixed. If pursued later, the clean approach is probably a small patch to the vendored GTK backend's `SetScrollBars` to gate `showSBHorizontal` on `scrollWidth > pageWidth` (keeping `horizontalScrollBarVisible` — and thus tracking — untouched), landing alongside any other vendored-Scintilla carries.

### 7.5 Phase 5 cross-platform parity checklist

A user opening Code++ on Linux or macOS in Phase 5 should see *every* user-visible feature that's already in Win32, behaving the same way. The Phase 5 demo (DESIGN.md §7.2) is the functional gate; this checklist is the explicit work list so nothing slips. It splits cleanly into "already cross-platform — just plug in `UiPlatform`" and "needs new per-platform plumbing."

**Already cross-platform (no Phase 5 design work; the GTK/Cocoa backends inherit it for free by implementing the existing `UiPlatform` trait + `Shell` API):**

- **Session persistence.** `core::session::{Session, Tab, WindowGeometry}` round-trip via `quick-xml`; `Shell::save_session` / `load_session_entries` already drive every backend. `codepp-core`'s and `codepp-shell`'s suites cover the data-shape contract on every CI runner (true since CI widened to `cargo test --workspace`; see the test-discipline note below).
- **Untitled buffer survival** (the `<config_dir>/backup/` mechanism), **dirty saved-file backup**, **`SessionRestoreEntry::DirtyFromBackup`** with mtime-conflict detection, **`Shell::deferred_dialogs`** queue. All in `shell`. `platform::backups_dir()` already resolves the per-OS path.
- **Window size + maximized persistence.** `WindowGeometry` round-trips in session.xml; the platform applies its native equivalent of `SetWindowPos` + `ShowWindow(SW_SHOWMAXIMIZED)`.
- **Tab reorder logic.** `Shell::move_tab` and its 6 unit tests are platform-agnostic. Each backend wires the platform's drag-detection primitive (Win32 subclass + `WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`MOUSEMOVE`/`LBUTTONUP`; GTK `GestureDrag`; Cocoa `NSResponder` mouse events) to call it.
- **`PendingDialog`** plumbing — `ConfirmReload` and `Error` dialogs are returned by `Shell::drain` and presented by the UI. Each backend just maps the two variants to its native dialog primitive.
- **File loading, encoding/EOL detection, file watching, find-in-files, plugin host, NPPM/NPPN dispatch.** All in `core` / `shell` / `plugin-host`; cross-platform from day one.

**Per-platform work for `ui_gtk` and `ui_cocoa` (the user-visible features Phase 5 must replicate):**

- **Main window chrome.** Menu bar (with mnemonic underlines), status bar (7-part layout matching the Win32 spring), toolbar (32 buttons across 10 separator-delimited groups, with the same SVG icon set under `assets/icons/`), tab strip.
- **Tab drag-to-reorder.** GTK: `GtkEventControllerLegacy` or `GestureDrag` on the `GtkNotebook` tab labels, hit-test → `Shell::move_tab`. Cocoa: `NSTabView` doesn't natively support drag-reorder; either subclass `NSTabViewItem`'s tracking area or build a custom tab strip on top of `NSScrollView` (matches what most Cocoa editors do).
- **Modal dialogs.** Goto, Find/Replace, FIF progress, About (with clickable home-page link + F1 binding), reload-confirm, error. Each platform has its own primitive — `GtkDialog` / `NSAlert` / `NSWindow` modal — but the *content shape* and the *trigger conditions* are documented by the existing Win32 implementations and by `PendingDialog`. Don't reinvent the UX; copy it.
- **Periodic auto-save.** Today on Win32 via `SetTimer` + `WM_TIMER` arm calling `Shell::save_session`. GTK uses `g_timeout_add(7000, …)`; Cocoa uses `NSTimer` scheduled for 7-second intervals. **Suggested abstraction** when the GTK backend lands: a new `UiPlatform::start_periodic(period_ms, callback)` returning a cancellation handle — would let `Shell` own the cadence and remove the timer-id constants from each backend, but keep the `WM_TIMER` arm in Win32 as the single subscriber.
- **Window-size restore.** Each platform applies its equivalent of `SetWindowPos(width, height) + ShowWindow(SW_SHOWMAXIMIZED)` from `Shell::saved_window_geometry()`. The toolbar-floor calculation is shared (`toolbar::natural_min_width_px` returns the inner width; the AdjustWindowRectEx step is platform-specific frame-chrome math that GTK/Cocoa replace with their own).
- **Drag-and-drop file open.** Win32 `DragAcceptFiles` / `WM_DROPFILES`. GTK `gtk_drag_dest_set` + `drag-data-received` signal. Cocoa `NSDraggingDestination` protocol on the main window.
- **Native open / save-as / folder-pick dialogs.** Win32 `GetOpenFileNameW` / `GetSaveFileNameW` / `SHBrowseForFolderW`. GTK `GtkFileChooserNative`. Cocoa `NSOpenPanel` / `NSSavePanel`.
- **Accelerators / hotkeys.** F1 → About, Ctrl+S → Save, Ctrl+W → Close, Ctrl+F/H → Find/Replace, Ctrl+G → Goto, Ctrl++/-/0 → Zoom, F3/Shift-F3 → Find Next/Prev. The list is in the Win32 `CreateAcceleratorTableW` block as the source of truth — GTK uses `GtkApplication::set_accels_for_action`, Cocoa uses `NSMenuItem.keyEquivalent`.
- **Per-tab document binding.** `UiPlatform::activate_tab(idx, scintilla_doc)` — Scintilla's `SCI_SETDOCPOINTER` is the same call regardless of host UI; only the editor-handle plumbing differs. The doc-pointer-swap helpers (`capture_text_from_doc`, `is_doc_dirty`) are mechanically the same on every platform.
- **Application icon, title bar.** Win32 embeds `code++.ico` via `app/build.rs`. GTK uses `gtk_window_set_icon_from_file` (or the GResource bundle). Cocoa picks up the icon from the `.app` bundle's `Info.plist`. The shared `assets/code++.png` source feeds all three (the `tools/codepp-app-icon/generate.py` extension point is documented in the script's docstring).

**Test discipline.** The whole workspace's tests run on every CI runner, so the backend-agnostic logic stays verified continuously. This was *not* true from Phase 0 as earlier revisions of this document asserted: CI ran `cargo test -p codepp-core` only, a line written when `core` was the sole crate with tests, so `shell`'s, `editor`'s and `udl`'s suites were verified on contributors' machines and nowhere else — and `ui_win32`'s 130 tests, being behind `#![cfg(target_os = "windows")]`, ran nowhere at all. Widened to `--workspace` in Phase 5. UI-level tests (e.g. "Phase 5 m1 demo: open the GTK build, drag a tab, restart, observe the new order persisted") are manual, gated on the §7.1 phase-rule end-of-phase demo. Don't bypass the demo.

---

## 8. Hard Performance Constraints

These are non-negotiable and verified at each phase boundary.

| Constraint | Budget | Verification |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cold start (warm cache) to interactive | < 80 ms | Stopwatch from `WinMain` / `main` to first paint. Logged when `--perf` flag is set. |
| Single keystroke latency (typed char → Scintilla redraw) | < 5 ms p99 | Frame-time log on a 10k-line file. |
| Open 10 MB UTF-8 file | UI never blocks | Worker thread reads; marshal posts buffer to UI thread incrementally if needed. |
| Memory floor (one empty buffer) | < 25 MB **PSS** (proportional set size; private-resident is the fallback where PSS is unavailable) | Linux: `/proc/<pid>/smaps_rollup`. Windows: private working set from `GetProcessMemoryInfo` (`PROCESS_MEMORY_COUNTERS_EX.PrivateUsage`). macOS: `phys_footprint` from `task_vm_info`. |
| Undo history | Bounded, default 1000 ops, configurable | Scintilla `SCI_SETUNDOCOLLECTION` + periodic trim. |
| Plugin load on startup | Zero plugins loaded until first user interaction touches them, **except** the plugins a restored dock panel names (§6.4) — with Preferences → Security's guard on, only a panel whose record Code++ signed | Trace log at startup shows zero `LoadLibrary` calls until menu open, for a session with no plugin dock panel open. With one, exactly that plugin's module is mapped and no other — check the process's module list, not the log. |
| File I/O thread | Dedicated worker pool, never UI thread | Lints fail if `core::file` is called synchronously from a UI crate. |

**Why the memory metric is PSS and not RSS.** The original wording said "< 25 MB RSS", which was written and measured against Win32 and does not survive the move to a second platform. RSS counts every resident page a process can see, including read-only, file-backed shared-library text that is demand-paged from the page cache and shared with every other process using the same toolkit. That inflates the number by an amount that varies with how heavy the platform toolkit is, not with anything Code++ does — so the same binary "fails" on one OS and "passes" on another while costing the system an identical amount. PSS attributes shared pages proportionally and is the figure that reflects real cost; on the platforms without a PSS equivalent, private-resident is the closest analogue and is stricter, not looser. The budget number itself is unchanged at 25 MB.

**Measured, Phase 5 m3, release build, one empty buffer, x86_64 Linux + GTK 3.24:**

| Figure | Value | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PSS | **11.5 MB** | the constraint above — 46% of budget |
| Private (clean + dirty) | 10.3 MB | of which 6.3 MB dirty |
| `Shared_Clean` | 27.0 MB | GTK / GDK / pango / cairo / glib library text, shared with every GTK process |
| RSS | 37.3 MB | PSS + the shared pages above; recorded for continuity with the old metric |

The shared-page cost is not avoidable by writing the UI layer differently: `libscintilla.a` alone has 305 distinct undefined `gtk_*` / `gdk_*` / `pango_*` / `cairo_*` / `g_object_*` symbols, because Scintilla's GTK backend is itself a GTK client. Any Linux build that links Scintilla maps the whole stack, whether the Rust above it is gtk3-rs, `gtk-sys`, or hand-written `extern "C"`.

**Where the growth came from — measured, not inferred.** The figure recorded at m1 was 8.5 MB PSS; the table above is 11.5 MB. That is past §8's own ">20% regresses the constraint" bar if read as measurement-over-measurement, even though the absolute figure is under half the budget. An earlier revision of this section guessed the cause ("the likely bulk is the lexer theme table"). That guess was wrong, and the measurement is below.

m1 (`e64f530`) and m3 (`HEAD`) were built and run back to back on the same machine with an empty session, and `/proc/<pid>/smaps` attributed per mapping:

| Bucket | m1 | m3 | Delta |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| binary `.text` | 0.89 MB | 3.49 MB | **+2.60 MB** |
| binary `.rodata` | 0.21 MB | 1.06 MB | +0.85 MB |
| shared libs | 2.21 MB | 2.33 MB | +0.11 MB |
| anonymous | 1.30 MB | 1.37 MB | +0.07 MB |
| **heap** | **3.46 MB** | **3.46 MB** | **±0.00 MB** |
| Total PSS | 8.17 MB | 11.81 MB | +3.64 MB |

Three things fall out of that, and only the third matches the old guess:

1. **71% of the growth is code, not data.** The theme table itself is `.rodata` — 85 `const` items that rvalue-promote when `lang_theme` hands out `&'static` references — and accounts for at most the +0.85 MB there, under a quarter of the total. What actually grew is `.text`: the whole of m2 and m3 (the `Shell` wiring, 34 `UiPlatform` methods, the status bar, the menus, session restore, the tab strip) plus the per-language theme *application* code, which is an 85-arm dispatch each issuing ~20 styling calls. Code, not tables.
2. **Heap did not move at all** — 3.46 MB at both ends, to the kilobyte. Nothing added between m1 and m3 allocates more at rest. Whatever this is, it is not a leak and it does not scale with what the user opens.
3. **So it is a one-off static floor rise**, which was the old conclusion, reached for the wrong reason.

Resident `.text` is 3.49 MB of 4.07 MB on disk (88%, up from 74% at m1), so this is not "the binary got bigger and the pages are idle" — startup genuinely executes most of it. Reducing it means executing less code at startup or generating less of it: `lang_theme`'s 85-branch `if` chain compiled to a table lookup, `opt-level = "s"` for the release profile, or `lto = "fat"` over the current `"thin"`. None has been tried; each is a measurable experiment rather than a guess.

**The decision this needs is recorded in §7.4.** The numbers above are the evidence for it: 11.8 MB against a 25 MB budget, with the growth being static code that does not scale with usage, and zero movement at runtime.

**Measured, Phase 5 m1, release build, one empty buffer, arm64 macOS 26.5 + Cocoa:**

| Figure | Value | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `phys_footprint` | **73.0 MB** | the §8 metric on this platform — **292% of the 25 MB budget** |
| `phys_footprint` (peak) | 96.7 MB | during startup |
| Writable regions | 264.9 MB mapped, 68.5 MB resident, 16.9 MB actually written | |
| Library text | 1.8 GB mapped, 443 MB resident | all `SM=COW`, 0 K dirty — shared, clean, file-backed |

**This is the largest budget miss anywhere in the project and it is not yet explained.** Recorded rather than quietly deferred, and stated as unexplained rather than guessed at — §8's own history (the retracted frame-clock conclusion, the wrong "the theme table did it" attribution) is why guessing here would be a mistake.

What the attribution does show is suggestive but not conclusive. `vmmap` reports the process mapping **WebCore (57.6 MB), JavaScriptCore (26.9 MB), SwiftUI (23.3 MB), GeoServices (30.2 MB), Network (24.1 MB), ANECompiler (22.5 MB) and libusd_ms (29.5 MB)** — none of which a text editor has any use for, and none of which Code++ links against. They arrive through AppKit's own initialisation on this OS version. Two things stop that being the answer on its own:

1. Those are all clean `r-x` `SM=COW` text regions with **0 K dirty**, which is exactly the category `phys_footprint` is supposed to exclude — the same category §8's RSS→PSS switch exists to stop counting. So they should not be in the 73 MB, and the resident 443 MB of library text plainly is not.
2. The 68.5 MB of *resident writable* memory is the figure that actually needs explaining, and only 16.9 MB of it has ever been written. A large mapped-and-resident-but-mostly-untouched writable arena is more consistent with an allocator or framework reservation than with anything Code++ does with one empty buffer.

So the honest summary is: the metric says 73 MB, the budget says 25 MB, and the cause is a resident writable arena that has not been attributed to a caller yet. The next step is `malloc_history` / `heap` against a live process, not another `vmmap` summary. Tracked in §7.4.

One caveat on comparing this to the Linux row: `phys_footprint` and PSS are not the same quantity, so 73 MB vs 11.5 MB is not a like-for-like 6× — §8 deliberately specifies a different metric per platform because each OS exposes a different honest one. The comparison that matters is each figure against the shared 25 MB budget, and macOS is the one that fails it.

**Re-measured at Phase 5 m2** (release, one empty buffer, same machine), because §7.4's post-mortem on the Linux drift concluded that measuring once is how a regression goes unnoticed:

| Figure | m1 | m2 | Delta |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `phys_footprint` | 73.0 MB | 73.0 / 73.1 / 73.2 MB | **±0.0 MB** |
| Cold start | 107.8 / 111.6 / 112.8 ms | 119.4 / 123.9 ms | +9% |

Re-measured again at **m3a** (tab strip, drag-and-drop, close gate): `phys_footprint` **72.5 / 72.6 / 72.7 MB**, cold start **118.1 / 122.5 ms**. Both flat against m2 — the third consecutive milestone where the footprint does not move, which was read at the time as evidence that the 73 MB is an AppKit-established floor rather than anything that scales with what Code++ builds. **That inference was wrong**, and the m4b block below has the measurement that refutes it: a minimal AppKit app with the same window is 13.6 MB. The figure is flat because it is dominated by the window's *backing surfaces*, which depend on window size — not because AppKit established it.

Re-measured at **m4b** (release, one empty buffer, same machine), and this row carries a correction as well as a number:

| Figure | m3a | m4b | Delta |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `phys_footprint` | 72.5–72.7 MB | **76.5 / 76.5 / 77.2 / 77.6 MB** (later reduced to ~50 MB — see the colour-space change below) | +4 MB |
| Cold start | 118.1 / 122.5 ms | **156.0 / 164.1 / 167.3 / 174.7 ms** | +38% |

Re-measured at **m5** (release, one empty buffer, `session.xml` cleared first, same machine), against the milestone's own stashed baseline:

| Figure | baseline (m5 stashed) | m5 | Delta |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `phys_footprint` | 19.4 / 19.5 / 19.5 MB | **19.4 / 19.5 / 19.6 MB** | ±0.1 MB |
| Cold start | 154 / 157 / 247 ms | **153 / 156 / 253 ms** | indistinguishable |

The plugin host's whole startup cost is four `stat` pairs (staging, which copies nothing when the destinations are current) plus one directory read (discovery, which records paths and maps nothing). That it does not move either figure is the expected answer, and is recorded because "expected" is not "measured". The high run in each triple is the first, discarded elsewhere in this section as cold-FS-cache; both triples show it, which is what makes them comparable.

**This also resolves the open question §7.4 raised at m4c, and the answer is that 18.5 MB was the honest figure.** m4c recorded 47.1–47.4 MB under the same cleared-`session.xml` protocol and could not reconcile it with the 18.5 MB measured at m4b, leaving the discrepancy explicitly open. It does not reproduce: **19.4 MB**, confirmed by two independent instruments on the same live process — `vmmap --summary`'s Physical footprint and `footprint(1)`'s `phys_footprint`, the latter being exactly the `task_vm_info` field §8 specifies. Whatever produced the m4c reading was local to that measurement rather than to the code, and the m4c-era attribution recorded alongside it (IOSurface 20.5 MB, `MALLOC_SMALL` 14.4 MB dirty, settling 57 → 47 MB over eight seconds) is retracted with it. **macOS meets the 25 MB memory budget** at roughly 78% of it.

**The correction is that this section says every macOS milestone should re-record both figures, and m3b, m3c, m3d, m3e and m4a did not.** So the deltas above are not m4b's — they are five milestones' worth, and attributing them needs the intermediate rows that were never taken. That is precisely the failure mode §7.4's post-mortem on the Linux memory drift identified, restated as a rule in this section, and then not followed. Recorded plainly rather than quietly folded into an m4b row.

**What m4b itself cost is separable, and is nothing.** The same four-run protocol against `HEAD` with the m4b work stashed gives `phys_footprint` **75.8 / 76.9 / 77.0 / 77.2 MB** and cold start **156.0 / 157.6 / 161.5 / 169.8 ms** — indistinguishable from the figures above at this run-to-run spread. The dock builds a table, a scroll view, three columns and four small controls at startup and decodes nothing, so that is the expected answer; it is recorded because "expected" is not "measured".

**Correcting the row above, immediately: every macOS cold-start figure this document has ever recorded measured the wrong endpoint, and the two suspects named in the paragraph that used to sit here were both wrong.**

`ui_gtk` and `ui_win32` both close the interval on a real `SCN_PAINTED`. `ui_cocoa` closed it *before* `-[NSApplication run]`, on the stated grounds that "the window is on screen and the event loop is about to take over" — which had been false since m3b moved `makeKeyAndOrderFront` into the application delegate. At the old mark the window has never been shown, the app has never been activated, and nothing has painted. So the macOS column was never comparable to the other two, and it under-reported.

Instrumented per phase, three runs, release, empty buffer:

| Phase | Elapsed at end |
| --- | --- |
| `main()` → `ui_cocoa::run` entry | 0.1 ms |
| **`NSApplication::sharedApplication`** | **55–61 ms** |
| `setActivationPolicy` + delegate install | 56–62 ms |
| the nine-menu tree, ~88 language rows included | 63–70 ms |
| `Shell::new` | 63–71 ms |
| `scintilla_cocoa_new` | 70–78 ms |
| `NSWindow` + content view + status bar + tab strip | 107–120 ms |
| the 32-button toolbar (32 PNGs × 2 scales) | 114–128 ms |
| the Find-in-Files dock | 117–131 ms |
| *(old mark fired here)* | 154–177 ms |
| `applicationDidFinishLaunching:` | 180–232 ms |
| **first `SCN_PAINTED`** | **225–262 ms** |

Three things fall out, and only the first was suspected:

1. **`NSApplication::sharedApplication` alone costs ~55–61 ms — 70–76% of the entire 80 ms budget — before Code++ constructs a single object.** `run` entry is 0.1 ms, so nothing in `main()` contributes. This is AppKit's own runtime initialisation; Cocoa, AppKit and QuartzCore are load-time linked (`otool -L`), so it is not dylib mapping. **Bundling does not help**: the same binary inside a minimal `.app` with an `Info.plist` measures 61–69 ms for the same call. That refutes the obvious hypothesis — an `.app` bundle fixes *activation* (§7.4) and nothing about this. And it is a **platform floor rather than anything Code++ provokes**: a twelve-line Objective-C program whose whole body is `[NSApplication sharedApplication]` measures 43.8 / 49.1 / 50.9 / 66.6 ms for that call. There is nothing on our side of it to optimise.
2. **Both previously-named suspects are refuted.** The toolbar's 32 PNGs at two scales cost **6.9 ms**, and the whole nine-menu tree **6.7 ms**. The real Code++-side costs are window plus chrome construction (~37 ms) and the post-build startup work — styles, session restore, view settings (~35 ms).
3. **The honest figure is worse than the retracted one.** With the mark moved to `SCN_PAINTED`, cold start measures **220.5 / 225.7 / 242.1 / 250.5 ms** across four consecutive release runs. Against an 80 ms budget that is ~3×, not the ~1.9× the old mark reported.

This is the third retracted conclusion in this section, and the pattern is the same each time: a plausible instrument that was never checked against what it claimed to measure. The mark now lives in `ui_cocoa`'s `SCN_PAINTED` arm alongside `painted()`, so the three platforms measure the same span.

**What the 80 ms budget actually costs on this platform, and why the memory figure was misattributed for four milestones.** Prompted by the obvious follow-up question — *could a Cocoa app be written that comes close to the Win32 port?* — and answered by building the smallest possible one rather than reasoning about it. The reference is a twelve-line Objective-C program: `NSApplication`, one `NSWindow` at Code++'s own 1024×768, one `NSView` whose `drawRect:` fills white, and nothing else.

| | minimal Cocoa app | Code++ (m4b) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `main()` → first paint | **149.7 / 182.9 / 195.0 ms** | ~235 ms |
| `phys_footprint` (settled) | **13.6 MB** | 76–77 MB |
| dylibs loaded at first paint | **1 036** | — |

**On cold start the answer is no, and not marginally.** An empty Cocoa window costs 150–195 ms to first paint on this machine, and Win32's *entire* cold start is 94 ms — below the macOS floor for a blank white rectangle. Code++'s own contribution is the difference, ~40–85 ms, and driving that to zero would still leave the platform floor. Instrumenting `_dyld_image_count` says where it goes: the process holds 517 images at `main`, `sharedApplication` adds **85** (AppIntents, RelevanceKit, CoreSpotlight, Contacts, ClassKit, CloudDocs, FileProvider, QuickLookThumbnailing…), and the stretch from `makeKeyAndOrderFront:` to the first `drawRect:` adds **420 more** — including **WebKit, WebCore, JavaScriptCore, libwebrtc, SafariServices, WebInspectorUI, WritingToolsUI** and the generative-assistant stack. A plain `NSView` filling one colour pulls in a browser engine and Apple Intelligence's UI layer, because on macOS 26 they sit behind the text/accessibility machinery every window initialises. Nothing in that is reachable from application code.

**On memory the answer is the opposite, and it overturns what §8 has said since m1.** The same minimal app settles at **13.6 MB**, so the ~77 MB is *not* an AppKit floor — roughly 63 MB of it is Code++'s. The reason it never moved across milestones is not that AppKit set it; it is that its dominant term depends on window size, which never changed. Attributed with `vmmap`, same window, same display:

| Region | minimal app | Code++ | |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **IOSurface** | 3.3 MB | **46.2 MB** | window and layer backing store |
| `MALLOC_SMALL` (dirty) | 4.5 MB | 11.5 MB | real heap |
| CoreAnimation | 0.2 MB | 2.7 MB | |
| IOAccelerator | — | 3.9 MB | |

So **~60% of the macOS memory figure is GPU-shared backing store for the window and its layers**, not anything Code++ allocates — on a 2880×1864 Retina panel a full-window surface is ~12.6 MB on its own, and Code++ has eight of them against the minimal app's six much smaller ones. That also makes the cross-platform comparison unfair in a way §8 did not previously account for: on Linux the equivalent compositing buffers live in the X server, not in the editor's PSS, so the two numbers were never measuring the same thing. The leading candidate for why Code++'s surfaces are so much larger is already recorded in §7.4 — macOS 26's `SCIScrollView` carries a scroll-edge-effect `NSVisualEffectView` measured at 690 pt inside a 548 pt scroll view, and a visual-effect view forces layer backing on its subtree. Untested, and named as a candidate rather than a cause.

The earlier claim that 68.5 MB of resident writable memory was unexplained is therefore **retracted**: most of it is IOSurface, which `vmmap`'s writable-region summary counts and which is not heap at all.

**Correction, and it is the largest one in this section: every macOS memory figure recorded before this point was measured with the wrong buffer set, and the budget is not actually missed.**

§8's memory constraint says *one empty buffer*. The figures above — 73 MB at m1, 77 MB at m4b, and the 50 MB after the colour-space change — were all taken by launching the release binary and sampling it, which **restores the previous session**. By the time the m4b work was done that session held five files including a 10 000-line `.rs`, a `.log`, and a crash-recovery backup. Deleting `session.xml` first and re-measuring the *same binary*:

| Condition | `phys_footprint` | Cold start |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **One empty buffer — §8's stated condition** | **18.5 MB** | **165–170 ms** |
| One 10 000-line `.rs` open | 29.3 MB | ~159 ms |
| The accumulated session (5 files + a backup) | 76 MB | ~470 ms |

**So macOS meets the 25 MB memory budget at 18.5 MB, and has presumably done so since the colour-space change.** It was never re-measured under the stated condition, so the miss was reported for four milestones on the strength of a number that answered a different question. The 25 MB figure is a *floor* — what the editor costs before the user opens anything — and measuring it with a working session loaded is simply not that measurement.

Two things worth taking from it beyond the number. First, the ordering trap: the app restores its session *by design*, so on this platform "run the binary and read `phys_footprint`" silently measures whatever the developer last had open, and it drifts upward over a working day. Any repeat of this must clear `session.xml` first, and DEVELOPMENT.md now says so. Second, the retracted savings figure: the "77 MB → 50 MB, 35%" attributed to the sRGB pin below was a before/after across that same loaded session. Re-measured clean it is **18.9 MB → 18.5 MB**, because at idle with one empty buffer few compositing surfaces are live for the format change to halve. The **per-surface** halving is not retracted — it was observed directly, `RGhA` at 20.0 MB becoming `BGRA` at 10.2 MB — so the saving is real but scales with how many surfaces the compositor has allocated, which scales with what is on screen. Keep the pin; do not keep the headline number.

Cold start is unaffected by the correction in the direction that matters: ~167 ms against an 80 ms budget, still missed, but now sitting *inside* the 150–195 ms range a minimal Cocoa app takes to first paint. Code++'s own contribution to it is close to nothing.

**Following that up: the memory is compositing surfaces, and a one-line change removes a third of it.** The lead named above — the scroll-edge-effect `NSVisualEffectView` — was **tested and refuted**: removing all three of them from the live view tree saved nothing (47.4 MB either side). What the footprint actually tracks is window *area*: 27.6 / 47.8 / 70.3 MB at 400×300, 1024×768 and 1600×1200. Note the first of those — a 400×300 window is already over the 25 MB budget.

`vmmap`'s per-surface detail names the cause. Both Code++ and the minimal app hold one `CA Whippet Drawable` per buffer, and the difference is the *pixel format*:

| | surface | format | bytes/px | size |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| minimal app | 2048×1600 | `A008` | 1 | 3.2 MB |
| Code++ (before) | 1926×1346 | **`RGhA`** | **8** | **20.0 MB** ×3 buffers |
| Code++ (after) | 1926×1346 | `BGRA` | 4 | 10.2 MB |

`RGhA` is half-float RGBA — the wide-gamut / EDR format CoreAnimation picks for a window on a display that can show more than sRGB, which is every recent Mac. **Pinning the window's colour space to sRGB drops it to 8-bit `BGRA`**, and `phys_footprint` from ~77 MB to **~50 MB, a 35% saving**, scaling with window area (70.3 → 49.9 MB at 1600×1200).

Nothing is lost, because Code++ has no wide-gamut content: the lexer themes are hex literals, Scintilla takes packed RGB, and the toolbar icons are sRGB PNGs — those values *are* sRGB, so a half-float pipeline cannot render them more accurately. Verified by rendering the same highlighted buffer either side of the change and comparing pixels: the dominant colours are identical to the pixel and the same counts (`ffffff` ×216 014, `e4e4e4` ×10 067, `1e1e1e` ×8 903), with ~26 antialiasing-edge pixels on coloured glyphs differing — which is inherent to blending in a different space, and now matches what the Win32 and GTK backends do. Two things that look equivalent and are not, both measured: `setDepthLimit(NSWindowDepthTwentyfourBitRGB)` changed nothing, and neither did `setOpaque(true)`.

**macOS after this change: `phys_footprint` 49.5 / 49.5 / 50.2 MB, cold start 246 / 250 / 253 ms.** Still 2× the memory budget and ~3× the cold-start one, but the memory figure is now the smaller of the two misses rather than the larger, and what remains of it is compositing buffers whose size is set by the window the user chose.

**macOS keystroke latency, measured for the first time.** The two missing hooks are now wired — `key_pressed` from a local `NSEvent` monitor (a monitor rather than a subclass: the pump is `-[NSApplication run]` and the view that receives keys is inside the vendored Scintilla tree, so neither backend's shape was available) and `text_modified` on `SCN_MODIFIED`. Driven by posting synthetic key events from a timer, 400 samples per row:

| Buffer | Input rate (measured) | p50 | p90 | p99 | max |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 000-line `.rs` | 8 char/s (125 ms gaps) | 5.12 ms | 18.67 ms | **20.56 ms** | 32.06 ms |
| Empty | 8 char/s (125 ms gaps) | 5.30 ms | 19.08 ms | **20.88 ms** | 32.42 ms |
| 10 000-line `.rs` | 20 char/s (50 ms gaps) | 3.91 ms | 10.91 ms | **11.64 ms** | 29.71 ms |
| 10 000-line `.rs` | ~7 char/s, jittered 128–156 ms | 5.32 ms | 18.15 ms | **20.27 ms** | 30.65 ms |

**p99 ≈ 20 ms against a 5 ms budget — 4× over, and the worst of the three backends** (GTK 4.62 ms, Win32 8.89 ms). Three properties, all measured rather than inferred:

1. **Content-independent.** An empty buffer and a 10 000-line lexed `.rs` file are identical to within noise, so Lexilla and buffer size are not involved — the same refutation GTK's dedicated test made.
2. **It gets *better* at twice the input rate** (p99 20.6 → 11.6 ms), which rules out queueing outright — the opposite of a backlog.
3. **The distribution is bimodal**, roughly half the samples near 5 ms and most of the rest near 19 ms.

The obvious objection — that a fixed 125 ms interval is exactly 7.5 frames and would alias against the 60 Hz refresh, pinning every keystroke to one of two phases — was **tested and refuted**: jittering the interval across 128–156 ms, a range wider than a frame, leaves the distribution unchanged. The bimodality is real, not a sampling artefact.

**Profiled, and the cause is not what the shape suggested.** An earlier revision of this section read the ~14 ms separation as close to a 60 Hz frame and concluded that some keystrokes make the current composite while the rest wait for the next. **That is retracted.** Splitting the interval and measuring main-thread CPU time across it says otherwise:

| | p50 | p99 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| key press → `SCN_MODIFIED` | **0.21 ms** | 0.45 ms |
| `SCN_MODIFIED` → `SCN_PAINTED` | 7.99 ms | **19.91 ms** |
| main-thread CPU *within* that window | 8.63 ms | 19.79 ms |

CPU time equals wall time to within 0.04 ms in both modes, so **the thread is busy for the entire interval — it is not waiting for a frame at all**. Scintilla's handling of the keystroke itself is free (0.2 ms); every millisecond is in the paint, and it is work.

`sample` names the work. The stack is `-[SCIContentView drawRect:]` → `ScintillaCocoa::Draw` → `SyncPaint` → `Editor::Paint` → `EditView::PaintText` → **`EditView::DrawLine`** → `DrawForeground` → `SurfaceImpl::DrawTextTransparent` → **`CTLineCreateWithAttributedString`**. So each paint re-runs CoreText line layout — typesetting, glyph encoding, the lot — and it does so for *many* lines rather than the one that changed.

**Confirmed by varying the window height**, which is the cleanest possible test of "how many visible lines":

| Window height | ≈ visible lines | p50 | p99 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 200 pt | 14 | 5.13 ms | **6.41 ms** |
| 500 pt | 35 | 11.09 ms | 14.28 ms |
| 900 pt | 64 | 10.89 ms | **21.09 ms** |

Latency scales with the number of visible lines. At 14 lines it is nearly inside the budget. **Every keystroke repaints every visible line.**
